<meta>This page serves as a modern frontispiece and legal disclaimer for a digital surrogate of a historical manuscript. It establishes the provenance of the digital file as part of the Google Books project.</meta>

This is a digital copy of a book that was preserved for generations on library shelves before it was carefully scanned by Google as part of a project to make the world’s books discoverable online.

It has survived long enough for the copyright to expire and the book to enter the **public domain** <note>A legal status where a work is no longer protected by intellectual property laws, allowing the public to freely access and use it.</note>. A public domain book is one that was never subject to copyright or whose legal copyright term has expired. Whether a book is in the public domain may vary country to country. Public domain books are our gateways to the past, representing a wealth of history, culture and knowledge that’s often difficult to discover.

Marks, notations and other marginalia present in the original volume will appear in this file - a reminder of this book’s long journey from the publisher to a library and finally to you.

## Usage guidelines

Google is proud to partner with libraries to digitize public domain materials and make them widely accessible. Public domain books belong to the public and we are merely their custodians. Nevertheless, this work is expensive, so in order to keep providing this resource, we have taken steps to prevent abuse by commercial parties, including placing technical restrictions on automated querying <note>The use of software or "bots" to automatically search or scrape data from a website.</note>.

We also ask that you:

+ *Make non-commercial use of the files* We designed Google Book Search for use by individuals, and we request that you use these files for personal, non-commercial purposes.
+ *Refrain from automated querying* Do not send automated queries of any sort to Google’s system: If you are conducting research on machine translation, optical character recognition <note>Often abbreviated as OCR; this is the technology used to convert images of printed or handwritten text into machine-encoded text.</note> or other areas where access to a large amount of text is helpful, please contact us. We encourage the use of public domain materials for these purposes and may be able to help.
+ *Maintain attribution* The Google “watermark” you see on each file is essential for informing people about this project and helping them find additional materials through Google Book Search. Please do not remove it.
+ *Keep it legal* Whatever your use, remember that you are responsible for ensuring that what you are doing is legal. Do not assume that just because we believe a book is in the public domain for users in the United States, that the work is also in the public domain for users in other countries. Whether a book is still in copyright varies from country to country, and we can’t offer guidance on whether any specific use of any specific book is allowed. Please do not assume that a book’s appearance in Google Book Search means it can be used in any manner anywhere in the world. Copyright infringement liability can be quite severe.

## About Google Book Search

Google’s mission is to organize the world’s information and to make it universally accessible and useful. Google Book Search helps readers discover the world’s books while helping authors and publishers reach new audiences. You can search through the full text of this book on the web at <insert>http://books.google.com/</insert>

<note>This cover appears to be decorated with <term>blind-stamping</term>, a technique where decorative tools are pressed into the leather without the use of gold leaf or ink, creating a subtle, recessed design.</note>

The decorative layout includes:
*   A central <term>medallion</term> <note>A circular or oval decorative piece used as a focal point in the center of the binding.</note>.
*   Ornate corner pieces or flourishes.

<vocab>
**Bookbinding:** The process of physically assembling a book from an ordered stack of paper sheets.
**Leather cover:** A durable outer protective layer made from animal hide.
**Embossing:** The process of creating a three-dimensional image or design in paper or leather.
**Decorative ornament:** Aesthetic additions to the binding intended to show the value or importance of the manuscript.
</vocab>

# PREFACE.

---

**THE** present volume contains two curious documents concerning Dr. Dee <note>John Dee (1527–1608) was a famous Elizabethan mathematician, astronomer, and advisor to Queen Elizabeth I, known for his vast library and his later controversial studies into the spirit world.</note>, the eminent philosopher of Mortlake <note>A village on the Thames where Dee lived and kept his world-renowned collection of books and scientific instruments.</note>, now for the first time published from the original manuscripts. I. His Private Diary, written in a very small illegible hand on the margins of old Almanacs <note>These were annual calendars containing astronomical data; Dee used the blank spaces to record personal events, dreams, and spiritual observations.</note>, discovered a few years ago by Mr. W. H. Black, in the library of the Ashmolean Museum at Oxford. II. A Catalogue of his Library of Manuscripts, made by himself before his house was plundered by the populace <note>In 1583, while Dee was traveling in Europe, a mob attacked his home in Mortlake, destroying many of his scientific instruments and stealing or damaging his irreplaceable books.</note>, and now preserved in the library of Trinity College, Cambridge.

The publication of this Diary will tend perhaps to set Dee’s character in its true light, more than any thing that has yet been printed. We have, indeed, his *Compendious Rehearsall* <note>original: "Compendious Rehearsall"; This was a formal autobiographical petition written by Dee in 1592 to justify his career and reputation to the Queen's officials.</note>, which is in some respects more comprehensive, but this was written for an especial purpose, for the perusal of <term>royal commissioners</term> <note>High-ranking officials appointed by the monarch to investigate specific legal, financial, or personal matters on behalf of the Crown.</note>, and he has of course carefully avoided every allusion which could be construed in an unfavourable light. In the other, however, he tells us his dreams, talks of mysterious noises in

<summary>This preface introduces two primary sources regarding the life of the scholar John Dee: his personal diary, found in the margins of old almanacs, and a catalog of his extensive library created before it was looted by a mob.</summary>
<keywords>John Dee, Private Diary, Mortlake, Ashmolean Museum, Library Catalogue, Compendious Rehearsall, Royal Commissioners, Elizabethan history</keywords>

# PREFACE.

his chamber, evil spirits, and alludes to various secrets of <term>occult philosophy</term> <note>A branch of Renaissance thought that sought to understand the hidden laws of nature through a blend of science, magic, and religion.</note> in the spirit of a true believer. Mr. <term>D’Israeli</term> <note>Isaac D’Israeli (1766–1848), a prominent literary historian and the father of British Prime Minister Benjamin Disraeli.</note> has given a correct and able view of his character in his "Amenities of Literature," which is remarkably confirmed in almost every point by the narrative now published. "The imagination of <term>Dee</term>," observes that elegant writer, "often predominated over his science; while both were mingling in his intellectual habits, each seemed to him to confirm the other. Prone to the mystical lore of what was termed the <term>occult sciences</term> <note>In Dee's time, this included astrology, alchemy, and communication with spirits—subjects he treated with the same rigor as mathematics.</note>, which in reality are no sciences at all, since whatever remains occult ceases to be science, <term>Dee</term> lost his better genius." I shall refer the reader to this popular work instead of attempting an original paper on the subject, which would necessarily be greatly inferior to that drawn by the masterly hand of the author of the "Curiosities of Literature."

The <term>Catalogue of Dee's Library of Manuscripts</term>, although long since dispersed, is valuable for the notices which it preserves of several middle-age treatises not now extant. He is said to have expended on this collection the sum of three thousand pounds <note>An immense fortune in the 16th century, worth millions in modern currency, illustrating Dee's dedication to preserving knowledge.</note>, a very large sum in those days for a person of limited income.

->J. O. H.<- <note>James Orchard Halliwell, the editor and a noted 19th-century antiquarian.</note>

->35, Alfred Place,<-
->March 15th 1842.<-

<sig>B</sig>
<meta>CAMDEN SOCIETY — DEE.</meta>

# DR. DEE'S DIARY.

1554. Aug. 25th, Bartholomew Hickman born at Shuckburgh in Warwickshire toward evening. My conjecture, upon his own report of circumstances. Oct. 25th, Dr. Daniel Vander Meulen at Antwerp, in the morning at the fourth hour <note>original: "Antwerpiæ, mane hora quarta"</note>.*

1555. April 22nd, Jane Fromonds <note>Dee’s future wife, who would become a significant figure in his later spiritual journeys.</note> born at Cheam at noon. Aug. 1st, Edward Kelly born at the fourth hour in the afternoon <note>original: "natus hora quarta a meridie"</note>† as his father left noted. Oct. 12th, the Lord Willoughby born at the seventh hour in the morning, before noon, Latitude 51° 30', at Wesel in Gelderland <note>Now part of the modern-day Netherlands and Germany.</note>.

1557. July 30th, Mr. Arundell of Cornwall born around the fourth hour <note>original: "natus circa [horam] quartam"</note> in the afternoon.

1558. Dec. 14th, Mary Neville, also known as Mary Lewknor, born between 11 and noon in the morning, by Chichester.

1560. July 8th, Margaret Russell, Countess of Cumberland, at 2 hours and 9 minutes in the morning at Exeter <note>original: "Exoniæ mane"</note>.

1561. Aug. 14th, Mr. Heydon, of Baconsthorpe in Norfolk, at the 11th and a half hour of the night born in the county <note>original: "comitatu"</note> of Surrey.

1563. March 23rd, Mr. William Fennar [reckoning] from noon <note>original: "a meridie"</note> between the eleventh and twelfth hour at night. June 23rd, Jane Cooper, now

---

\* It is almost unnecessary to observe that this and the following are notes of <term>nativities</term> <note>Historical term for birth records used to cast a horoscope or astrological chart.</note>. They are not for the most part contemporary notices, but apparently inserted at various times by Dee when professionally consulted as an astrologer.

† "In the year 1555, Aug. 1, at the fourth hour in the afternoon at Worcester, Mr. Edward Kelly was born," <note>original: "Anno 1555, Aug. 1, hora quarta a meridie Wigorniæ natus Dominus Edouardus Kellæus"</note> Manuscript Ashmole 1788, folio 140, where there is a <term>horoscope</term> of this nativity in the handwriting of Dr. Dee. <term>Ashmole</term> <note>Elias Ashmole (1617–1692), a celebrated antiquary and successor to Dee’s interests in alchemy and astrology.</note>, in his Manuscript 1790, folio 58, says "Mr. Lilly <note>William Lilly, a famous 17th-century English astrologer.</note> told me that John Evans informed him that he was acquainted with Kelly's sister in Worcester, that she showed him some of the gold her brother had <term>transmuted</term> <note>In alchemy, the supposed transformation of base metals like lead into gold.</note>, and that Kelly was first an apothecary in Worcester."

<summary>This page contains a list of "nativities" or birth dates and times for various figures, including Dee's associate Edward Kelly and his wife Jane Fromond, recorded for astrological purposes. The editor provides footnotes regarding the source of these entries and Kelly’s early life in Worcester.</summary>

# DR. DEE'S DIARY.

[1563.

Mistress Kelly, toward evening. Sept. 28th, Mr. John Ask <term>before noon</term> <note>original: "ante meridiem"</note>, six miles this side of York; Elizabeth Mownson, <term>around 9 o'clock in the morning</term> <note>original: "circa horam 9 mane"</note>, <term>sister of Master</term> <note>original: "soror magistri"</note> Thomas Mownson <term>and wife of Master</term> <note>original: "et uxor magistri"</note> Brown.

1564. Mrs. Bridget Cooke born about seven of the clock on Saint David's Day <note>March 1st, the feast day of the patron saint of Wales.</note>, which is the first day of March, being Wednesday; but I cannot yet learn whether it was before noon or after. But she thinks herself to be only 27 years old in the <term>year</term> 1593, <term>on the first of March</term> <note>original: "anno 1593, Martii primo"</note>, but it cannot be so. June 20th, Mr. Hudson, <term>at the seventh hour before noon</term> <note>original: "hora septima ante meridiem"</note> [7:00 AM]. Aug. 21st, Winifred Goose, <term>between 9 and 10 in the afternoon</term> <note>original: "inter 9 et 10 a meridie"</note> by Kingston.

1565. Sept. 12th, John Pontoys, <term>between 9 and 10 before noon</term> <note>original: "inter 9 et 10 ante meridiem"</note> <term>near</term> <note>original: "prope"</note> Stony-Stratford; <term>I think rather at 8 o’clock and 43 minutes</term> <note>original: "puto potius hora 8 min. 43"</note>. Oct. 17th, Thomas Kelly* <term>at the fourth hour in the afternoon</term> <note>original: "hora quarta a meridie"</note> at Worcester. Dec. 21st, Mr. Thomas Mownson at 11 of the clock in the morning.

1568. July 14th, William Emery born at Danbury in Essex <term>a little after the eleventh hour of the night</term> <note>original: "paulo post undecimam horam noctis"</note> [11:00 PM]. Sept. 24th, Margaret Anderson <term>in the morning between 7 and 8</term> <note>original: "mane inter 7 et 8"</note>.

1571. Samuel Swallow born at Thaxted in Essex Feb. 15 <term>before noon</term> <note>original: "ante meridiem"</note>, <term>between the eleventh and twelfth hour</term> <note>original: "inter horam undecimam et duodecimam"</note>, <term>perhaps half an hour after eleven</term> <note>original: "forte hora media post undecimam"</note>.

1573. July 31st, Simeon Steward <term>born an hour and a half before daybreak</term> <note>original: "natus ante diluculum per horam 1 1/2"</note> at Shinfield: his grandfather on his mother’s side was Dr. Huyck, the Queen's physician.

1577. Jan. 16th, the Earl of Leicester, Mr. Philip Sidney, Mr. Dyer, etc., came to my house.** <note>Robert Dudley (Earl of Leicester), Philip Sidney, and Edward Dyer were influential courtiers and intellectuals who frequently consulted Dee on matters of science and exploration.</note> Jan. 22nd, The Earl of Bedford.

\* The brother of the celebrated <unclear>astrologer</unclear> <note>Edward Kelly, Dee's famous scryer and assistant.</note> before mentioned.
\** <unclear>Dr. Dee lived in a house near the water side, a little westward from the church at Mortlake. The buildings which Sir Francis Crane erected for the manufacture of tapestry hangings—which are still (1675) employed for that purpose—were built upon the ground where Dr. Dee's laboratory and other rooms for that use stood. Upon the wall is a square stone, and this was in the house where Dr. Dee lived, now inhabited by a Mr. Selbury, and underneath was his garden.—Ashmolean Manuscript 1788, folio 149. The same account adds: "Dr. Dee was much beloved and respected by all persons of quality; King Edward etc. often invited him to their houses or came to his."</unclear>

# 1577.] DR. DEE'S DIARY.

[...someone] came to my house. Feb. 19th, a great southwest wind, close and cloudy. March 11th, I fell upon my right <term>ankle bone</term> <note>The text uses "nuckul bone," likely referring to the malleolus of the ankle.</note>, <term>around 9 o'clock in the morning</term> <note>original: "hora 9 fere mane"</note>; it was eased beyond all hope within 24 hours with oil of <term>St. John's Wort</term> <note>original: "Hypericon"; a common herbal remedy used then and now for nerve pain and wounds.</note>: God be thanked for such goodness of His creatures! March 24th, Alexander Simon the <term>Ninevite</term> <note>A traveler from Nineveh, in modern-day Iraq; Dee often met with travelers to gather geographic and mystical knowledge.</note> came to me and promised me his service on a journey into Persia. May 1st, I received from Mr. William Herbert of St. Gillian his notes upon my ***Monad***.* <note>original: "Monas"</note> May 2nd, I learned of one Vincent Murfyn’s abominable mistreatment of me behind my back; Mr. Thomas Besbich told me his father is one of the cooks of the Court. May 20th, I hired the barber of Chiswick, Walter Hooper, to keep my hedges and <term>knot gardens</term> <note>Intricate, formal gardens with plants clipped into interlacing patterns.</note> in as good order as he saw them then; this is to be done with at least two cuttings a year, and he is to have five shillings annually, [plus] food and drink. June 10th, a shower of hail and rain. June 18th, borrowed £40 from John Hilton of Fulham. June 19th, I learned more of Vincent Murfyn’s knavery; borrowed £20 from Bartholomew Newsam. June 20th, borrowed £27 upon the gold chain. June 26th, Ellen Lyne gave me a quarter's notice. June 27th, showers of rain and hail. Aug. 19th, the ***British Six Days***† <note>original: "Hexameron Brytanicum"</note> was put to printing.

Nov. 3rd, William Rogers of Mortlake, about 7 o'clock in the morning, cut his own throat at the devil's instigation. Nov. 6th, Sir Humphrey Gilbert <note>The famous explorer and pioneer of English colonization in North America.</note> came to me at Mortlake. Nov. 18th, borrowed £30 from Mr. Edward Hynde of Mortlake, to be repaid at <term>Hallowtide</term> <note>The season of All Saints, around the beginning of November.</note> next year. Nov. 20th, two tides in the morning <note>This refers to a rare "double tide," a phenomenon where the tide in the Thames rose, fell slightly, and rose again, which was often viewed with wonder or as an omen.</note>,

---

\* This refers to his celebrated *Hieroglyphic Monad* (*Monas Hieroglyphica*), which was printed many times. The nature of this complex occult symbol was explained in a paper for the Society of Antiquaries. According to <term>Ashmolean Manuscript 1789</term> <note>original: "MS. Ashm. 1789"</note>, Mr. Herbert "lived then in Mortlake and was an intimate friend of Dr. Dee's."

† This was his work printed in 1577 under the title *General and Rare Memorials pertaining to the Perfect Art of Navigation*, a large-format book that is now extremely rare. The original manuscript is in <term>Ashmolean Manuscript 1789</term>, and Dee's own copy of the published work with handwritten notes is kept in the British Museum. In his *Apologetic Letter* (published in London, 1603), he refers to this work as *The British Monarchy*, noting it was written in 1576.

# DR. DEE'S DIARY.
## [1578.

<margin>1577</margin>
...the first 2 or 3 hours too soon. Nov. 22nd, I rode to Windsor to the Queen’s Majesty. Nov. 25th, I spoke with the Queen <term>at the fifth hour</term> <note>original: "hora quinta"; likely 5:00 PM, a common time for private audiences.</note>. Nov. 28th, I spoke with the Queen <term>at the fifth hour</term>; I spoke with Mr. Secretary Walsingham.* <note>Sir Francis Walsingham, the Queen's "spymaster" and Secretary of State.</note> I declared to the Queen her title to <term>Greenland, Estetiland, and Friseland</term>. <note>Dee was a key proponent of British expansion. "Estetiland" and "Friseland" appeared on 16th-century maps as islands in the North Atlantic, though they are now known to be phantom islands or misidentified parts of North America.</note>

Dec. 1st, I spoke with Sir Christopher Hatton; he was made a knight that day. Dec. 1st, I went from the court at Windsor. Dec. 30th, that <term>most inexplicable slander regarding R. Edward</term> <note>original: "inexplissima illa calumnia de R. Edwardo"</note> was most unjustly denounced against me in some part; some days having passed, [the Lord] in His wisdom [showed] me innocent.

<margin>{ <unclear>illegible handwritten notes</unclear></margin>
1578. Feb. 5th, <term>betrothal</term> <note>original: "sponsalia"; a formal ceremony of engagement.</note> with Jane Fromonds around the first hour. April 28th, I caused Sir Rowland Haywood to examine <insert>Francis Baily of his slandering me, which he denied utterly.</insert> June 13th, rain and in the afternoon a little thunder. June 30th, I told Mr. Daniel Rogers,† Mr. Hakluyt of the Middle Temple being by, that King Arthur and King <term>Malgo</term> <note>The transcription "Maty" likely refers to Malgo (Maglocunus), a legendary king of Britain mentioned by Geoffrey of Monmouth.</note>, both of them, did conquer <term>Gelindia</term> <note>Dee’s name for the mythical island of Friseland.</note>, lately called Friseland, which he so noted presently in his written copy of <term>Geoffrey of Monmouth</term>‡ <note>original: "Monumethensis"; referring to Galfridus Monumetensis’s 12th-century history, *The Deeds of the Kings of Britain*.</note>, for he had no printed book thereof. July 14th, my sister Elizabeth Fromonds came to me. July 27th, at 9:15 <term>in the evening</term> <note>original: "a meridie"</note>, [visited by] Frances, Countess of Hertford.

Aug. 5th, Mr. Reynolds of Bridewell took his leave of me as he passed toward Dartmouth to go with Sir Humphrey Gilbert toward <term>Hochelaga</term> <note>original: "Hocheleya"; the site of present-day Montreal, Canada. Gilbert was an explorer seeking the Northwest Passage.</note>. Aug. 15, I went toward Norwich with my work of <term>The British Empire</term>§ <note>original: "Imperium Brytanicum"; Dee's influential manuscript arguing for English maritime supremacy.</note>. Aug. 23rd, I came to London from Norwich. Aug. 31st, I went to my father-in-law Mr. Fromonds at Cheam.

---

\* Elias Ashmole informs us that Walsingham continued for a long time as one of Dr. Dee's best patrons.
† Rogers was a member of the University of Oxford, and a large <term>commonplace book</term> <note>A personal journal used to compile quotes, notes, and information.</note> in his handwriting is in Archbishop Tenison's library in St. Martin's-in-the-Fields.
‡ That is, Geoffrey of Monmouth’s *On the Deeds of the Kings of Britain*. Hakluyt mentions this fact in his collection of voyages.
§ This is the book just mentioned under the title of *General and Rare Memorials*, published in London in 1577.

# DR. DEE'S DIARY.
## [1578.

September 1st, I came from Cheam. September 6th, Ellen Lyne, my maidservant, departed from this life immediately after midday had passed, after she had lain sick for a month less one day. September 12th, Jane Gale entered my service, and she is to have four <term>nobles</term> <note>A gold coin worth 6 shillings and 8 pence; her annual salary was 26s. 8d.</note> per year. September 25th, Her Majesty came to Richmond from Greenwich. September 26th, the first rain that came for many a day; all the pasture around us was withered: rain in the afternoon like April showers. October 8th, the Queen's Majesty had a conference with me at Richmond <term>between 9:00 and 11:00</term> <note>original: "inter 9 et 11".</note>. October 16th, Dr. Bayly consulted [with me] regarding the Queen's illness. October 22nd, Jane Fromonds <note>Dee's wife.</note> went to the court at Richmond. October 25th, a fit from 9:00 in the evening to 1:00 after midnight. October 28th, the Earl of Leicester and Sir Francis Walsingham, Secretary, decided on my journey abroad on behalf of the Queen's Majesty. November 4th, I was directed to my voyage by the Earl of Leicester and Mr. Secretary Walsingham at the <term>ninth hour</term> <note>original: "hora nona"; 9:00 AM.</note>. November 7th, I came to Gravesend. November 9th, I went from Leigh-on-Sea to the open sea. November 14th, I arrived at Hamburg at the <term>third hour</term> <note>original: "hora tertia"; 3:00 PM.</note>. December 11th, to Frankfurt-an-der-Oder. December 15th, news of <term>Thurneysser's</term> <note>Leonhard Thurneysser was a famous Swiss alchemist, physician, and miracle-worker who served the Elector of Brandenburg.</note> arrival at <term>eight o'clock in the morning</term> <note>original: "hora octava mane".</note>, by a special messenger.

1579. A damp March and not windy. June 10th, I showed to Mr. John Lewis and his son, the physician, the method for distilling aromatic oils. At that time my cat caught a fledged young sparrow which naturally had only a right wing. June 15th, my mother surrendered the Mortlake houses and land, and was granted the estate <term>in open court for the term of her life</term> <note>original: "in plena curia ad terminum vitæ". This was a legal arrangement allowing her to live there until death while securing the future ownership.</note>, and the right of future ownership was also delivered to me <term>by the staff</term> <note>original: "per virgam". This refers to the legal ceremony of "livery of seisin," where a rod or staff was used to symbolize the transfer of land ownership.</note>, and to my wife Jane through me, and thereafter to my heirs and assigns forever; witnessed by Mr. Bullock and Mr. Taylor, surveyor, at Wimbledon, under the tree by the church. June 22nd, Mr. Richard Hickman and Bartholomew his nephew came to me with Mr. Flower, recommended by the Vice-Chamberlain Sir Christopher Hatton.

July 6th, Mr. Hitchcock, who had worked on the <term>plat</term> <note>original: "plat"; here meaning a plan, chart, or proposal, likely regarding Hitchcock's published scheme for developing the English fishing industry.</note> for fishing, made my acquaintance and offered me great courtesy.

# DR. DEE'S DIARY. [1579.

July 13th, **Arthur Dee was born**, <note>original: "Arthurus Dee natus"; Arthur would grow up to be a physician to Tsar Michael I of Russia and King Charles I.</note> a boy, in the morning at approximately 4:30 AM, or rather 4:25, at the very rising of the sun, as I estimate. <margin>His horoscope is found in the Ashmolean manuscripts. Mr. Arthur Dee's birth was accompanied by the unhappy accident of the death of Mr. Fromonds, his mother's father, who died that morning.</margin> After 10 o'clock this night, my wife’s father Mr. Fromonds became speechless, and he died on Tuesday (July 14th) at 4 o'clock in the morning. July 16th, Arthur was christened at 3 o'clock in the afternoon; Mr. Dyer and Mr. Doctor Lewys, judge of the Admiralty, were his godfathers; and Mistress Blanche Parry of the Private Chamber was his godmother. However, Mr. John Herbert of East Sheen acted as deputy for Dr. Lewys, and Mistress Aubrey acted as deputy for my cousin Mistress Blanche Parry.

August 8th, John Elmeston, <note>A student of Oxford who is not mentioned by the university's biographers.</note> a student of Oxford, came to me for <term>dialling</term> <note>The mathematical art and science of designing and constructing sundials.</note>. August 9th, Jane Dee was <term>churched</term> <note>A traditional ceremony of blessing and thanksgiving for a woman after childbirth.</note>. August 16th, **Monsieur** <note>François, Duke of Anjou, the younger brother of the King of France and a suitor to Queen Elizabeth I.</note> came secretly to the court from Calais. August 20th, windy, cloudy, rainy. August 26, Monsieur went back again to France. September 10th, I had a dream of being naked, and my skin was all covered with patterns like a kind of <term>tuft mockado</term> <note>A fabric made to imitate velvet, often with a raised, tufted pattern.</note>, with blue and red crosses; and on my left arm, around the arm in a wreath, I read this word—**without me you can do nothing** <note>original: "sine me nihil potestis facere"; a Latin quotation from the Gospel of John 15:5.</note>: and another dream the same night regarding Mr. Secretary Walsingham, Mr. Cavendish, and myself.

October 3rd, Sir Lionel Duckett sent an unkind letter regarding money. October 4th, Goodman Hilton requested that his two sons be allowed to visit my house. October 5th, a raging wind from the West and South, chiefly during the night. October 9th, 10th, 11th, 12th, great rain for three or four days and nights. October 13th, this day the weather cleared; the foot-boat for the ferry at Kew sank and six persons drowned, due to the negligence of the ferryman who allowed the boat to be overturned by the guide-rope because of the violent and high waters. October 18th, Mr. Adrian Gilbert and John Davis reconciled themselves to me, and disclosed some of Emery's most dishonest—

# DR. DEE'S DIARY. [1579.

...hypocritical, and devilish dealings and devices against me and others, and likewise of that notorious strumpet's abominable words and deeds; and John Davis <note>John Davis (c. 1550–1605) was one of the primary English explorers of the Elizabethan era, later famous for his search for the Northwest Passage.</note> said that he might curse the time that he ever knew Emery, and so much followed his wicked counsel and advice. So just is God! 

Oct. 31st, paid a 20-shilling fine for me and Jane my wife to the Lord of Wimbledon (the Queen), by goodman Burton of Putney, for the surrender taken from my mother of all she has in Mortlake to Jane and me, and then to my heirs and assigns, etc. <note>Dee is recording a legal property transfer. Nov. 25th, the Lord Clinton <note>Henry Clinton, 2nd Earl of Lincoln.</note> came to me and offered Skirbeck by Boston in exchange for Long Leadenham. 

Nov. 29th, I received a letter from Mr. Thomas Jones. 

Dec. 9th, <note>In the original manuscript, Dee wrote this entire entry using Greek letters to phonetically spell out English words, likely to keep this private family matter a secret from casual readers.</note> This night my wife dreamed that one came to her and touched her, saying, “Mistress Dee, you are conceived with child, whose name must be Zacharias; be of good cheer, he shall do well as this one does!” 

Dec. 22nd, I paid Jane 13 shillings and 4 pence for her wages until last Michaelmas <note>Sept 29th; one of the traditional "quarter days" when servants were paid.</note>, for the half year, so that I still owe her 6 shillings and 8 pence. 

Dec. 28th, I revealed to Roger Coke <note>Roger Cooke was a long-time assistant to Dee. They eventually had a bitter falling out over these very alchemical secrets.</note> the great secret of the elixir of the salt of **acetum** <note>original: "ακετελς"; phonetic English for "acetels," likely referring to distilled vinegar or "spirit of vinegar" used as an alchemical solvent.</note>, one upon a hundred. <note>This "one upon a hundred" refers to the potency or "multiplication" of the alchemical medicine—its ability to transform one hundred times its own weight into gold or to heal.</note>

### 1580.] DR. DEE'S DIARY.
->CAMD. SOC.—DEE. c<-

...made my bows to her Majesty; she beckoned with her hand for me; I came to her coach side, she very quickly pulled off her glove and gave me her hand to kiss; and in short, asked me to frequent her court, and to let her know when I came there; at 6:15 in the afternoon <note>original: "hor. 6 1/4 a meridie"</note>. September 14th, I began my legal case against Vincent Murphyn. September 15th, I wrote to the Bishop of London. September 22nd, my declaration against Vincent Murphin was put into the court of Guildhall <note>The Guildhall was the center of administration and law in the City of London.</note>.

October 3rd, on Monday, at 11 o’clock in the morning, I delivered my two rolls regarding the Queen’s Majesty’s title <note>These "rolls" were historical and genealogical documents Dee prepared to justify Elizabeth's "British Empire," claiming her right to North America and other lands based on ancient legends.</note> to her own hands in the garden at Richmond, who appointed a time after dinner to hear more of the matter. Therefore, between one and two in the afternoon, I was sent for into her Highness's **Privy Chamber**, where the **Lord Treasurer** <note>William Cecil, Lord Burghley, the Queen's most powerful advisor.</note> also was, who, having the matter then under brief consultation, did seem to doubt much that I had or could make the argument probable for her Highness's title as I claimed. Whereupon I was to declare to his honor more plainly, and at his leisure, what I had said and could say therein, which I did on Tuesday and Wednesday following, at his chamber, where he treated me very honorably. October 7th, on Friday I came to my Lord Treasurer, and he being told of my being outside, and also I standing before him at his coming forth, did not or would not speak to me; I do not doubt some new grievance was conceived. October 10th, the Queen’s Majesty, to my great comfort (at five o'clock <note>original: "hora quinta"</note>), came with her train from the court and at my door graciously calling me to her, while on horseback, exhorted me briefly to take my mother’s death patiently <note>Dee's mother, Jane Dee, had lived with him at Mortlake until her recent death.</note>, and withal told me that the Lord Treasurer had greatly commended my work regarding her title, which he had to examine, and which title in two rolls he had brought home two hours before; she remembered also how at my wife’s death it was her fortune likewise to call upon me.* At 4—

\* His first wife <note>Katherine Constable</note> died on the 16th of March 1575, when "the Queen's Majesty, with her most honorable Privy Council, and other her Lords and Nobility, came purposely to have visited my library: but finding that my wife had been buried from the house only four hours before, her Majesty refused to come in; but desired me to fetch my glass <note>A reference to Dee's "shew-stone" or scrying crystal, which he used for divination and spiritual communication.</note>

### 1581.] DR. DEE'S DIARY.

...at [unclear] o’clock in the morning my mother Jane Dee died at Mortlake; she made a godly end: God be praised therefore! She was 77 years old. October 20th, I had £100 in damages awarded to me by my jury at Guildhall against Vincent Murphyn the <term>cosener <note>A sixteenth-century term for a small-time swindler or fraudster.</note></term>. October 22nd, with much effort I received judgment against Murfin at Guildhall. A marvelous hoarseness and practically a state of speechlessness took hold of me, though I was not otherwise sick at all. October 25th, Morrice Kyffin departed from my service with my leave. November 2nd, the Lord Treasurer <note>William Cecil</note> sent me a haunch of venison. Thomas Suttley had the Bishop of Canterbury’s letter for Sir Richard. November 3rd, I wrote to my Lord Treasurer. November 6th, Helen came into my service. November 12th, somewhat better in my voice. November 22nd, the <term>blazing star <note>original: "blasing star." A contemporary term for a comet. Dee includes a sketch of it in the margin.</note></term> <margin>Dee has made a rough sketch of the appearance of this comet, with its long tail, on the margin of the manuscript.</margin> I could see no more, though it were a clear night. December 1st, news came by Dr. Deny from Ireland of the defeat of the Italians whom the Pope had sent <note>This refers to the Siege of Smerwick, where a papal-backed invasion of Ireland was crushed by English forces.</note>, the Queen then staying at Richmond. December 6th, the Queen moved from Richmond. December 8th, I received letters from Rome, written by brother Laudervicea <note>original: "recepi literas Roma, scriptas per fratrem Laudervicea"</note>.

1581. <margin>An original diary of the chemical experiments made by Dr. Dee in this year is preserved in the Bodleian Library.—MS. Rawl. Miscel. 241.</margin> February 9th, I agreed with Mr. Gentle Godolphin to release the swindler Vincent Murphin. February 11th, Harry Prise, of Lewisham, came to me at Mortlake and told of his dreams, which were often repeated; and upon my prayer to God this night, his dream was confirmed, and better instruction given. February 12th, Sir William Herbert came to Mortlake. February 23rd, I made acquaintance with <term>Jean Bodin <note>original: "Joannes Bodinus." A famous French jurist and political philosopher whose works on witchcraft and sovereignty were highly influential.</note></term>, in the Presence Chamber at Westminster, the ambassador being present from <term>Monsieur <note>The Duke of Anjou, the Queen's French suitor.</note></term>. February 26th, a very fair, calm, warm day.

---

—***Compendious Memorial***, p. 516. This glass <note>Dee’s "glass" usually refers to his scrying stone or obsidian mirror used for spiritual communication.</note> is spoken of again.

### 1581.] DR. DEE'S DIARY. 11

<margin>
<unclear>Sketch of an owl or a vessel</unclear>

March 8th, it was the 8th day, being Wednesday, at 10 or 11 o’clock at night, there was a strange noise of knocking in my chamber; and a voice, repeated ten times, somewhat like the screech of an owl, but more long-drawn and more soft, as if it were inside my chamber. March 12th, all accounts paid to Mr. Hudson, £11. 17s. March 13th, Elizabeth Kyrton came into my service. March 23rd, Hugh Smyth came to me at Mortlake, having returned from the Straits of Magellan and <term>Vaygatz <note>An island in the Arctic, now Vaygach. Smyth was likely involved in the search for a Northeast Passage.</note></term>; after that, rainy, stormy wind from the South West. March 25th, Helen was hired on Lady Day for the year for a wage of four <term>nobles <note>A gold coin worth about 6 shillings and 8 pence.</note></term>; she received her <term>covenant penny <note>A small sum given to seal a contract of employment.</note></term>, and also 6s. 8d. for the work she has done since she arrived. April 3rd, I rode toward Snedgreene to see John Browne, to hear and see the manner of his activities. April 14th, I came home from Snedgreene. May 25th, I was offered sight in the <term>Crystal <note>original: "Χρυσταλλω" (Chrystallo). This is a pivotal entry, marking Dee’s first successful attempt at scrying or "crystal-gazing."</note></term>, and I saw. June 7th, at 7:30 in the morning, Katherine Dee was born. June 10th, Katherine was baptized at 5:30 in the afternoon. Mr. Packington of the court, Lady Katherine Crofts (wife to Sir James Crofts, the Controller of the Queen’s household), and Mistress Mary Skydmor of the <term>Privy Chamber <note>The private apartments of the Queen; Mary was a close personal attendant and cousin to Queen Elizabeth I.</note></term>, acted as godparents by their deputies and christened Katherine Dee. June 17th, young Mr. Hawkins, who had been with Sir Francis Drake, came to me at Mortlake. June 30th, Mr. John Leonard Haller, of Hallerstein, near Worms in Germany, received many instructions for his journey to <term>Quinsay <note>An old name for Hangzhou, China, often used by explorers following Marco Polo’s accounts.</note></term>; I encouraged him to take this journey and instructed him thoroughly on the variation of the compass, which he is to observe in all the places he passes through.

<margin>
Drake
[compass]
</margin>

July 6th, my wife <term>churched <note>A traditional ceremony of blessing and thanksgiving for a woman after childbirth.</note></term>. July 7th, in the morning at 1:45 after midnight, Mr. Hinde’s son was born. July 10th, my right shoulder and elbow joint were in such extreme pain that for 14 days I was not able to lift my arm outward even an inch; the pain was extreme. I was treated by Mr. Larder, Mr. Alles, and Alice Davyes, and around the 25th day I began to mend. July 12th, about 10:30 in the morning, <note>The following text was originally written in Greek characters as a phonetic code to hide a private grievance.</note> **Roger’s incredible stubbornness and ungratefulness against me to my face; he was almost ready to lay violent hands on me, as Major Henry can partly tell.** <note>original: "ροψερ ις ινκρεδιβλε δογγεδνες ανδ ινγρατεφυλνες..." This refers to Roger Cook, an assistant with whom Dee had a very volatile relationship.</note> On the same day the Earl of Leicester fell

A "Mensa" is a magic square used in planetary magic.</note></term> or the <term>Seal of the Planets <note>original: "Sigillum Planetarum." These were symbolic or mathematical diagrams believed to harness planetary influences.</note></term>. Aug. 26th, about 8:30 (at night) a strange meteor in form of a white cloud crossing the <term>Milky Way <note>original: "galaxiam"</note></term>, when it lay north and south over our <term>zenith <note>The point in the sky directly above the observer.</note></term>; this cloud was at length from the South East to the South West, sharp at both ends, and in the west end it was forked for a while; it was about sixty degrees high, it lasted an hour, all the sky clear about, and fair starshine.

Sept. 5th, Roger Cook, who had been with me from his 14 years of age till 28, of a melancholic nature, picking and devising occasions of just cause to depart on the sudden, about 4 of the clock in the afternoon requested of me license to depart, whereupon rose hot words between us; and he, imagining with himself that he had on the 12th of July deserved my great displeasure and finding himself barred from view of my <term>philosophical dealing <note>This likely refers to Dee's secret alchemical experiments or his early scrying sessions with spirits.</note></term>—

...with Mr. Henrik, [he] thought that he was utterly withdrawn from intended goodness toward him. Despite Roger Cook’s improper behavior, I promised him that if he conducted himself well toward me now during his absence, I would give him one hundred pounds <note>A very large sum in 1581, equivalent to several years' wages for a skilled craftsman.</note> as soon as I could afford it out of my own clear means; and furthermore, if he lived a good life toward God and the world, I promised him some ingenious <term>alchemical <note>The study of transforming matter, specifically the pursuit of the Philosopher's Stone and the elixir of life.</note></term> experiments, by which he might earn an honest living. Sept. 7th, Roger Cook left my service for good. Sept. 29th, Robert Gardner, of Shrewsbury, entered my service.

Oct. 8th, I received news of chests of books found at Oundle in Northamptonshire; Mr. Barnabas Saul told me of them, but I found no truth in the report. Oct. 9th, Barnabas Saul, while sleeping in the . . . . . . . . hall, was strangely troubled by a <term>spiritual creature <note>Dee’s term for a supernatural entity, such as an angel or spirit.</note></term> around midnight. Oct. 13th, I rode to South Mimms. Oct. 14th, to St. Neots. Oct. 16th, stayed at Mr. Hickman's. Oct. 20th, at Towcester. Oct. 21st, Oxford, [visited] Dr. Cradock. Oct. 23rd, from Oxford to Wycombe. Oct. 24th, I returned home. Robert Hilton entered my service. Nov. 16th, the Queen moved to Whitehall, accompanied by <term>Monsieur <note>Francis, Duke of Anjou, a suitor of Queen Elizabeth and the younger brother of the King of France.</note></term>. Nov. 27th, I rode to Grinstead. Nov. 28th, to Goodman Wykham’s, two miles past Chailey near Lewes. Nov. 29th, I became acquainted with Mr. George Kilmer regarding Sir George’s books. Nov. 30th, I returned home. Dec. 1st, Katherine Dee’s nurse was paid 6 shillings, so nothing further is owed to her. Dec. 5th, Ellen my maid fell sick. Dec. 7th, George, my manservant, had a great fall from a ladder, at <note>original: "hora 10 fere mane"</note> about 10 o’clock in the morning. Dec. 8th, I sent a letter to Mr. Kilmer. Dec. 22nd, the Lord Chancellor’s son, Mr. Bromley, and Sir William Herbert visited me. Helen Cole was paid her wages and final settlement up to this Christmas, and thus left my service, having just recovered from her <term>ague <note>A fever or shivering fit, often associated with malaria-like symptoms.</note></term>. It was her wish to return to her friends.

1582. Jan. 11th, Robert Gardener asked for my permission to go live [elsewhere]...

Looking at standard translations of Dee's diary, it's often translated as "a bloody-looking soldier" or "bloody streaks."

### 1582.] DR. DEE’S DIARY. 15

Mr. Talbot <note>This name refers to Edward Kelley, who initially used the alias "Edward Talbot" when he first entered Dee’s service as a medium.</note> declared a great deal of Barnabas’s naughty dealing toward me, such as telling Mr. Clerkson ill things of me—that I was making use of his friend, that I was weary of him, and that I would so flatter his friend, the learned man, that I would borrow him from him. But his friend told me, before my wife and Mr. Clerkson, that a spiritual creature told him that Barnabas had censured both Mr. Clerkson and me. The injuries which this Barnabas had done me in diverse ways were very great. March 22nd, Mr. Talbot went to London, to begin his journey.

April 16th, Nurse Garet received her 6 shillings for her month ending on the 20th day. April 22nd, a goodly shower of rain this morning early. May 4th, Mr. Talbot went away. May 13th, Jane rode to Cheyham. May 15th, <note>original Latin: "nocte circa nonam cometa apparuit in septentrione versus occidentem aliquantulum; cauda versus astrum tendente valde magna, et stella ipsa vix sex gradus super horizontem."</note> at night around nine o'clock, a comet appeared in the north, slightly toward the west; its tail was very large and stretched toward the stars, and the star itself was barely six degrees above the horizon. May 20th, <note>original Latin: "Robertus Gardinerus Salopiensis lætum mihi attulit nuncium de materia lapidis, divinitus sibi revelatus de qua . . . . ."</note> Robert Gardener of Shrewsbury brought me joyful news concerning the matter of the <term>Philosopher’s Stone <note>The legendary substance capable of turning base metals into gold and granting eternal life.</note></term>, which was divinely revealed to him, concerning which... May 23rd, Robert Gardener declared unto me at 4:30 o'clock a certain great philosophical secret, as he had termed it, from a spiritual creature; he was willed this day to come to me and declare it, which was solemnly done, and with common prayer. May 28th, Mr. Eton of London came with his son-in-law Mr. Edward Bragden, concerning the Upton parsonage, wanting me to resign or let it to his said son-in-law; I promised to let him know whenever I myself would consent to forego it. June 9th, I wrote a letter in Latin to the Archbishop of Canterbury: Mr. Doctor Aubrey carried it. June 14th, Maurice Kyffin visited me. June 22nd, Nurse Garret had 6 shillings for a month ending the 18th day of May; she is to have for a month—

---

* <margin>You that read this underwritten, assure yourself that it is a shameful lie, for Talbot neither studied for any such thing nor showed himself dishonest in anything. <note>This note was scrawled into the diary by Edward Kelley himself to discredit the account of his deceit.</note></margin> <gloss>This is Mr. Talbot—or that "learned man"—his own writing in my book, very unduly done as he came by it. <note>Dee’s later realization and response to Kelley's tampering with the diary.</note></gloss> There are several other notices of Talbot erased, but whether by him or by the Doctor it is impossible to say, but most probably the former.

<summary>This page details the growing friction between Dee's associates Barnabas Saul and Edward Kelley (Talbot), alongside Dee's observations of a comet and alchemical revelations from Robert Gardener. It also highlights an instance where Kelley personally tampered with Dee's diary to hide his own "naughty dealing."</summary>

<meta>This page continues Dr. John Dee’s diary entries for the summer of 1582. It follows a period of professional and personal tension, particularly regarding his legal troubles and his relationship with Edward Kelley (using the name Mr. Talbot) and his former assistant Barnabas Saul.</meta>

<page-num>16</page-num>
### DR. DEE'S DIARY. [1582.

...wages ending the 15th day of this June. My wife went this Friday there with Benjamin. June 27th, Mistress Stafford arrested me <note>In the Elizabethan era, a "civil arrest" was often used to compel someone to appear in court or settle a debt.</note> at 11 o’clock: I paid everything in full.

July 3rd, at 12:15, Arthur Dee <note>Dee’s eldest son, who would later become a physician to the Tsar of Russia.</note> fell from the top of the Water-gate stairs down to the bottom, and cut his forehead on the right eyebrow. Sir Richard brought the rent. July 6th, in fear of being arrested by Proctor Lewys <note>A "proctor" was a legal practitioner in the ecclesiastical or admiralty courts.</note>: from 1:00 in the afternoon until 9:30 at night at the Doctor’s coming. July 12th, Proctor Lewys agreed with everything. July 13th, Mr. Talbot <note>Edward Kelley, Dee's medium, who was at this time using the alias "Edward Talbot."</note> came about 3 o'clock in the afternoon, with whom I had some words of unkindness: we parted friendly; he said that Lord Morley had Lord Mounteagle’s books. He promised me some of Doctor Myniver’s books. July 16th, Mr. William Pole, whom Phillip Simons—formerly barber to the old Earl of Tavistock—knows, came to me and made my acquaintance: he promised to come again within 14 days. Jane my wife went to Nurse Garret’s to pay her 12 shillings for her wages due until last Friday, which was Saint Margaret’s day, and brought her 12 pence for candles: she went by water <note>Travel by boat on the River Thames was the primary way to move between Dee's home in Mortlake and the City of London.</note>; Mistress Lee went with her, and Robyn Jackesbite. Jane this night was sorely troubled with a colic and cramp in her belly; she vomited more this Monday, and every night her soles grew stiff likewise.

<margin><unclear>Notes</unclear> {</margin>
At 3:30 in the afternoon <note>original Latin: "A meridie hor. 3 1/2"</note> Sir George Peckham <note>An explorer and merchant adventurer interested in North American colonization.</note> came to me to know the title for <term>Norumbega <note>A historic name for the region in northeastern North America, roughly encompassing present-day New England and the Maritime provinces.</note></term> in respect of Spain and Portugal’s division of the whole world’s discoveries <note>The OCR reads "distilleryes," but given the context of Peckham and Norumbega, this is likely a transcription error for "discoveries" or "distributions."</note>. He promised me a gift from himself and from his patient . . . . . . of the new conquest, and thought to get as much of Mr. Gerard’s gift to be sent to me with a seal within a few days. July 18th, Barthilmew Knaresburgh’s son was born at break of day about 3 o'clock. June 19th <note>This likely refers to July 19th, as it follows July 18th in the manuscript sequence.</note>, Barnabas Saul came to see me at <term>Mortlake <note>The location of Dee's home and his famous library, which was one of the largest in England at the time.</note></term>: I scolded him for his many untrue reports. July 23rd, Mistress Franklin’s son was born at noon. July 24th, Robert Gardiner came, and went on the 26th.

August 8th, Kate <note>Katherine Dee, John Dee's daughter.</note> was sickly. August 11th, Mr. Bacon <note>Likely the young Francis Bacon, who was beginning his rise at court.</note> and Mr. Phillips of the court came. August 20th, Katherine still seemed to be diseased. August 25th, Katherine was taken home from Nurse Garret.

**Aug. 31st**, Benjamin Lock told me of his father's mind to send him to Spain within three or four days. 

**Sept. 1st**, I devised for Sir John Killegrew a way of protestation <note>A formal legal declaration used to avoid liability or preserve rights.</note> to keep him from legal harm regarding a settlement with a Spaniard who had been robbed: he promised me fish for Lent. 

**Sept. 10th**, Mr. John Leonard Haller, of Hallersteyn, near Worms in Germany, came again to me to declare his readiness to travel toward Quinsay <note>Quinsay was the name used by Marco Polo for Hangzhou, China; it reflects Dee's ongoing interest in global exploration and the Northwest Passage.</note>; and how he would stay at Venice all this winter, and from there travel to Constantinople. I requested Mr. Charles Sted to help him transfer his money to Paris and Nuremberg, and to help him with the <term>searcher</term> <note>A customs official responsible for inspecting goods and passengers.</note> of Rye to let his horse pass, and to help him with Mr. Osborne the alderman <note>Edward Osborne, a wealthy merchant and a founder of the Levant Company, which traded with the Ottoman Empire.</note> regarding his letters to Constantinople.

**Oct. 13th**, I rode from Tonbridge to Mr. Covert's at Slaugham. 

**Oct. 15th**, I came home from Slaugham, and dined at Mr. Holten’s, the <term>parson</term> <gloss>parson</gloss> of Oxted near Tonbridge, who is also a <term>physician</term> <note>In the 16th century, it was common for rural clergy to supplement their income and serve their community by practicing medicine.</note>.

...to my house to burn my books when I was dead, and I thought he looked sourly at me. **Dec. 1st**, George my servant, who had stayed out all night, this morning treated me very dishonestly and said he owed me no service. Mr. Bettgran the justice was not at home. **Dec. 13th**, thunder in the afternoon and at sunset. **Dec. 15th**, the 15th day being counted as the 25th, so ten days are imagined as spent, which have crept in between the day of Christ's birth regarding the position of the sun; the sun's position is not actually the 25th day of this month according to a civil equation, but mathematically and religiously it must be substantiated to be the true term of the periods of the annual revolutions of the sun since the day of Christ's birth. <note>Dee is discussing the Gregorian calendar reform. In 1582, many Catholic countries skipped 10 days to realign the calendar with the solar year. England, a Protestant nation, resisted this change, but as a mathematician and astronomer, Dee was tasked by the Queen to calculate its validity.</note>

<margin><unclear>read / under / note</unclear></margin>

**1583. Jan. 13th**, on Sunday the stage at Paris Garden <note>A popular entertainment area on the south bank of the Thames known for theaters and animal blood sports.</note> fell down all at once, being full of people watching the bear-baiting. Many were killed by it, more were hurt, and all were amazed. The godly explain it as a deserved plague from God for the wickedness used there, and for the Sabbath day being so profanely spent. **Jan. 19th**, Mr. John Leonard Haller went to London and is then to go toward Scotland. **Jan. 23rd**, the Right Honorable Mr. Secretary Walsingham <note>Sir Francis Walsingham, Queen Elizabeth I’s principal secretary and "spymaster."</note> came to my house, where by good luck he found Mr. Adrian Gilbert, and so talk was begun of the discovery of the <term>North-west Straits</term> <note>The "Northwest Passage," a sea route through the Arctic connecting the Atlantic and Pacific.</note>. The Bishop of St. David’s (Mr. Middleton) came to visit me with Mr. Thomas Herbert. Lord Grey came to see Mr. Secretary, and so they went to Greenwich(?). **Jan. 24th**, I, Mr. Adrian Gilbert, and John Davis <note>A famous English explorer who later made three voyages in search of the Northwest Passage.</note> went by appointment to see Mr. Secretary at Mr. Beale’s house, where only we four were in secret; we made Mr. Secretary privy to the <term>Northwest Passage</term>, and all charts and <term>rutters</term> <note>A "rutter" (from the French 'routier') was a book of sailing directions, providing coastal landmarks, depths, and tides.</note> were agreed upon in general.

**Feb. 2nd**, Roland Dee was baptized <note>original: "Rolandus Dee baptizatus"</note>. **Feb. 3rd**, Mr. Savile and Mr. Powell the younger, both travelers, and Mr. Ottomeen's son, came to be acquainted with me. **Feb. 4th**, Mr. Edmunds of the Privy Chamber and Mr. Lee, who had been in Muscovy <note>The Grand Duchy of Moscow/Russia.</note>, came to be acquainted with me. **Feb. 11th**, the Queen, staying at Richmond, went to Mr. Secretary Walsingham's for dinner; she, coming by my door, gra- <note>The sentence likely concludes "graciously greeted me," a common occurrence in Dee's diary when the Queen passed his home at Mortlake.</note>

...graciously called me to her, and so I went by her horse's side as far as where Mr. Hudson lived. <margin>]</margin> <term>Her Majesty asked me secretly about Monsieur's state: I said he will die a violent death. <note>original: "Ερ μαιεστι αξεδ με οβσκυρελι οφ μουνσιευρις στατε : διξι βιοθανατος εριτ." Dee wrote this entry in a Greek-character cipher for privacy. "Monsieur" refers to the Duke of Anjou, a suitor of the Queen. Dee predicts his death using the Latin "dixi" (I said) and the Greek-derived "biothanatos" (one who dies violently).</note></term> Roland went with his nurse to her house at East Sheen. **Feb. 18th**, the Lady Walsingham came suddenly into my house very freely, and shortly after she had gone, Sir Francis himself <note>Sir Francis Walsingham, Queen Elizabeth's Secretary of State and spymaster.</note> and Mr. Dyer came. **Feb. 24th**, Jane churched. <note>Dee's wife, Jane Fromond, underwent the "Churching of Women," a blessing and purification ceremony after childbirth.</note> **Feb. 26th**, I delivered my book to the Lord Treasurer <note>Lord Burghley</note> for the correction of the Calendar.

<margin>]</margin> **March 6th**, I, and Mr. Adrian Gilbert and John Davis, did meet with Mr. Alderman Barnes, Mr. Townson and Mr. Young and Mr. Hudson, about the Northwest voyage. <note>The search for the "Northwest Passage" to the Pacific; John Davis was a famous explorer who later gave his name to the Davis Strait.</note> **March 17th**, Mr. John Davis went to Chelsea with Mr. Adrian Gilbert to Mr. Radforth's, and so on the 18th day from there toward Devonshire. **March 18th**, Mr. North arrived from Poland; after he had been with the Queen he came to me. I received greetings from Łaski, <note>Albrecht Łaski, a Polish nobleman who would later become Dee’s patron.</note> Palatine in Poland; greetings by Mr. North who came first to the Queen, and next to me was his message, at 12:00. <note>original: "hor. 12"</note> Nurse Lydgatt is at East Sheen.

---

* <note>This work, although never entirely printed, created much sensation at the time, and was the cause of considerable controversy among the politicians as well as the learned. The Memorial on this subject which Dee presented to the Privy Council has been printed by Hearne and others, but it is not generally known that the original manuscript of the actual treatise on the correction of the Calendar is still preserved in Ashmole's library, No. 1789, and is the very book which Dee alludes to above. It is inscribed "to the Right Honorable and my singular good Lord, the Lord Burghley, Lord Treasurer of England," with the following verses:—</note>
<margin>]</margin> > “To the **fact** <note>original: <term>ὁτι</term></note> and the **reason why** <note>original: <term>το διοτι</term></note>,
> I show the thing and reason why;
> At large, in brief, in middle wise,
> I humbly give a plain advice;
> For want of time, the time untrue
> If I have missed, command anew
> Your honor may. So shall you see
> That love of truth does govern me.”

<note>The work itself is entitled, "A Plain Discourse and Humble Advice for our Gracious Queen Elizabeth, her most Excellent Majesty to peruse and consider, as concerning the needful Reformation of the Common Calendar for the civil years and days accounting, or verifying, according to the time truly spent."</note>

## DR. DEE'S DIARY. [1583.

<margin>Raleigh</margin>
...was paid for 5 pounds of candles, 6 pounds of soap, and the wages due since the birth of Rowland <note>Rowland was Dee's second son, born earlier that year.</note>. **April 18th**, the Queen went from Richmond toward Greenwich, and at her departure on horseback, having just risen, she called for me because Mr. Raleigh <note>Sir Walter Raleigh, the famed explorer and courtier, was one of Dee's many influential acquaintances.</note> reminded her of me, and she said "what is deferred is not taken away," <note>original: "quod defertur non aufertur"; a Latin proverb meaning that a delay in royal favor does not mean the favor is cancelled.</note> and gave me her right hand to kiss. **April 24th**, the nurse was paid all her wages for Rowland until Monday the 22nd of this month, at 16 pence a week; she had received all her candles and soap previously.

<margin>Ash[...]</margin> <note>Likely a reference to Elias Ashmole, the 17th-century antiquarian who later collected and annotated Dee's manuscripts.</note>

This refers to Ascension Day in the liturgical calendar.</note> **May 13th**, I became acquainted with Albert Laski at 7:30 at night, in the Earl of Leicester's chamber in the court at Greenwich. He came in the afternoon and stayed for supper, and departed after sunset. Nurse Rowland was paid in full until the 20th of this month. **June 15th**, about 5 o'clock, the Polish Prince Lord Albert Laski came down from Bisham, where he had lodged the night before, having returned from Oxford where he had gone specifically to see the universities, where he was very honorably treated and entertained. He had in his company Lord Russell, Sir Philip Sidney <note>The famous Elizabethan poet and soldier.</note>, and other gentlemen: he was rowed by the Queen's men, he had the barge covered with the Queen's cloth, the Queen's trumpeters, etc. He came specifically to do me honor, for which God be praised! **June 19th**, the Lord Albert Laski came to me and stayed at my house all night. Nurse Rowland was paid her wages ending the 17th day of this month.

\* “Her Majesty being informed by the Right Honourable Earl of Leicester, that whereas the same day in the morning he had told me that his Honour and Lord Laski would dine with me within two days after, I confessed sincerely unto him that I was not able to prepare them a convenient dinner, unless I should presently sell some of my plate or some of my pewter for it. # DR. DEE'S DIARY. [1586.

Letters of Jacob Memschitus. **Dec. 8th**, Monday about noon Mr. Edward Garland came to Třeboň to me from the Emperor of Muscovy <note>Tsar Feodor I of Russia, who was interested in recruiting Dee for his scientific and medical skills.</note>, according to the articles before sent unto me by Thomas Hankinson. **Dec. 11th**, Stephen of Poland <note>original: "St. Poloniensis," referring to Stephen Báthory, King of Poland.</note> died: born in the year 1530 on the 13th day of January, at the fourth hour of the morning, 26 minutes, in Transylvania. He died at the second hour after midnight, as I understood from the letters of Lord Laski <note>Prince Albert Laski, the Polish nobleman who first brought Dee and Kelley to the Continent.</note>, received on the 29th day through Alexander. **Dec. 19th**, the 19th day (according to the new calendar) <note>The Gregorian calendar, which was used in Catholic Bohemia but not yet in England.</note> to please Lord Edward Garland and Francis his brother—which Edward had been sent as a messenger to me from the Emperor of Muscovy so that I might come to him—<term>E. K.</term> <note>Edward Kelley.</note> performed a projection of the stone <note>An alchemical "projection" refers to the act of transforming base metal into gold using a small amount of the Philosopher's Stone.</note> in the proportion of one . . . . . . grain of sand upon one and a quarter ounces of common [metal], and nearly an ounce of the best gold was produced: which gold we afterwards distributed from the crucible; one [piece] we gave to Edward. **Dec. 30th**, <term>E. K.</term> toward Prague.

<margin>* He frequently speaks of Prince Albert Laski under the title of Most Illustrious.</margin>
<margin>† It is almost unnecessary to observe that these initials refer to Edward Kelley.</margin>

# DR. DEE'S DIARY.
### 1587.

...300 ducats for us. We received from the Most Illustrious Lord <note>Lord Vilém z Rožmberka (William of Rosenberg), Dee's primary patron in Bohemia.</note> 3300. **March 9th**, a royal journey. **March 14th**, we came to Reichstein <note>A town in Silesia known for its gold mines.</note>. **March 17th**, the return from Reichstein. **March 21st**, <term>E. K.</term> <note>Edward Kelley, Dee's medium and alchemical partner.</note> gave me 170 more, and of the 200 for changing 60 remain. Insults and contempt from Cholek and from Schonberg. **March 23rd**, we came to Třeboň. **March 26th**, the Lord Biberstein, coming from Krumlov <note>original: "Cranbaw," referring to Český Krumlov.</note> from the Lord Rosenberg, passing by Třeboň, sent for me to his inn to make acquaintance with me. <term>E. K.</term> rode to Krotovia. **April 4th**, the third action <note>Dee used the term "action" to refer to a spiritualist session or "scrying" session where they communicated with angels.</note> began. **April 18th**, the end of the third action. **May 1st**, I saw (with my teacher showing the way) the Messenger Michael, not Mersael. Praise be to God and to my teacher <term>E. K.</term>! **June 14th**, the wedding of Mr. Thomas Kelley <note>Edward Kelley's brother.</note>. **June 17th**, after this full moon Jane had them not <note>original: "αφτερ θις φυλ μουν ιανε αδ θεμ υοτ." Dee wrote this in English using Greek characters to keep his wife Jane’s menstrual cycles private.</note>. **June 22nd**, Mr. Francis Garland went toward England from Třeboň.

**July 5th**, Sunday, I set the two earths with their water again upon them <note>An alchemical reference to his ongoing experiments with "the work."</note>. **July 9th**, Mr. Francis Pucci came and brought Christian Franken with him, who, he said, had now recanted his wicked book against Christ, whereof I was glad. The Most Illustrious Lord <note>Rosenberg</note> had come to Třeboň with his Lady. **July 11th**, a conference between the Most Illustrious Lord, Mr. <term>E. K.</term>, and me, in the afternoon, between us three. **July 13th**, Francis Pucci departed. **July 19th**, a certain kind of recommendation between our wives <note>This refers to one of the most famous events in Dee's life: Kelley claimed the angels commanded them to share everything "in common," including their wives, Jane Dee and Joanna Kelley.</note>. Next day saw the relenting of <term>E. K.</term> also by my Lord's entreaty. **July 20th**, the Most Illustrious Lord went away with his princess toward Krumlov <note>original: "Cremoniam," referring to the Rosenberg seat at Český Krumlov.</note>. **Aug. 13th**, I spoke in a friendly manner with Mr. Edward Kelley about those three vows. **Aug. 17th**, <term>E. K.</term> with his brother and Ludovic . . . . . . . . . . . . . **Aug. 18th**, we understood how <term>E. K.</term> went to Budweis <note>České Budějovice.</note> to bed, and went but this day at noon from thence. **Aug. 20th**, John Basset came to Třeboň. **Aug. 23rd**, Mr. <term>E. K.</term> came from Linz fair. **Sept. 1st**, Tuesday morning, covenanted with John Basset to teach the children the Latin tongue, and I do give him seven ducats by the quarter, and the term to begin this day; and so I gave him presently seven ducats of Hungary, in gold, before my wife. God speed his work! **Sept. 3rd, 4th**, continual rain, as it were.

for those two days. **Sept. 4th**, Basset’s commotion with Mr. Thomas Kelley. **Sept. 16th**, the Lord Biberstein came to Třeboň, and Cracht with him. **Sept. 22nd**, my Lord <note>Lord Rosenberg</note> came from Krumlov to Třeboň with my Lady. **Sept. 26th**, my Lord went toward Prague. **Sept. 28th**, I delivered to Mr. Edward Kelley (who earnestly required it as his portion) half of all the <term>animal</term> <note>original: "animall." In alchemy, this refers to the "Animal Stone," a specific substance or stage in the creation of the Philosopher’s Stone believed to have restorative powers.</note> which was made. K. terribilis expostulatio, accusatio..."</note> Edward Kelley’s terrible argument, accusation, etc., at three o'clock in the afternoon. **Nov. 17th**, John Basset had seven ducats in advance for his second quarter's wages, beginning the 1st. **Nov. 21st**, Saturday at night Mr. Francis Garland came from England to Třeboň and brought me a letter from Mr. Dyer <note>Sir Edward Dyer, a prominent courtier and friend to Dee.</note> and my brother Mr. Richard <note>Richard Dee.</note>. **Nov. 24th**, at the marriage upon Krizin, the Grand Captain disdained to come there to supper in the <term>Town Hall</term> <note>original: "Rad howse," from the German 'Rathaus.'</note> of Třeboň because Edward Kelley and I were there; and said further that we were . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . **Dec. 1st to 11th**, my Lord lay at Třeboň and my Lady all this time. **Dec. 10th**, Mr. John Carp went toward Prague to marry the maiden he had troubled <note>A polite 16th-century term meaning he had gotten her pregnant or compromised her reputation.</note>; for the Emperor's Majesty, by my Lord Rosenberg's means, had so ordered the matter. **December 12th**, a little after noon, Mr. Edward Kelley knocked over his lamp. The <term>spirit of wine</term> <note>High-proof alcohol (ethanol) used as fuel for lamps or as a solvent in alchemical experiments.</note> had burned down too low, and the glass vessel was not propped up with books around it as it usually was; because the glass tilted to one side, the spirit spilled out and burned everything on the table where it stood: linens and written books. These included the Book of Zacharius with the *Alkanor* <note>Likely referring to the works of the alchemist Denis Zachaire. "Alkanor" refers to a specific alchemical vessel or treatise.</note> which I translated out of French (which for some spiritual reason I could not save); Rowlaschy’s third book of philosophical waters; the book called *Angelicum Opus* <note>Latin: "The Angelic Work."</note>, consisting entirely of illustrations of the <term>Great Work</term> <note>The process of creating the Philosopher's Stone.</note> from beginning to end; the copy of the "Conclusions" by the man from <term>Badwise</term> <note>Budweis (modern České Budějovice in the Czech Republic).</note> regarding the **Transmutation** of metals; and 40 leaves in quarto titled <term>Extractiones Dunstani</term> <note>Latin: "The Extractions of Dunstan." Referencing St. Dunstan, a 10th-century Archbishop of Canterbury who, by Dee's time, was legendary as an alchemist.</note>, which Kelley himself had extracted and noted from Dunstan’s book. The original book of Dunstan had fortunately just been moved from the table onto the bed nearby.

<margin>$\times$</margin>
**1588. Jan. 1st**, about nine o'clock in the evening, Michael—behaving like a child with a sharp stick eight inches long and a small wax candle lit on the end of it—fell upon the floorboards in Mary’s chamber. The sharp point of the stick entered through the lid of his left eye toward the corner next to the nose and pierced through, so that a great deal of blood came out from under the lid in the very corner of the eye. The hole on the outside is no bigger than a pin's head. It was anointed with <term>St. John's oil</term> <note>An infusion of St. John's Wort flowers in oil, a traditional remedy for wounds and nerve pain.</note>. The boy slept well. God grant success to the rest of the cure! The next day it appeared that the first touch of the stick's point was at the very middle of the pupil of the eye, and so (by God's mercy and favor) it glanced off to the place where it entered. With the strength of his head and the "fire of his fullness" <note>Perhaps referring to the boy's youthful vitality or "animal spirits."</note>, I may yet see it healed, to the praise of God for His mercies and protection. 

**Jan. 11th**, Nicolas <note>Dee’s servant.</note> was sorely hurt around 8:30 at night. 

**Jan. 13th**, at dinner time, Mr. Edward Kelley sent his brother, Mr. Thomas Kelley, to me with these words: “My brother says that you study too much, and therefore, seeing it is too late to go today to <term>Cromlaw</term> <note>Krumlov (Český Krumlov), the seat of the Rosenberg family.</note>, he wishes you to come spend the time with him at play.” I went after dinner and played—he and I against Mr. Francis Gore and Mr. Robert—until supper time in his dining room; and

The end of our talk was a strange speech of Mr. Kelley to Francis.

The Lord and Lady <note>Vilém z Rožmberka (William of Rosenberg), Dee’s patron, and his wife.</note> came to Třeboň. September 12th, **hope confirmed** <note>original Latin: "spes confirmata." This likely refers to a successful step in an alchemical experiment.</note>. September 15th, the Lord Chamberlain came to Třeboň, and went away on the 17th. The bitterness and deceit are now obvious to me; God deliver me! I was not sent for <note>Dee feels increasingly isolated and excluded from the meetings between Kelley and their high-ranking visitors.</note>.

October 25th, Mr. Edward Kelley and John Carpio rode toward Prague: tonight they stayed at Veselí <note>original: "Wesely," likely Veselí nad Lužnicí.</note>, two miles away. November 5th, I dreamed that the tooth next to my top tooth could scarcely hang in my head—the tooth on the upper right side <note>In the folklore of the time, dreaming of losing teeth was often interpreted as an omen of the death of a friend or relative.</note>. November 6th, Mr. Kelley came home from Prague along with Mr. Francis Garland and Edward Rolls from Cheb <note>original: "Eglis," the German name for the city of Cheb is Eger.</note>. November 15th, in the forenoon, snow and thick clouds. November 16th, the Lord and Lady Rožmberk came from Krumlov to Třeboň in the evening. November 20th, this Sunday before dinner, the Lord and Lady Rožmberk went from Třeboň toward Prague. November 23rd, Mr. Francis Garland and Edward Rowly (Mr. Dyer’s servant) went from Třeboň toward England. I wrote to the Queen’s Majesty, Mr. Dyer, Mr. Young, and Edward Hilton. December 4th, I gave to Mr. Edward Kelley my <term>Glass</term> <note>A "glass" or "shew-stone"—a polished crystal or mirror used by Dee for "scrying" or communicating with spirits.</note>, which is so highly and long esteemed by our Queen, and by the Emperor Rudolf II <note>original: "Randolph the second."</note>, **of which mention is made in the preface to Euclid.** <note>original Latin: "de quo in præfatione Euclidis fit mentio." This refers to Dee's 1570 preface to the first English translation of Euclid's Elements.</note> The letter of 500,000 ducats was required. December 7th, **great friendship promised for many, and two ounces of the thing.** <note>Dee wrote this sentence using Greek characters to keep it secret: "γρεατ φρενδσιπ προμισιδ φορ μανι, ανδ τνο συνκεσ οφ θε θινγ." The "thing" is almost certainly the "powder of projection" used in alchemy to attempt to transmute base metals into gold.</note> December 13th, Mr. Edward Kelley...

---

<note>* This refers to the earliest English translation of Euclid by Billingsley, which was published in 1570, with a long preface by Dr. Dee. Professor De Morgan is of the opinion that the translation also was by Dee, or that Billingsley may have been only a pupil who worked immediately under his directions. The passage to which Dee alludes is as follows:—"A man might be terribly afraid of his own shadow; yes, so much so that if you, being alone near a certain glass, were to offer to strike at the glass with a dagger or sword, you would suddenly be moved to pull back (in a way) because of an image appearing in the air between you and the glass with a similar hand, sword, or dagger, and with the same speed, striking at your very eye just as you do at the glass. This is strange to hear of, but more marvelous to behold than these words of mine can describe; and nevertheless, through a demonstration of optics, the order and cause of it is proven; even so, as the effect follows the cause." I also refer the reader to Mr. Barlow's History of Optics in the Encyclopedia Metropolitana.</note>

<summary>In late 1588, John Dee records the movements of his patron, the Rožmberks, and his associate Edward Kelley, while noting his own feelings of being excluded. The page is notable for Dee gifting his famous scrying glass to Kelley and a coded entry regarding an alchemical "thing" (likely the philosopher's stone).</summary>

...ear given by her mother did bleed at the nose very much <note>The previous page ended with a mention of a child; this likely refers to one of Dee's daughters receiving a blow to the ear or a medicinal treatment that resulted in a prolonged nosebleed.</note>, which did stay for an hour and more; afterward she did walk into the town with nurse; upon her coming home she bled again.

May 11th, John of Glotz <note>likely Hans von Glande, an associate of Dee.</note> came to Bremen. May 13th, I came to lie at my hired house. May 17th, the three saddle horses put to grass to the town meadows for nine ducats until Michaelmas <note>September 29th, a major feast day used as a quarterly marker for debts and contracts.</note>.

May 21st, the Landgrave of Hesse <note>William IV, Landgrave of Hesse-Kassel, a prominent German prince and astronomer.</note> his letters to me and the city of Bremen. May 25th, I sent the Landgrave my twelve Hungarian horses. June 2nd and 13th, Mr. Duerend and Mr. Hart went toward Stade <note>A port city near Hamburg where the English Merchant Adventurers had their base.</note>. They had escaped from the Spanish service in Flanders with Sir William Stanley <note>An English commander who famously defected to the Spanish side in 1587; Dee’s associates were likely returning to English loyalty.</note>. July 6th, Thursday the 26th of June (by the old account and the 6th of July by new account) <note>Dee is noting the ten-day difference between the Julian calendar (Old Style) still used in England and the Gregorian calendar (New Style) adopted by many continental regions.</note> Mr. Hart, the minister of the English company, and Mr. ...... the governor's deputy of the English company at Stade, did visit me at my house in Bremen.
