August 2nd, <term>in the old style</term> <note>original Latin: "veteri stilo."</note>, the night following, my terrible dream that Mr. Kelly would by force bereave me of my books, toward daybreak. <note>By this time, the relationship between Dee and Edward Kelley had soured; Dee’s dream reflects his fear that Kelley would seize his valuable library and alchemical manuscripts.</note> August 5th, <term>in the new style</term> <note>original Latin: "novo stylo."</note>, Edmond Hilton went toward Stade, to go toward England, with my letters to disclose the treason of Perkins <note>Christopher Perkins, a former Jesuit and diplomat whom Dee suspected of working against English interests.</note>. There went in this company two English people, Mr. Rolous Tattin and George Losin. August 7th, the first of the seven half-fasts. August 14th, Theodor went. August 21st, John Hammond to Stade. September 12th, the wind came East after five weeks mostly West. September 16th, <term>at the hour before noon</term> <note>original Latin: "ante meridiem hora."</note>

# DR. DEE'S DIARY. [1589.

On the 9th, in the year of Christ, I fell into <term>faintings</term> <note>original: "delinquiis"—likely referring to fits of depression or physical swooning caused by emotional distress.</note> brought on by the ingratitude I perceived in the words of my wife and Anne Marie. September 22nd, <term>Old Style</term> <note>original: "stilo veteri"—the Julian calendar used in England, which was then ten days behind the Gregorian calendar used in continental Europe.</note>, I delivered my letters to Mr. Jacob to be sent to England by way of Emden.

October 3rd, Dr. Witischindi’s hard dealings with me: he told Mr. Harper the Secretary to give me notice to leave my house. October 9th, I was warned out of my house <term>at one o'clock in the afternoon</term> <note>original: "hora prima a meridie."</note>. October 14th, John Hanward gave me notice that he desired to go travel toward Italy; but first to go to Master Kelley, from whom he hoped to have good help. October 17th, Mr. Southwell and Mr. Gawyne Smith came to me at Bremen. October 23rd, Mr. Southwell and Mr. Smith departed from Bremen. October 29th, Winifred Goose <term>between 9 and 10 in the evening</term> <note>original: "inter 9 et 10 a meridie." This likely refers to a birth or a specific domestic event regarding a servant or child.</note>. October 31st, letters sent to Stade for Gerwein Greven for her Majesty, Mr. Young, and Mr. Dyer. November 1st, news of Mr. Dyer being sent as ambassador to Denmark. November 3rd, <term>Old Style</term>, I resolved to go into England, hoping to meet Mr. Edward Kelley at Stade, who was also going into England; and I suspected this based upon the letters of Mr. Secretary Walsingham. November 13th, Edmond Hilton and his brother arrived from England, and John of Glotz. November 17th, <term>on Monday</term> <note>original: "die lunæ."</note>, I met Mr. Dyer coming to Stade, right in the middle of the town. November 18th, Edmond went toward Stade. November 19th, I took ship on the *Vineyard*. December 2nd, we came into the Thames at Gravesend. December 3rd, from the ship to Stratford to Mr. Young's house. December 19th, at Richmond with the Queen's Majesty. <note>According to the antiquarian John Aubrey, who received information from the astrologer William Lilly, Dee was very favorably received by her Majesty upon his return.</note> December 20th, I agreed with Nicholas Fromonds for my house, to occupy it as a tenant with better order. December 25th, I slept this night for the first time at my house. December 29th, Mr. Adrian Gilbert <note>A gentleman explorer and the half-brother of Sir Walter Raleigh; he was interested in alchemy and the Northwest Passage.</note> came to me at Mortlake and offered me as much as I could require from him, both regarding my goods that were carried away and for the mines.

1590. January 15th, a terrible tempest of wind, South by West. January 23rd, Mr. Thomas Kelley came from Brentford; he put me in good hope of Sir Edward Kelley's returning: he offered me the loan of ten pounds in gold, and afterward sent it to me in Hun— <note>The text cuts off, likely referring to "Hungarian ducats," a common gold currency of the time.</note>

->### CAMDEN SOCIETY — DEE. [Signature F]<-

...received new <term>ducats</term> <note>original: "ducketes" — gold coins used throughout Europe for trade</note> by John Croker, the same evening. January 26th, I wrote two letters to Mr. Adrian Gilbert. I decided on the terms to be offered for an agreement with Nicholas Fromonds regarding my house and goods. On the 5th of March (according to the <term>Old Style calendar</term> <note>original: "old accownt" — the Julian calendar used in England, which was ten days behind the Gregorian calendar used in Europe</note>), Madinia Newton, my daughter, was baptized at Mortlake; her godfather was Sir George Cary, and her godmothers were Lady Cobham and Lady Walsingham. March 12th, Mrs. Anne Deny was born between 8 and 9 in the evening. March 14th, Mr. Dyer came home from Stade <note>a port city in Germany</note>. March 17th, Sir Edward Kelley's letter arrived via Francis Garland. March 21st, Sir George Cary came to Mortlake. March 27th, Jane <term>was taken ill</term> <note>original: "apprehended" — in this context, it likely refers to the onset of a physical ailment or a specific domestic event</note> <term>at five o'clock in the afternoon</term> <note>original: "hora quinta a meridie"</note>. At this <term>Lady Day</term> <note>March 25th, the Feast of the Annunciation and the traditional start of the English legal year</note> in Lent, my children began to go to school at Mortlake with the schoolmaster Mr. Lee: I gave him his house-rent and forty shillings yearly for my three sons and my daughter. The house-rent was almost 4 shillings yearly for Mr. Fisher's new house. April 7th, <margin>Spenser</margin> John Spenser came to me, newly returned from Venice, and told me of the Venetian philosopher and the high quality of his gold. April 16th, good Sir Francis Walsingham died at night <term>at eleven o'clock</term> <note>original: "hora undecima" — Walsingham was Queen Elizabeth's famous "spymaster" and a patron of Dee</note>. April 19th, I delivered my letters to Mr. Thomas Kelley for his brother Sir Edward Kelley, knight, at the Emperor's court in Prague. Francis Garland was present, as was Mr. Thomas Kelley’s wife. God send them well there and back again! Mr. Emery had generously paid out to me £25 between <term>Shrovetide</term> <note>the period of preparation before Lent</note> and this May. May 5th, Mr. Thomas Jack restored to me part of my <term>lodestone</term> <note>original: "magnes stone" — a naturally magnetized mineral used for navigation and scientific study</note>. May 8th, I received 20 <term>marks</term> <note>a currency unit worth about 13 shillings and 4 pence</note> from Sir Richard Lagney, of Longlernay. May 16th, I gave Mr. Lee the schoolmaster 5 shillings as part of his wages. May 18th, <margin>[bracketed mark]</margin> the two gentlemen, the uncle Mr. Richard Cavendish, and his nephew the most famous Mr. Thomas [Cavendish]...

<note>The editor of the 1842 edition provided the following note regarding Jane Dee:</note>
There are a great many brief notices in this diary relative to Jane Dee, most of which are expressed in astrological symbols; and as they are exceedingly difficult to decipher satisfactorily, and are certainly of very little, if any importance, I have thought it expedient to omit them. The entry of "Jane <term>at her time</term> <note>original: "αδ θει" (ad thei) — Dee used Greek letters as a shorthand, likely referring to his wife's menstrual cycle or private domestic matters</note>" is also of frequent occurrence, though what "thei" can refer to I have not been able to discover.

**Candish**, <note>Richard Cavendish (c. 1560–1592), the famous English explorer and privateer who was the third person to circumnavigate the globe.</note> who had sailed round about the world, visited me at Mortlake. May 20th, after dinner, I, with my brother, Mr. Justice Young, went to the Archbishop of Canterbury at Lambeth, about the **parsonages** <note>original: "personagis" — ecclesiastical properties or benefices that provided income</note> who treated me well. <margin>pencil bracket</margin> May 21st, I showed my indignation against a **festival of Bacchus** <note>original: "Bacchus feast" — likely a "Whitsun Ale" or a similar rowdy village festival involving heavy drinking</note> intended at Brentford; I gave the Bishop of London warning, who took it in very good part. Katharine, my daughter, was placed with Mistress Brayce at Brentford; her mother and Arthur went with her after dinner. May 23rd, I lent to **Goodman** <note>A social title for a man below the rank of "gentleman," usually a farmer or craftsman</note> Dalton, the carpenter, 20 shillings for a month. <margin>pencil brace</margin> May 29th, 30th, **good news from the efforts of Mr. Richard Cavendish, with the Queen and the Archbishop and Sir George Carey, concerning the proposal for obtaining the legal rights for Eton College.** <note>original: "bona nova de industria Domini Richardi Candishii, cum Regina et Archiepiscopo et Domino Georgio Carey, de propositione Etonensis Collegii obtinendi legem."</note> He sent me a **hogshead** <note>a large cask containing about 63 gallons</note> of claret wine as a gift. The Lady Cobham sent my wife sugar and pepper, etc. June 2nd, I wrote to Sir Edward Kelley by Mr. William Fowler, a merchant living by Leadenhall. June 3rd, I was very sick after eating two or three sage leaves in the morning; I felt better suddenly at night; when I vomited them up, I was well. The pump was taken out and the well scoured. June 5th, Thomas Hankinson and Antony, my man, came from beyond the seas to Mortlake. June 5th, **terrible bad news of Edward Kelley against me.** <note>This marks a significant point of conflict between Dee and his former scryer and alchemical partner, who was then in favor at the court of Emperor Rudolf II in Prague.</note> June 24th, received £20 from Mr. Cavendish by Edward Hilton. June 28th, I paid Mr. Hudson for all his corn, and also for the wood until May, received since I came home.

July 6th, Mr. Stockden was paid in full for his wood, 40 shillings. I gave the schoolmaster Mr. Lee 5 shillings as part of his wages: he brought me my hammer from Mr. Jack, so he has a quarter's wages of 10 shillings. July 8th, I received Sir Edward Kelley's letters, dated at Prague the 24th of May **New Style**. <note>original: "stylo novo" — the Gregorian calendar, which was 10 days ahead of the Julian calendar used in England at the time.</note> No mention is made of his brother Mr. Thomas Kelley coming over. July 10th, the executor of Lady Ducket required the debt. July 11th, I paid Nurse Barwick 12 shillings for two months' wages for Madinia: so she is paid for five months.

-># 1590.] DR. DEE'S DIARY.<-

July 13th, I went to the Archbishop of Canterbury: talked with him boldly of my right to the **parsonages** <note>original: "personages" — ecclesiastical properties or benefices that provided income</note>, and to the treatise of Sir Edward Kelley's alchemy. July 14th, Mr. Gawayn Smyth spoke friendly for me to the Queen, and she disclosed her favor toward me. July 16th, I felt somewhat inclined to deal with my **alchemical exercises** <note>Dee’s practical laboratory work in the pursuit of transmuting metals or creating medicines</note>. July 25th, I wrote a letter of thanks to the Lord Treasurer <note>William Cecil, Lord Burghley, the Queen's most powerful advisor</note> by Edmond Hilton. I sent the Lord Chancellor his letters from Brunswick, regarding the ill behavior of Conrad Nettlebronner <note>A former associate of Dee and Kelley in Germany who had caused legal or financial trouble</note>. July 31st, I gave Mr. Richard Cavendish the copy of Paracelsus's twelve letters, written in French with my own hand; and he promised me, before my wife, never to disclose to any that he has it; and that if he dies before me he will restore it again to me; but if I die before him, that he shall deliver it to one of my sons, most fit among them to have it. Theoddor <note>Theodore, Dee’s son born in 1588</note> had a sore fall on his mouth at midday. Aug. 2nd, Mrs. Stoner's son born **around three o'clock in the afternoon** <note>original: "circa horam tertiam a meridie"</note>. The nurse is in a great affliction of mind. Aug. 5th, Rowland <note>Rowland Dee, another of John’s sons</note> fell into the Thames over head and ears about noon or somewhat after. Aug. 8th, I gave Nurse Barwick six shillings, so she is paid for the half year due on Wednesday next. Aug. 9th, I paid to Mr. Lee the schoolmaster **five shillings** <note>original: "5s."</note>. Aug. 22nd, Ann my nurse had long been tempted by a wicked spirit: but this day it was evident how she was possessed by him. **God is, has been, and shall be her protector and deliverer! Amen.** Aug. 25th, Anne Frank was sorrowful, well comforted and stayed in acknowledging God's mercies. Aug. 26th, at night I anointed (in the name of Jesus) Anne Frank's breast with the holy oil. Aug. 30th, in the morning she required to be anointed, and I did very devoutly prepare myself, and pray for **virtue and power** <note>original: "vertue and powr" — referring to the spiritual efficacy of the ritual</note> and Christ’s blessing of the oil to the expulsion of the wicked one; and then twice anointed, the wicked one did resist a while. Sept. 1st, I received letters from Sir Edward Kelley by Francis Garland. Sept. 8th, Nurse Anne Frank would have drowned herself in my well, but by divine Providence I came to take her up before she was overcome by the water. Sept. 23rd, Sunday, I gave

<meta>This page continues the chronological entries of Dr. John Dee’s diary for the autumn of 1590. It resolves the "great affliction" of Nurse Anne Frank mentioned on the previous page with a tragic account of her suicide, while also documenting Dee’s financial struggles and his ongoing relationship with Queen Elizabeth I and the high officials of her court.</meta>

-># DR. DEE'S DIARY. [1590.<-

Paid Nurse Barwick six shillings for a month's wages to end on Wednesday coming a fortnight; Mrs. Stackden was present. Sept. 29th, Nurse Anne Frank most miserably did cut her own throat, in the afternoon about four o’clock, pretending to be in prayer before her keeper, and suddenly and very quickly rising from prayer, and going toward her chamber, as the maiden her keeper thought, but indeed straightway down the stairs into the hall of the other house, behind the door, did that horrible act; and the maiden who waited on her at the stair-foot followed her, and missed finding her in three or four places, until at length she heard her rattle in her own blood.

Oct. 11th, Mr. Cumber came to me. Oct. 14th, paid Nurse Barwick six shillings for one month ending on the seventh, being Wednesday. Oct. 15th, this afternoon and all the night following a great storm of wind at North-West. One Prichard, that had married **Proctor Lewes’s widow** <note>A "proctor" was a legal practitioner in ecclesiastical or admiralty courts</note>, demanded £24 of me upon an **obligation** <note>A legal bond or written promise to pay a debt</note> of £64: whereof by the very note on the back of the same £48 is paid, so that £16 only remain and not £24, as he unduly demanded: which £16 I challenged for the costs of his son John, three years and longer being with me in Mortlake, and having also his learning free. Notwithstanding, my wife, being afraid, paid a pound or two to Mr. Lewys of that £16, and yet Prichard will go to law. Nov. 12th, the Archbishop of Canterbury gave me £5 in **ryals and angels** <note>Gold coins; a "ryal" was worth about 15 shillings and an "angel" about 10 shillings</note> **around ten o'clock in the morning** <note>original: "circa horam decimam matutina"</note>. Nov. 20th, Her Majesty came to Richmond. Nov. 27th, the Queen’s Majesty, being at Richmond, graciously sent for me. I came to her at three-quarters of the clock in the afternoon, and she said she would send me something to keep Christmas with. Nov. 28th, **Mr. Cavendish** <note>original: "Mr. Candish" — likely Richard Cavendish, a friend and patron</note> on Saturday gave my wife forty shillings, and on Tuesday after sent £10 in ryals and angels, and before he sent me £20, £32 in all. My cousin Mr. Thomas Jones came in the end of the term about **St. Andrew's Eve** <note>November 29th</note>. Dec. 1st, Her Majesty commanded Mr. John Herbert, **Master of Requests** <note>A judge of the Court of Requests, which handled the petitions of poor people and members of the Royal Household</note>, to write to the Commissioners in my behalf. ->### 1590.] DR. DEE'S DIARY. 37<-

...by the Commissioners for my house and goods. Her Majesty told Mr. Cavendish that she would send me an hundred **angels** <note>A gold coin depicting the archangel Michael, worth about 10 shillings at the time</note> to keep my Christmas with. Dec. 3rd, Goodwife Tyndale paid for Anthony's lodging for eleven weeks due at his going away: 5 shillings 6 pence; and before she had paid for seven weeks. Dec. 4th, the Queen's Majesty called for me at my door **around a quarter past three in the afternoon** <note>original: "circa 3¼ a meridie"</note> as she passed by, and I met her at East Sheen gate, where she graciously, putting down her mask, did say with merry cheer, "I thank thee, Dee; there was never promise made but it was broken or kept." I understood her Majesty to mean the hundred **angels** she promised to have sent me this day, as she yesterday told Mr. Richard Cavendish. Dec. 6th, Mr. Thomas Griffith my cousin from Llanbeder came to see me, and stayed all night with me, and also Mr. Thomas Jones; and on the Monday morning went by water to London, and so the same day homeward. **In the afternoon around 3 o'clock I received from the Lady Queen** <note>original: "A meridie circa 3a recepi a Regina Domina"</note> £50. Dec. 8th, at Chelsea disputing with *Doctor Mather*, Bishop of Bristol; in danger of the water <note>Likely referring to a dangerous crossing or high tide on the River Thames</note> **at half-past five** <note>original: "hora 5½"</note> I stayed at Chelsea. Dec. 14th, the Queen's Majesty called for me at my door as she rode by to take the air, and I met her at East Sheen gate. Dec. 16th, Mr. Cavendish received from the Queen's Majesty a warrant by word of mouth to assure me to do what I would in <term>philosophy</term> <note>The study of natural science and the underlying principles of the universe</note> and <term>alchemy</term> <note>The ancient branch of natural philosophy focused on the transformation of matter, particularly the pursuit of the Philosopher's Stone</note>, and none should check, control, or molest me; and she said that she would before long send me £50 more to make up the hundred pounds. I gave Mr. Cavendish the Bishop of Scotland's conclusion with merchants. Mr. Cavendish went from Mortlake at four o'clock at night toward London and so into Suffolk. Dec. 18th, Mr. Robert Maynard **was born around ten o'clock in the morning in London** <note>original: "natus circa horam decimam ante meridiem Londini"</note>.

1591. Jan. 21st, utterly put out of hope for recovering the two **parsonages** <note>Church appointments or "livings" that would have provided Dee with a steady clerical income</note>* by the Lord Archbishop and the Lord Treasurer. Feb. 13th, Bartholomew came up. March 2nd, borrowed £20 upon

May 12th, I paid Goodwife Welder 12 shillings for seven weeks ending then, starting from the Wednesday before Easter Day last. May 25th, of the old calendar, Sir Thomas Jones, Knight, (unasked) offered me his castle of Emlyn in Wales to dwell in so long as he had any interest in it; his lease lasts yet twelve years, and he offered it freely, with the <term>commodityes</term> <note>useful resources or conveniences</note> adjoining it; and also to have as much <term>mow land</term> <note>meadow land suitable for harvesting hay</note> for rent as might sufficiently please me. On the 27th day he confirmed the same offer again before Mr. John Herbert, Master of the Requests, in his hall in Mortlake; I did accept these offers, and he was glad of it. May 31st, Bartholomew <insert>Hickman</insert> came up and brought Jane his daughter with him. Also Mr. R. Ed. his book and letter. June 8th, William Aspland of Essex and Th. Collen. June 12th, on Saturday I lent the *Great Chronicle of Holland* <note>original: "Chronica Hollandiæ Magna"</note> to Mr. Beale, a manuscript which Mr. Webb lent to me. June 14th, Jane Hickman went to Goodwife Tyndall’s to learn. June 27th, Arthur <note>Dee's eldest son</note> was wounded on his head by his own <term>wanton</term> <note>reckless or playful</note> throwing of a <term>brick-bat</term> <note>a fragment of a brick</note> straight up, and not successfully avoiding the fall of it again; this happened at Mr. Herbert's around sunset. The half-brick weighed 2½ pounds. June 30, Madinia was taken home from Goodwife Welder.

July 28th, Mr. Dyer sent me 20 <term>angels</term> <note>Gold coins depicting the archangel Michael, worth about 10 shillings each</note> by Mr. Thomas Webb. July 30th, the reconciliation between Mr. Dyer and me was solemnized.

July 31st, by the old calendar, about eleven o'clock Jane <note>Jane Dee, John Dee's second wife</note> was at London very faint and sick, ready to swoon, and in a faint sweat. It was thought that then she quickened. <note>The "quickening" is the historical term for the first moment a pregnant woman feels the fetus move</note> <margin>]</margin> The last of July, Saturday by the old account, Bartholmew came up; he went down on Tuesday, the 3rd day of August, from Mortlake. December 14th, I had a very gentle answer at the Lord Treasurer's <note>William Cecil, Lord Burghley, the Queen's chief advisor</note> hand **at ten o'clock in the morning** <note>original: "hora decima ante meridiem"</note> at the court of Whitehall. December 20th, a gentle answer from the Lord Treasurer that the Queen would have me have something at this promotion of bishops at hand. <note>Dee was constantly seeking a permanent, income-providing position, such as a mastership or a high-ranking church office</note>

1592. January 1st, my daughter Frances born on New Year's day at the sun-rising exactly. January 2nd, Bartholmew and his brother Ambrose came this Sunday to Mortlake. January 9th, Frances christened in the afternoon. Frances went with her nurse to Barnes Elms. Mr. Edward Maynard born in the morning between 2 and 3 after midnight. Arthur <note>Dee's eldest son, then about 12 years old</note> fell into a <term>mild daily fever</term> <note>original: "quotidian jentle ague"; a fever that returns every day</note> at 9 o'clock in the morning as he was at the service in the hall. January 24th, Mr. Thomas Oliver became acquainted with me at Mortlake. March 6th, the Queen granted my suit to Dr. Aubrey. <note>Dr. William Aubrey, a Master of Requests and influential lawyer</note> March 9th, the <term>privy seal</term> <note>A seal used for documents of minor importance or as a warrant for the Great Seal</note> at night. March 16th, the <term>great seal</term> <note>The primary seal used by the monarch to symbolize royal assent to important documents</note>. March 18th, Arthur and Katharine were let blood at London by Doctor Dodding's counsel. <note>A standard medical treatment of the time involving the controlled withdrawal of blood to balance the "humors"</note> March 24th, £25 from Mr. Thomas Monson. March 25th, I paid £10 to Nicholas Fromonds <note>Dee's brother-in-law</note> **shortly before sunset** <note>original: "paulo ante solis occasum"</note>, when he most abominably reviled me. March 30th, on Thursday Mr. Saunders of Ewell sent home my great sea compass, but without a needle; it came in the night by water.

April 5th, the Lady Russell robbed a little after midnight of pearls, diamonds, etc. One John Smyth is suspected, a young man...

->### DR. DEE'S DIARY. [1592.<-

...of thirty years old, very skillful in many manual crafts, and of a melancholy temperament. April 8th, Richard entered my service, for 40 shillings a year and a uniform. July 29th, Robert Theneth of Rushmere, near Ipswich, introduced himself to me; he told me about a Mr. Carter, an eighty-year-old man in Yorkshire. August 6th, I went to the court at Nonsuch Palace, where the Countess of Warwick sent me word through Mr. Ferdinando about the Queen’s kind remarks made at St. Cross; the Lord Archbishop told me the same. August 8th, after midnight on Monday (the 7th), a second <term>attack of kidney stones</term> <note>original: "fytt of the stone"</note> in my kidneys troubled me for six or seven hours. ->### 1592.] DR. DEE'S DIARY.<-

...requested the Lord Treasurer <note>William Cecil, Lord Burghley, the Queen’s chief advisor</note> to help me obtain the position at St. Crosses <note>The Hospital of St. Cross in Winchester; Dee frequently sought the mastership of this wealthy charitable institution to solve his financial problems</note>, which he promised to do his best in. August 11th, Mr. Kemp of Mitcham, my old acquaintance, at almost eleven o'clock before noon, told me of the rare appearing. August 17th, I went to Mitcham to see Mr. Kemp. August 21st, I went to London to visit the Lord Cobham and Lady Cobham. August 23rd, Mr. Cholmely and his maid visited <term>at 11:30 in the morning</term> <note>original: "ante meridiem hor. 11½"</note>. A pain <note>Dee uses "humor," referring to the bodily fluids thought to govern health</note> so suddenly fell into the calf of my left leg that it felt as if a stone had hit me. August 26th, Mr. Heriot <note>Possibly Thomas Harriot, the famous mathematician and astronomer, or a London goldsmith</note> 40 shillings. Auditor Hill, £4. Remember that everything is paid to our nurse at Barnes for the girl Frances Dee <note>Dee’s daughter, born earlier that year</note> from her birth until the end of her eighth month, except for 12 shillings; and on Sunday, August 27th, we settled the account, at which time we gave the nurse ten shillings. The eight-month period ended (counting from the morning of last New Year's Day) on the 12th of this month. September 4th, 5th, and 6th, very tempestuous and windy from the Southwest. September 5th, the Thames was very shallow at London. September 6th, Goodman <note>A social title for a man of respected status but below the rank of a gentleman</note> Warren of Market Harborough visited. Robert Web came from Mr. Ponsoys to work as a scribe, and is to return again within three weeks. September 7th, Robert Charles of Northamptonshire and Goodman Warren of Market Harborough, while in my house at Mortlake, promised to help Bartholomew Hickman <note>Hickman was a long-time associate of Dee’s who later served as his medium or "scryer"</note> with £12 to pay on Michaelmas Day <note>The feast of St. Michael, September 29th, a standard day for settling debts and contracts</note> next to discharge the bond for his brother-in-law. This they promised on the condition that I would be legally bound to them to see that they were repaid. I sent a letter to Sir Robert Thaneth at Rushmere by Ipswich via the wagon-man who stays at the George Inn in Lombard Street. He said that Robert Thaneth was at home and well. September 19th, on Sunday at about 7 o'clock in the evening, I had the cramp most extremely in the very center of the calves of both my legs; and in the place where I had the sudden grief <note>pain</note> on St. Bartholomew’s Eve <note>August 23rd</note> last, I had pain so intolerable as if the veins or arteries would have broken by extreme stretching, or how else to describe it I cannot tell. The pain...

->### 1592.] DR. DEE'S DIARY.<-

...lasted about half a quarter of an hour. I took my purgative dose of six grains. I began in the morning to drink the medicinal drink for the stone in the kidney <note>Dee frequently recorded his treatments for kidney stones, a common ailment for him</note>. Sept. 28th, Mr. Laiesley promised me ten sheep and four quarters <note>A unit of weight or volume for grain, roughly 8 bushels</note> of wheat. Sept. 30th, Elizabeth Denby left my household to enter service at Mistress Herbert’s.

<margin><insert>Hand-drawn square bracket highlighting the entry for Oct. 13th.</insert></margin>
Oct. 13th, I presented to the Archbishop of Canterbury <note>John Whitgift</note> two books of blasphemy against Christ and the Holy Ghost, desiring him to cause them to be refuted: one was by Christian Franken, printed in the year <note>original: "anno"</note> 1585 in Poland; the other was by a certain Sombius against a certain Carolius, printed at Ingolstadt in the year 1582 in octavo <note>A book size where each sheet is folded to create eight leaves</note>. Oct. 14th, 15th, a mighty wind from the southwest. Oct. 30th, 31st, on one of these two days I hurt my left shin against the sharp small end of a wooden rammer <note>A heavy tool used for tamping earth or paving</note> at about four o'clock in the afternoon. Nov. 1st, Mr. Ashley, his wife, and their family, came to my house and remained there. They occupied my mother’s chamber, the maid’s chamber, and all the rest of the house. Nov. 9th, Her Majesty’s grant of my petition for commissioners to come to me. The Lady Warwick <note>Anne Dudley, Countess of Warwick, a close confidante of Queen Elizabeth and one of Dee’s most consistent supporters at court</note> obtained it. Nov. 22nd, the commissioners from Her Majesty, Mr. Secretary Woolley and Sir Thomas George, came to my house at Mortlake. Nov. 28th, paid Richard Walkdyne 20 shillings of his wages. Dec. 1st, a little after noon the very virtuous Countess of Warwick sent me word very quickly by her gentleman, Mr. Jones, from the court at Hampton Court, that today Her Majesty had granted to send me immediately one hundred marks <note>A mark was a unit of currency worth two-thirds of a pound; 100 marks was roughly £66, a substantial gift from the Queen</note>, and that Sir Thomas George had dealt very honorably for me in the matter. Dec. 2nd, Sir Thomas George brought me a hundred marks from Her Majesty. Dec. 24th to 31st, stayed at Mr. Luresey's in Tooting during all these days, and New Year’s Day also; we then came home by coach (just as we went) by Tuesday noon—myself, my wife, Arthur, Kate, etc. Dec. 31st, at Tooting at Mr. R. Luresey’s house; about three o'clock after dinner, the Bishop of Laigham served legal process <note>A formal summons to appear in court</note> upon me for the <term>nangle</term> <note>This term likely refers to a specific legal dispute or piece of property, though its exact meaning in this context remains a subject of scholarly debate</note>, but most unfairly.

1593. January, the Lord Treasurer <note>William Cecil, Lord Burghley</note> lay dangerously sick at the beginning of this month. Jan. 2nd, I came home from Tooting.

<meta>This page continues the diary of Dr. John Dee into the early months of 1593. It follows his efforts to maintain his household at Mortlake after the Queen's commissioners visited him in late 1592 to investigate his reputation and financial distress.</meta>

->### 1593.] DR. DEE'S DIARY.<-

Jan. 7th, I received letters from the Lord Lasky <note>Albertus Laski, a Polish nobleman who had previously hosted Dee and the medium Edward Kelley in Europe</note> from his governorship in Livonia <note>A historical region in the Baltic, now part of Latvia and Estonia</note>, and I wrote a reply. Jan. 10th, death claimed him today. This day at noon died Edward Maynard, exactly one year old. Jan. 11th, buried this day at ten o’clock. Jan. 15th, Mr. Ashley, the clerk of the council, his wife and whole family moved from my house in Mortlake to their house in Holborn, London, with his entire family. He and she had treated me, my wife, and children with great respect and generosity in exchange for our friendship shown to them in the loan of our house and lodgings since last All Saints’ Day <note>original: "Allhallow-tyde"</note>. Master Maynard also moved his household on the 15th and 16th day to London, and my stable was returned to me. Jan. 20th, I sent my letters for Lord Lasky to be carried in a ship from Danzig <note>original: "Dansk"</note> called the *John of Danzig*. Jan. 21st, Sunday, about noon, Wenefryde Goose’s son was born and died, and she did thereupon, in a fit of her old melancholy, destroy herself <note>A tragic reference to postpartum depression or psychosis leading to suicide</note>. Note, my nurse at Barnes had 16 shillings more besides the last 40 shillings in the beginning of this month. Feb. 14th, Francys Dee <note>Dee's daughter</note> came home from the nurse at Barnes; the woman was very restless and ungrateful. Feb. 15th, Her Majesty graciously accepted my few lines of thanks delivered to her by the Countess of Warwick at two o’clock in the afternoon <note>original: "hora secunda a meridie"</note> at Hampton Court, two or three days before the move to Somerset House. Feb. 21st, I borrowed £10 from Mr. Thomas Digges <note>A prominent English mathematician and astronomer</note> for one full year. Feb. 22nd, a sharp argument between me and the Bishop of Leighton in the Tower <note>The Tower of London, where the Bishop was presumably a prisoner</note>, because he would not show his further interest in Nangle: he said that after I had seen his great seal of commendation, I had received the formal appointment and installation <note>original: "institution and induction"</note> to Nangle. I then said his lordship was telling tales <note>original: "did fable"</note>. He was so moved by that that he spitefully called me a "conjurer" <note>OCR: "coniver." This was a common and dangerous accusation against Dee, implying he practiced illicit magic</note>. I told him that he lied in saying so, and that I would test his physical strength <note>original: "try on the fleysh of him"</note>, or give him a sound beating <note>original: "bastaned gown"</note>, if he were not a prisoner in the Tower.

---

<note>This notice is particularly interesting, showing the intimate connection which existed between the first English mathematician of the day, Thomas Digges, and the philosopher of Mortlake, John Dee.</note>

<meta>This page continues Dr. John Dee’s diary entries for the year 1593, following his return to regular life at Mortlake. It documents his social interactions with figures like the Lieutenant of the Tower, his recurring dreams of his former partner Edward Kelley, and his ongoing alchemical teaching in exchange for much-needed funds.</meta>

->### 1593.] DR. DEE'S DIARY.<-

At 12 o'clock and 2 o'clock in the afternoon, my sharp anger with the Bishop of Leighlin in the Lieutenant’s dining parlor before the Lieutenant Sir Michael Blunt <note>Sir Michael Blount was the Lieutenant of the Tower of London from 1590–1595</note>. Mr. Lieutenant Nant and Mr. Blunt are witnesses. March 12th and 13th, these two nights I dreamed much of Mr. Kelly <note>Edward Kelley, Dee's former assistant in alchemical and spiritual experiments, who was at this time in Europe</note>, as if he were in my house, familiar with his wife and brother. March 17th, Francis Garland came home and brought me a letter from Mr. Thomas Kelly. I made acquaintance with Sir Thomas Chaloner, Knight, who married Sergeant Fleetwood's daughter; Mr. Thomas Webbes was the intermediary. At six in the evening received from Mr. Francis Nicholls £15, part of one hundred pounds, the rest of which—£85—is to be received from Mr. Nicolls within a fortnight after the Feast of the Annunciation of Our Lady <note>Also known as Lady Day, celebrated on March 25th</note> next; and after that in the beginning of June £100, and in July the third hundred pounds: and I am to teach him the conclusion of fixing and tinning <term>the moon</term> <note>In alchemy, "the moon" (Luna) refers to silver; "fixing" and "tinning" (or teyning) refer to chemical processes intended to stabilize or transform the metal</note>, etc.

April 3rd, Bartholomew Hickman and Robert Charles came up. Letice came with Bartholomew, and went away again. April 8th, Letice came again from Barnet to enter my service. I received £50 of Mr. Nichols. April 9th, I gave Bartholomew Hickman £12 in new angels <note>Gold coins worth about 10 shillings, named for the image of the Archangel Michael</note> to give and pay to Robert Charles, which he had paid for him at Michaelmas <note>The feast of St. Michael, September 29th</note> last. I gave him also a double pistolet <note>A Spanish gold coin</note> for his courtesy. Little Adolph Webbes came to me. April 10th, Bartholomew and Robert Charles went homeward. May 7th, Thomas Richardson of Bisham came to Mortlake to see me. May 9th, he and Mr. Laward of the Chandry <note>The department of a royal or noble household responsible for candles and wax</note> came. Our court day at Wimbledon. May 11th, at eight o’clock in the morning <note>original: "mane hora octava"</note> William Emery of Danbury in Essex became my retainer <note>A domestic servant or dependent</note> at Mortlake, recommended by Mr. Thanet of Rushmere by Ipswich; he was born July 4, 1568. I gave Robert Web 10s., Richard 10s., and Elizabeth 3s. in the beginning of this month. May 21st, be it remembered that on this 21st day of May I bargained with and bought from Mr. Mark Pierpoint of Mortlake that next mansion house with the plot of land, and all the appurtenances about it for £32, just as the said Mr. Pierpoint had recently bought it at the last court-day and had received surrender of it.

->### 1593.] DR. DEE'S DIARY.<-

...made to him by Thomas Knaresborough for £25, due by Midsummer next. Around two o'clock in the afternoon, in the street and in front of my wife Jane, I gave him a <term>saffron noble</term> <note>A gold coin, so called for its yellow color, worth about 6 shillings and 8 pence</note> as <term>earnest money</term> <note>A small sum paid to confirm a contract</note> for a <term>drink-penny</term> <note>A tip or gratuity given to seal a bargain</note>. Mr. Hawkins, from London, arrived at that very moment intending to buy it.

May 27th, Mr. Francis Blunt—brother to the late Lord Mountjoy, uncle to the current Lord Mountjoy, and to Sir Charles of the court—came to introduce himself to me; he has recently been a traveler at Constantinople.

June 4th, Bartholomew Hickman came to Mortlake in the morning.

June 22nd, I received my copy of the <term>manor-roll</term> <note>A legal document proving land tenure</note> from our steward, Mr. Roger Dale, and had the £5 fine waived through the Lord’s generosity. I told the steward that I had bought the house from Mr. Mark Pierpoint; he asked to see the note of his copy, which I showed him. I told Mr. Pierpoint that I had been to London to prepare his payment, and I told him that I had seen the court-roll regarding his copyhold. I went to London to collect the £32 for Mr. Pierpoint and sent him word. This evening I brought the money, but he had already gone to bed. This morning I offered the money and counted it out at <term>Goodman</term> <note>A title for a man of respectable social standing, usually a farmer or householder below the rank of "Gentleman"</note> Welder’s house in front of Mr. Stockden and Goodman Welder, but Mr. Pierpoint refused to fulfill the agreement. <note>original Latin: "Deus bene vertat!"</note> May God turn it to good!

July 13th, I paid Robert 5 shillings toward his wages today.

July 14th, I gave 4 shillings to Letice, part of her <term>noble</term> <note>The noble was 6s 8d; this likely refers to a specific portion of her quarterly wage</note> for her quarterly wages which ended on the 9th day of this month.

July 18th, I bought Goodman Welder's hovel, which is located in the yard of the house next to mine—the one I bought from Mr. Mark Pierpoint. I gave him a new <term>angel</term> <note>A gold coin worth about 10 shillings, stamped with the image of the Archangel Michael</note> and five new shillings, and he is to receive 5 more shillings, totaling 20 shillings in all. If I cannot manage to take possession of the house, then he is to keep his hovel and return the money to me.

July 21st, I gave Richard 5 shillings toward his wages today.

July 22nd, I paid Mr. Childe £7, 13s, 4d for all his wood—twenty-six loads.

July 24th, the offer for the bargain was made again on Mr. Pierpoint's behalf; this is Mr. Stockden’s doing.

July 27th, let it be remembered that this Friday I paid Mr. Tomson £4 for his master, Mr. Herbert, which I had borrowed on December 12th, 1592. Mr. Herbert sent it back again for my further...

...promised on the 26th day to begin his work of fixing silver <term>silver</term> <note>original Latin: *lunam*. In the symbolic language of alchemy, the Moon (Luna) represents the metal silver. Dee is recording the passage of a painful kidney stone.</note>. God be thanked! Five shillings to Robert Webb, part of his wages.

**April 1st**, Captain Hendor made acquaintance with me, and showed me a part of his policy <note>In the Elizabethan era, "policy" often referred to strategic plans or intelligence regarding national security.</note> against the Spanish King’s intended mischief against Her Majesty and this realm. **April 4th**, John Stokden came to study with our children. Only the Lady Warwick and Sir Robert Cecil’s lady <note>Elizabeth Brooke, wife of the influential statesman Robert Cecil.</note> were in the garden with Her Majesty. **May 18th**, Her Majesty sent back to me the copy of the letter of G. K. <note>Likely Edward Kelley, Dee’s former scryer, who used the surname Kelly.</note> with thanks, delivered by the Lady Warwick. **May 21st**, Sir John Wolley moved my petition <term>sute</term> to Her Majesty. She granted it in a fashion, but referred everything to the Lord of Canterbury <note>John Whitgift, the Archbishop of Canterbury.</note>. **May 25th**, Dr. Aubrey moved my petition to Her Majesty, and the answer was as before. **May 29th**, with the Archbishop before the Queen came to her house. **June 3rd**, I, my wife, and seven children, [appeared] before the Queen at Isleworth <note>original: Thisellworth</note>. My wife kissed her hand. I presented my request for the Archbishop to come to my cottage. **June 6th**, supped with the Lord Archbishop. Invited him to my cottage. **June 11th**, given to Robert Webb at London seven shillings in the beginning of this month. **June 15th**, £40 from Mr. Thomas Harward. I should have £60 more. A great fit of the stone <note>A severe episode of kidney stone pain.</note> in my kidneys. **June 20th**, Mistress Magdalen Pierpoint was examined alone by our steward at the Temple <note>The Inner or Middle Temple in London, the center of legal activity.</note>. **June 22nd**, mortgaged my recent purchase <note>Likely a small plot of land or property.</note> to Mr. Richard White for £30, to be received within a few days. **June 23rd**, I discharged Robert Webb of my service, and gave him 40 shillings for a full satisfaction of all things. Thomas Sharpe. **June 24th**, on Midsummer Day, Antony Ryve Taylor came into my service, for <term>wages</term> <note>Dee meticulously recorded the hiring terms of his domestic staff.</note> of three pounds a year and a <term>livery</term> <note>A standard set of clothing or a suit of clothes provided by a master to a servant.</note>. Bartholomew Hickman arrived. **June 26th**, I dismissed Jane Hickman to go home to Northamptonshire with her father, Bartholomew, and I gave her ten shillings, and promised her another ten shillings at <term>Hallowtide</term> <note>The season of All Saints, occurring in early November.</note>. Bartholomew Hickman and Goodman Ball departed for home with Jane Hickman. **June 29th**, after I had heard the Archbishop's answers and discourses, and knowing that he had been at Theobalds <note>The grand country estate of Lord Burghley, the Lord Treasurer, where the Queen frequently stayed.</note> last Sunday with the Queen and the Lord Treasurer, I consider myself defeated in all my legal suits or hopes for anything that was. And so, farewell to the court and courting until God directs me otherwise! The Archbishop gave me a pair of <term>sovereigns</term> <note>original: "sufferings." This is Dee's phonetic spelling of the gold coins; the gift was a gesture of charity for Dee's "refreshment" or "drinking."</note> to drink. God be my help as He is my refuge! Amen.

**July 1st**, I gave Robert another <term>French crown</term> <note>A gold coin worth approximately six shillings.</note> as a farewell. **July 2nd**, given to Richard ten shillings toward his wages. **July 6th**, Michael <note>Dee’s young son, Michael, who was around nine years old.</note> became ill in his head and back. **July 9th**, in the morning my head began to ache and feel heavier than of late, and I had some <term>wambling</term> <note>A feeling of nausea, queasiness, or rumbling in the stomach.</note> in my stomach. I had broken my fast with <term>sugar sops</term> <note>Pieces of bread soaked in a warm, sweetened liquid like wine or milk; a common food for the sick or for breakfast.</note>, etc. I gave my servant Letice 5s. as part of her wages; with part of which she was to buy a <term>smock</term> <note>A linen undergarment.</note> and a <term>neckerchief</term>. **July 13th**, **at sunrise** <note>original: "ortu solis"</note> Michael Dee gave up the ghost after he said, "O Lord, have mercy upon me!" **July 19th**, Goodman Richardson began his work. **August 19th**, Elizabeth Felde came into my service; she is to have five <term>nobles</term> <note>A gold coin worth six shillings and eightpence.</note> a year and a smock. **August 26th**, Mr. Gerard, the <term>surgeon</term> and <term>herbalist</term> <note>Almost certainly John Gerard (1545–1612), the famous botanist and author of the most popular English gardening book of the era.</note>, [visited me]. **August 30th**, Monsieur Walter Mallet took his leave of me to go home to Toulouse. ->### 1594.] DR. DEE'S DIARY. 51<-

The day before <note>original: "Pridie"</note>, I writ and sent a letter to the Lady Scudamore <note>Mary Scudamore, a close lady-in-waiting to Queen Elizabeth I.</note>, in my wife's name, to move her Majesty that either I might declare my case to the body of the council, or else under the **great seal** <note>The primary seal used to authorize official state documents; here, Dee seeks a formal license to travel or work without restriction.</note> to have license to go freely anywhere. **Oct. 31st**, lightning without thunder in the afternoon and in the night following.

**Nov. 24th**, received a letter from Sir Edward Kelley <note>Dee’s former partner in alchemical and spiritual experiments, who remained in Europe after Dee returned to England.</note> by Rowley. **Dec. 2nd**, Francis Garland came to England from Prague. Just five years past I came to England from Bremen as Francis Garland came now: but the Stade fleet stayed at Harwich. The 2nd of our cold December, Bartholomew <note>Bartholomew Hickman, a long-term associate and later a scryer for Dee.</note> was preferred by me to the Lord Willoughby's service at Barbican, in the presence of the Countess of Kent: and the Lord Willoughby did presently write his warrant to Mr. Jonson in Fleet Street, tailor, to deliver to Bartholomew his cloth and cushions <note>Likely referring to the fabric for his official livery or servant's uniform.</note>, and so it was to Bartholomew delivered immediately. **Dec. 7th**, Jane my wife delivered her supplication <note>A formal written petition or prayer.</note> to the Queen's Majesty, as she passed out of the privy garden at Somerset House to go to dinner to the Savoy to Sir Thomas Heneage. The Lord Admiral took it of the Queen. Her Majesty took the bill again and kept [it] upon her cushion; and on the **8th day**, by the chief motion of the Lord Admiral, and somewhat of the Lord Buckhurst, the Queen's wish was to the Lord Archbishop presently that I should have Dr. Day's place in Paul's <note>St. Paul’s Cathedral in London. Dr. William Day was the Dean of St. Paul's.</note>. **Dec. 22nd**, paid seven shillings to Elizabeth Felde, part of her wages. Given to Lettyce 5s., part of her wages. Paid to Richard 8s., part of his wages; and all other reckonings paid.

**1595. Jan. 3rd**, the Wardenship of Manchester <note>The leadership of Christ’s College, Manchester; this was the post Dee eventually secured, though it proved to be a troubled appointment.</note> spoken of by the Lord Archbishop of Canterbury. **Feb. 5th**, my bill of Manchester offered to the Queen before dinner by Sir John Wolley to sign, but she deferred it. **Feb. 10th**, at two after noon I took a cutpurse <note>A pickpocket.</note> taking my purse out of my pocket in the Temple <note>The legal district of London.</note>. **Feb. 18th**, Mr. Laward his son Thomas born at noon or a little after, 1/4 or 1/2. *First consultation and deliberation with Marmion Haselwood at the end of this month.* <note>original: "Consultatio et deliberatio prima cum Marmione Haselwood in fine istius mensis."</note> **March 18th**, Mr. Francis Garland came this morning to visit me, and had much talk with me.

<meta>This page documents the pivotal moment in 1595 when John Dee finally secures the Wardenship of Manchester after years of petitioning the Queen. It tracks the subsequent administrative process, the hiring and firing of household staff, and the deaths of significant associates.</meta>

->### DR. DEE'S DIARY. [1595. 52<-

Concerning Sir E. K. <note>Sir Edward Kelley, Dee’s former assistant in alchemical and spiritual matters, who was at this time imprisoned in Bohemia.</note> March 20th, Mr. Marmion Haselwood, Mr. Dymock, and Mr. Hipwell, came to me to Mortlake. March 21st, Bartholomew Hickman <note>A long-time associate and later a medium for Dee’s spiritual experiments.</note> came to Mortlake. March 26th, Bartholomew homeward. March 29th, Mr. Laward and Mr. Alred came to me.

April 18th, my bill for the Manchester Wardenship <note>The position of Warden of Christ’s College in Manchester, a significant ecclesiastical and administrative post.</note> was signed by the Queen, Mr. Herbert <note>Sir John Herbert, a Master of Requests who handled petitions for Elizabeth I.</note> offering it to her. May 4th, paid Richard 20 shillings as part of his wages, and I gave him a further 10 shillings for full payment of all odd reckonings of late. May 5th, Mr. Cave died. May 8th, the Master of the Rolls’ <note>Sir Thomas Egerton, a powerful judge and legal official who showed Dee kindness.</note> courtesy, though I had never spoken unto him. May 9th, my cousin John Aubrey came to me, to recreate himself for a while. May 21st, I discharged Letice of my service, and paid all duties until this day, her year ending on the 8th of April. I gave her 2 shillings and 6 pence for a month extra, and for her travel expenses I gave her 2 shillings and 6 pence, Robert Charles and my wife being present in my study. May 25th, 26th, 27th, the Signet, the Privy Seal, and the Great Seal <note>The sequential steps of royal bureaucracy required to finalize his legal appointment to the Wardenship.</note> of the Wardenship; £3 12 shillings borrowed of my brother Arnold. June 1st, my young cousin, John Aubrey, was sent for by his father to London. Mr. Partrich, his brother, in London; Richard Ward, and others came for him. June 9th, Bartholomew Hickman went homeward. June 11th, I wrote to the Earl of Derby's secretary about Manchester. June 18th, Anne Powell came to my service; she is to have four nobles <note>A noble was a gold coin worth 6s 8d; her yearly wage was approximately £1 6s 8d.</note> by the year, a pair of hose and shoes. June 21st, the Earl of Derby's letter to Mr. Warren for the college. June 25th, Dr. Aubrey <note>Dr. William Aubrey, a distinguished lawyer and Dee's relative.</note> died at midnight. My cousin, Mr. George Broke, gave me £50 in gold, <term>at three o'clock in the afternoon</term> <note>original: "hora tertia a meridie"</note>. June 29th, Mr. John Blayney, of Over Kingsham in Radnorshire, and Mr. Richard Baldwin, of Duddlebury in Shropshire, visited me at Mortlake. The great-grandfather of the said John, and my great-grandmother by the father's side, were brother and sister.

July 1st, the two brothers, Master Willemots, of Oxfordshire, came to talk of hiring my house. Master Baynton came with Mistress Kathryn Hazelwood, wife to Mr. Fuller. July 7th, Mr. Morgan Jones, my cousin, came to me at Mistress Walls' twice.

She took it graciously; and was sorry that it was so far from hence, but that some better thing near at hand shall be found for me; and, if the opportunity of the time would serve, her Majesty would speak with me herself. I had a bill made by Mr. Wood, one of the clerks of the signet, for the <term>first fruits</term> <note>A tax paid to the Crown by a new holder of a church office, equal to the first year's income of the position.</note> given me by her Majesty. August 2nd, at Mr. Cosener’s table at Greenwich: I spoke that which greatly pleased Mr. Sergeant Oliver Lloyd; would have disputed again. September 2nd, the spider at ten of the clock at night suddenly on my desk, and suddenly gone; a most rare one in size and length of feet. I was in a great study at my desk. September 6th I gave Richard 2 shillings and 6 pence, part of his wages, when he went to his grandfather. September 13th, I dined with the Earl of Derby at Russell House, Mr. Timothy and Mr. John Statfeldt, Germans, being there. September 14th, to Elizabeth Feeld 2 shillings for the tailor. September 22nd, Elizabeth Feeld went from my service. I dined with the Earl of Derby. September 26th, £6 borrowed of my cousin William Hetherley for fourteen days to pay for Bartholomew Hickman. September 29th, Margery Stubble of Hounslow, our <term>dry nurse</term> <note>A nurse who cares for a child but does not breastfeed them.</note>, entered into [service]

->### 1595.] DR. DEE’S DIARY. [PAGE 54<-

The year of her service began on Michaelmas Day [September 29th], and she is to receive £3 in annual wages and a russet-colored cloth for a gown. Edward Edwards also began his year of service to me on Michaelmas Day; he is to receive 40 shillings for his year's wages and a livery <note>A specific suit of clothes provided to a servant, often in the master's colors.</note>.

<margin>high</margin>
October 7th: I was angry (at 5:00 a.m. <note>original: "hor. 5 a mer." — 5:00 ante meridiem</note>) with Edward my cook because of his disorderly behavior. October 8th: Mr. Richard Western lent me £10 for one year. October 9th: I dined with Sir Walter Raleigh <note>The famous explorer, poet, and courtier to Queen Elizabeth.</note> at Durham House. October 11th: Gave 2 shillings to Edward as part of his wages. Mr. Banks lent me £5 as a loan until after Christmas. Mr. Emery sent me £3 via my servant Richard Walkedine. October 14th: To Anne, 2 shillings toward wages. I paid Elizabeth Felde the remainder of her annual wages, plus an additional 2 shillings and 6 pence for the extra time she worked. October 19th: The old account between me and Edmond Hilton was settled. Against his claim for eleven pounds, I showed him my old notes indicating he had already received £6 and 15 shillings; after that, Sted received his 25 shillings, and Mr. Emery received the £3 lent to him; I showed him Sted’s letter and Mr. Emery's letter from last month. All these sums together make exactly eleven pounds. Paid to Nurse Stubble 5 shillings as part of her wages. October 20th: To Anne, 12 pence. Richard rode toward Oxford to retrieve my <term>Arabic book</term> <note>Dee’s library was one of the largest in England; he was deeply interested in the scientific and mathematical knowledge contained in Arabic manuscripts.</note>. October 25th: Sted petitioned me for help in a legal case against his father. November 8th: My belongings were sent ahead toward Manchester by Percival. November 19th: My <term>Arabic book</term> was restored to me by God's favor. November 21st: Goodwife Lidgatt paid 13 shillings and 4 pence for two quarters of rent ending at the next Feast of the Annunciation [March 25th]. Goodman Agar was present in my hall at Mortlake. November 25th: News arrived that Sir Edward Kelley was killed <note>Kelley was Dee's former assistant in "angelic conversations." He died while attempting to escape imprisonment by the Holy Roman Emperor in Prague.</note>. November 26th: Mr. Nicholas Bagwell of Manchester brought me a letter from my brother Arnold. Lent 12 shillings to Mr. Laurence Dutton. My wife and children all departed by water toward Coventry. December 10th: Mr. Lok’s <term>Arabic books</term> and a letter were delivered to me by Mr. Berran’s son. December 23rd: I paid John Norton, a stationer [bookseller/publisher], £10 in cash and entered into a <term>recognizance</term> <note>A formal legal obligation or bond recorded before a judge.</note> before Doctor Hone for the payment of the remainder: £10 yearly, paid at Christmas and Midsummer in installments of £5, until a further £53 is paid.

->### 1596.] DR. DEE’S DIARY. [PAGE 55]<-

14 shillings and 8 pence were paid. Received £30 from Mr. Paget as part of a payment of one hundred for my house at Manchester. December 26th: **a daughter was born to the Earl of Derby in London at about four o’clock in the morning** <note>original Latin: "nata filia Comitis Derby mane circa quartam horam Londini"</note>.

1596. February 15th: I arrived in Manchester **at five o'clock in the afternoon** <note>original Latin: "a meridie hora quinta"</note>. February 20th: I was installed in the Manchester <term>wardenship</term> <note>The office of Warden, the head of the collegiate church in Manchester (now Manchester Cathedral).</note> **between nine and eleven o'clock in the morning** <note>original Latin: "inter nonam et undecimam horam ante meridiem"</note>. March 14th: a public warning was given against Thomas Goodyer. March 21st: a public warning was given regarding a license granted to Thomas Goodyer.

April 2nd: Sir John Byron, knight, and Mr. John Byron, esquire, dined with me in the college. I raised the matter of 10 pence <note>The original text "Xyd" uses the Roman numeral X for ten.</note> per acre for hay ground from his tenants. He promised to help. April 6th: I went to visit Mr. Ashton of Lester <note>Likely referring to the Ashton family of Great Lever.</note> and Mr. Sherington. April 8th: Margaret Dee began to be weaned. May 7th: taking possession [of land] in Salford. May 11th: my brother Aubrey and Richard set out toward London. June 3rd: I gave Antony Cowley 20 shillings and dismissed him from service. June 4th: Antony Cowley left my house for good; I do not know where he went. June 14th: Mr. Henry Savile, the <term>antiquary</term> <note>A scholar of history and ancient artifacts; Savile was a famous polymath and provost of Eton College.</note>, came to visit me. June 15th: I wrote a letter via Mr. Henry Savile regarding the book located at Halifax to Christopher Saxton <note>England’s first great map-maker and surveyor.</note> at Denningley. I sent my letter to Sir Robert Cecil’s <note>The Queen’s Secretary of State and a powerful figure at court.</note> house by William Debdell. June 18th: the commission for the college was sent to London to be <term>engrossed</term> <note>To write a formal legal document in a large, clear hand on parchment for official use.</note> in the <term>Duchy office</term> <note>The administrative office of the Duchy of Lancaster, which held jurisdiction over Manchester.</note>. I sent it by Nicholas Baguely of Newton to Mr. Brogreton and to William Nicolson to follow up on it during this legal term. June 21st: Mr. Christopher Saxton came to see me. June 22nd: entered upon the Great Brereridings <note>A local field name or property.</note> in Salford. June 24th: Bartholomew arrived. June 25th: an order was settled by the sheriff between me and Ralph Holden. June 26th: the Earl of Derby, along with Lady Gerard, Sir [Richard] Molyneux and his lady (the daughter of Lady Gerard), Master Houghton, and others, arrived unexpectedly after three o'clock. I prepared a **scholar’s collation** <note>A light meal or refreshment, typically served at a university or to guests of an academic.</note> for them, and it was well received. I accompanied his honor and the ladies to Ardwick Green toward Lyme, to the house of Mr. Legh, twelve miles away. June 29th: windy and rainy.

July 5th: Mr. Savill and Mr. Saxton <note>Christopher Saxton (c. 1540–1610) was the famous cartographer who created the first county maps of England and Wales.</note> arrived. July 6th: I, Mr. Saxton, Arthur Rouland, John, and Richard went to Hough Hall. July 9th: I sent Roger Kay of Manchester into Wales with my letters. July 10th: The town of Manchester was surveyed and measured by Mr. Christopher Saxton. Given to Nurse Stubley 10 shillings, as part of her wages. July 10th to 14th: Occupied with petty legal disputes <term>low controversies</term> <note>Minor administrative or legal disagreements, common in Dee's management of the College's estates.</note>, such as with Holden of Salford and the tenants of Sir John Byron of Failsworth regarding the rights of the college; sending to . . . . . to the county, and sending for Mr. Tyldesley of Chester regarding town affairs <term>townsaylers</term> <note>Likely a local term for town-dwellers or officials involved in the legal dispute.</note>. July 12th: Given more money to the nurse when her son, John Stubley, left my service for London to be reconciled with his master <note>Likely a reference to an apprenticeship agreement.</note>. I gave him 5 shillings. The young man, Leon the hatter, went with him. July 14th: Mr. Saxton rode away. The court sessions day at Manchester. July 19th: Alice entered my service through the help of Mrs. Beeston. Thomas, my cook, left my service. July 21st: Isabel Bardman moved from the chamber to the kitchen. July 25th: Thunder in the morning; rain in the night. July 27th: The Earl of Derby traveled toward London; he dined at Mr. Curtis’s house. August 10th: Mr. Thomas Jones of Tregaron <note>A Welsh gentleman and associate of Dee.</note> came to me in Manchester and rode back toward Wales on the 13th to meet the cattle that were coming. August 13th: I rode toward York. The party from Halifax and Mr. Thomas Jones rode toward Wales. August 20th: I returned to Manchester from York. August 20th to 27th: Much unrest and dispute regarding the <term>tithe corn</term> <note>A tax, usually one-tenth of the harvest, paid by local farmers to support the church and the Warden.</note> of Hulme. August 30th: The grain tithe from Crumpsall was obtained by agreement, but was afterward questioned and partially refused; then it was utterly denied. September 1st: Mary Goodwin entered my service to govern and teach Madinia and Margaret, my young daughters. September 3rd, being Friday: I rode to Sir John Byron’s at Royton <note>Sir John Byron (died 1623) was a prominent landowner and an ancestor of the poet Lord Byron.</note> to talk with him about the dispute between the college and his tenants. He claimed <term>pretented</term> that we have included part of Failsworth Common within our land at Newton Heath, which I am certain cannot be proven. We agreed that James Traves (being his bailiff) and Francis Nutthall, acting as his servant, should meet with me to examine all the facts,

...and so duly to proceed. September 5th: seventeen head of cattle from my kinsfolk in Wales were brought by the courteous Griffith David, nephew to Mr. Thomas Griffith.

October 26th: Mr. Francis Nicols and Bartholomew came to Manchester. October 29th: they rode homeward. November 22nd: £4 6s. <note>4 pounds and 6 shillings.</note> given to my wife by Mr. Francis Woodcote. December 3rd: Mr. Palmer came to be curate.

**1597.** January 19th: I sent £4 to Bartholomew Hickman by Bradshaw the carrier. January 22nd: Oliver Carter’s threat to sue me with a legal summons <term>process</term> from London was this Saturday declared to the clerk in the church <note>Oliver Carter was a Fellow of the Manchester College who became a persistent thorn in Dee's side, often challenging his authority.</note>. February 5th: Richard Key of Weaverham, a curate, came to me on Mr. Heton’s recommendation, and I am to try him for three months for 50 shillings in wages. February 7th: John Morryce came to Manchester. February 11th: £5 borrowed from Mr. Matthew Heton. February 14th: this Monday, John Morryce went with my letters to Mr. John Gwyn and twelve more gentlemen <term>esquires</term> in Montgomeryshire. February 17th: delivered to Charles Legh the elder my silver tankard with the cover, all double-gilt, which was the Countess of Hertford’s gift to Frances <note>Dee’s daughter.</note> her goddaughter; it weighed 22 ounces "great weight." This was to be placed in pawn in his own name to Robert Welsham the goldsmith for £4 until within two days after next May Day. My daughter Katherine, John Crocker, and I myself (John Dee) were present at the delivery and weighing of it in my chamber; it was wrapped in a new cloth handkerchief <note>This poignant detail highlights Dee’s ongoing financial struggles, forcing him to pawn family heirlooms to meet daily expenses.</note>. February 25th: Mr. Heton borrowed *The Major Concordances of Robert Stephanus* <note>original: "Concordantiæ Majores Roberti Stephani"; a standard biblical reference work printed by the famous Estienne family.</note>. He also has my book *On the Lord's Supper* <note>original: "De Cœna"; by Christoph Pezel, a Lutheran theologian.</note> by Doctor Pezelius. March 7th: Mr. Heton lent me £5 more, and thereupon I gave him a signed promissory note <term>bill of my hand</term> for the full ten pounds, to be paid by next Michaelmas <note>The feast of St. Michael, September 29th, a standard day for settling debts.</note>. The other £5 was received on the 11th of February last. March 17th: Bartholomew Hickman came. March 19th: I lent Mr. Hopwood *Weyer on the Deceptions of Demons* <note>original: "Wierus de præstigiis Dæmonum"; a famous 16th-century treatise by Johann Weyer that provided naturalistic explanations for behaviors often attributed to witchcraft.</note>.

April 10th: a petition <term>supplication</term> was presented by the parishioners. April 11th, 12th: troublesome days regarding Mr. Palmer the curate. April 15th: I received my copy of *Weyer on the Deceptions of Demons* back from Mr. Hopwood, and lent him *The Scourge of Demons* <note>original: "Flagellum Dæmonum".</note> and *The Cudgeling of Demons* <note>original: "Fustio Dæmonum".</note> in octavo size <note>These two books by Girolamo Menghi were famous—and controversial—manuals for exorcism.</note>.

...for the period until Midsummer. April 21st, I sent Bartholomew Hickman 40 shillings. I sent many letters to London by Bradshaw <note>Bradshaw was a regular carrier who transported goods and mail between Manchester and London.</note>. I sent my great letter to the Bishop of Lincoln by Goodman Thurp of Salford, and one to Mr. Shallcross. April 22nd, in the afternoon, Sir Urien Legh, knight, and his brother, and Mr. Brown, and Mr. George Booth, the Sheriff of Cheshire, visited me. Mr. Booth said that he would yield that to me which he would not yield to the bishop nor any other. Mr. Wortley of Wortley also came the same day **at four o’clock in the afternoon** <note>original: "hora quarta a meridie"</note>. May 2nd, Mr. Hulme and Mr. Williamson came to me regarding the Lord Bishop of Lincoln's case for Hulme. May 4th, I—along with Sir Robert Barber, the curate, and Robert Talsley, the clerk of Manchester parish church, with various people of the town of different ages—went in <term>perambulation</term> <note>A traditional custom of walking the boundaries of a parish to confirm its borders and assert territorial rights.</note> to the boundaries of Manchester parish. We began at the Leeless Bench against Prestwich parish, and so had a view of the three corn stakes, and then down to Mr. Standysh's new enclosure on the Low, where we stayed and viewed the stake still standing in the bank of the ditch, being eleven measures of Mr. Standley’s stake (which he then held in his hand) from the corn, and two feet more; which I continued to measure afterward, and it contained 6 inches and three quarters in <term>Kentish feet</term> <note>A traditional local unit of measurement where a "foot" could vary; the Kentish foot was often considered longer than the standard 12 inches.</note>. The <term>survey geometricall</term> <note>A scientific, mathematical measurement of the land, as opposed to a simple visual inspection.</note> of the actual circuits of Manchester parish was ended on this, the sixth day of my work. May 11th, the way to Stopford <note>Now known as Stockport.</note> was surveyed by John Cholmeley and John Crocker. May 17th, 20 shillings of his wages were paid to Richard Walkeden. May 20th, Lady Booth introduced herself here. May 23rd, paid to Isabell Boordman 8 shillings and 8 pence to make up her full year's wages due at the **Annunciation of our Lady** last past <note>March 25th, a traditional "quarter day" for paying rents and wages.</note>. I allowed Mr. Williamson ten days more respite for his kinsman to bring in his evidence for the legal proceedings. Paid to the nurse 3 shillings to make up the full payment of her year's wages which ended at **Michaelmas** last <note>September 29th, another traditional quarter day.</note>. May 27th, open enmity with Palmer before Sir Edward Fitton. Sir Edward Fitton told Matthew Palmer to his face that he had known him to be a mutinous man and a <unclear>......</unclear> June 9th, Thomas Sankinson told me of John Basset's arrival in London. ->#### [PAGE 59]<-

...lawful assembly and riot <note>original: "rowte," a legal term for a gathering of people with the intent to perform an unlawful act</note> of William Cutcheth, Captain Bradley, John Taylor, and Ralph Taylor at Newton against my men, describing the disturbance at Newton. June 27th, news from Hull of 23 barrels of Danish rye <note>original: "Dansk rye"; during the 1590s, England suffered from poor harvests, and grain was often imported from the Baltic</note> sent to me from John Pontoys.

July 1st, I sent Roger Kay to Vanydles for cattle. July 4th, the carriers to Wakefield for the corn. July 5th, toward evening lightning and a little thunder. July 6th, thunder in the morning. July 7th, five horse-loads of Danish rye came home. July 19th, a strange pang in my back opening **at 6:15 in the morning** <note>original: "mane hora 6 1/4"</note>. In the church regarding Mr. Palmer's disorder against Mr. Lawrence. July 20th, the last of my Danish rye arrived, in all 21 horse-loads. August 6th, this night I had a vision and a display of many books in my dream, and among the rest was one great volume, thick and in large quarto, newly printed; on the first page of which, as a title in great letters, was printed "**God is Known in Judea**" <note>original: "Notus in Judæa Deus," the opening of Psalm 76</note>. Many other books I thought I saw newly printed, on very strange subjects. I lent Mr. Edward Hopwood of Hopwood my *Hammer of Witches* <note>original: "Malleus Maleficarum," a famous 15th-century treatise on the prosecution of witches</note> to use until next New Year's tide—a short, thick, old book with two clasps, printed in the year 1517. August 19th, the Earl and Countess of Derby came to Alport Lodge <note>The Earls of Derby were the most powerful landowners in the region and patrons of the Manchester College</note>. August 21st, the Earl and Countess of Derby had a banquet <note>original: "banket," a course of sweetmeats, fruit, and wine</note> at my lodging at the college **at 4:30 in the afternoon** <note>original: "hora 4 1/2"</note>. August 27th, John Addenstall arrived from Mr. Emery. I wrote back. September 3rd, Mr. Werall of Loversal <note>transcribed as "Lobester"</note>, within two miles of Doncaster, came to me to become acquainted with me. September 9th, very windy from the South and rainy. September 12th, hail this morning on Monday. September 15th, 40 shillings lent by Mr. Werall. John Cholmley went with him to provide him and others with medicine <note>original: "physick"</note>; and I paid John Cholmley back the 40 shillings. September 24th, Bartholomew arrived. September 25th, Mr. Oliver Carter’s impudent and evident dissoluteness in the church <note>Carter was a fellow of the college who frequently clashed with Dee over religious and administrative matters</note>. September 26th, he repented and some peace was made. September 27th, I granted a lease of three lives <note>A legal lease that remains in effect until the deaths of three specified individuals</note> to Mr. Radcliffe for two houses in Dean’s Square at 7 shillings rent for both; the fee <note>original: "fine"</note> was twenty nobles <note>A gold coin; twenty nobles was worth approximately £6 13s 4d</note>. September 28th, Mr. Yardley of Caldecott in Cheshire arrived, from about six miles outside of Chester, toward Holt. News regarding the philosopher Dr. Walder <note>original: "Nova de philosopho D. Waldero"</note>.

Sept. 30th, John Crocker (my good servant) had leave to go to see his parents. He went with Bartholomew Hickman and Robert Charles toward Branbroke <note>possibly Brampton or a similar local parish</note>, with Arthur Golding <note>the famous translator of Ovid, who was also known to practice medicine and surgery</note>, to be cured of his fistula <note>a painful, deep-seated infection or abnormal passage in the body, often very difficult to treat in this era</note>. John Crocker intends to return about Easter or at Whitsuntide <note>the seventh Sunday after Easter</note> next. God be his speed! Mr. Humphrey Davenport <note>a member of a prominent Cheshire legal family</note> was made our steward.

Oct. 12th, Ralph Holden preferred a bill <note>brought a formal legal complaint</note> against Richard Walkeson for entering Brereriding's chase <note>a "chase" was a private hunting ground, legally distinct from a forest</note>, which I and Antony Ryve . . . . . found to be false. The bill was not found <note>the grand jury did not find enough evidence to proceed with the charge</note>. Oct. 22nd, John Fletcher of Manchester went with my letters to Vanylos <note>likely a phonetic spelling of a Welsh location related to Dee's ancestral interests</note> this Sunday morning. Nov. 3rd, Mr. John Cholmeley departed toward London by way of Market Harborough. Nov. 7th, the fellows <note>the senior members of the College</note> and the receiver <note>the financial bursar</note> did not agree with me regarding the accounts. **A little after nine in the morning** <note>original: "Paulo post nonam mane"</note> Arthur's <note>Dee’s eldest son</note> left eye was hurt while playing at fencing with rapiers and daggers made of sticks, by a thrust <note>original: "foyne," a technical fencing term for a stabbing blow</note> from Edmond Arnold. Nov. 10th, Mr. Burch brought a letter from Matthew Palmer. Nov. 14th, the fellows would not grant me the £5 for my house-rent, as the Archbishops had granted; and our college foundation mandates a house be provided. Nov. 17th, I sent Edmond Arnold to London on foot with my letters to Dr. Julio <note>likely Dr. Giulio Cesare, a court physician</note>. Dec. 3rd, to Richard Walkeden 10s. in part of his wages. To the nurse 10s. Dec. 9th, I visited the grammar school. Dec. 13th, I wrote by the carrier Barret to Dr. Caesar <note>Sir Julius Caesar, a high-ranking judge and future Chancellor of the Exchequer</note>. Dec. 14th, Mr. George Brooke, son to Mr. . . . . Brooke of . . . . , came to make my acquaintance, whom I treated most friendly. Mr. Ratcliffe of Manchester came with him, but Mr. Heton also came on Tuesday afternoon when I had no leisure. Dec. 17th, I lent to Mr. Barlow for his son a Spanish grammar in octavo <note>a book size</note> printed at Louvain in the year 1555 by Bartholomæus Gravay <note>original: "Bartholomæus Gravay," a known printer of polyglot texts</note> in Spanish, French, and Latin. To R. Dickinson I paid £7 2s. for the plate and a new bell made until 1599, January 1st, £66.

1598. Jan. 4th, I wrote to Bartholomew and Charles by Bradshaw. Jan. 17th, my brother Arnold to Chester and Vaunlos. Jan. 18th, Edmond Arnold with my letter to London. Jan. 19th, **at two o'clock in the afternoon** <note>original: "hora secunda a meridie"</note> I came before the justices against James . . .

<summary>This page captures Dr. Dee's domestic and professional life in Manchester, including his son Arthur's fencing injury, ongoing financial disputes with the College fellows over house rent, and his continued exchange of scholarly books and letters with London officials.</summary>
