<meta>This page follows Dr. John Dee’s diary entries for late 1597. Having faced ongoing disputes with the fellows of Christ's College and managing his household in Manchester, Dee now enters the early months of 1598, recording legal settlements, physical repairs to the college, and personal moments of distress.</meta>

->### 1598.] DR. DEE’S DIARY.<-

[Legal action against] Shallcross and John Lawrence for misusing my name to deceive Mr. Harrughby. Jan. 20th, Walter Fletcher, surgeon <note>original: "chirurgien"</note>, arrived from Bartholomew Hickman. Jan. 22nd, after midnight the college gate toward Hunt’s Hall fell down, as did some part of the wall going down the lane. I received letters from Mr. John Pontoys. Jan. 24th, Walter Fletcher went with my letters to Bartholomew Hickman and Robert Charles. **My bitterness around midnight.** <note>original: "Amaritudo mea circa mediam noctem." Dee frequently used this phrase to record episodes of intense personal grief, psychological distress, or domestic conflict.</note> Jan. 28th, the field <term>original: "cloose," an enclosed area of land</term> was hired from Ed. Brydock for three pounds paid beforehand by me, John Dee, to the said Ed. Brydock; the total being £4 from the next Candlemas <note>February 2nd, a Christian feast day often used as a date for legal and financial deadlines</note> until the following Candlemas a year later. 

Feb. 9th, George Birch’s lawsuit was stayed at Chester upon his promise to settle <note>original: "compownd," to reach a financial agreement to end a dispute</note> with me for all tithes, hay, and other matters. I also stayed the lawsuit and excommunication of Thomas Goodyer, **saving his right in the meantime**. <note>original: "salvo interim jure suo." A legal phrase meaning the suspension of the case did not forfeit Goodyer's eventual legal claims.</note> I likewise stayed Baxter’s case at the Chester court. Feb. 12th, news from Mr. Smyth regarding the Upton parsonage arrived this Sunday. Feb. 13th, Edmond Arnold [went] to London; thereupon I sent [messages] speedily. Feb. 20th, I wrote by Oliver Ellet, the tailor, to Mr. Nicolls at Faxton. Feb. 22nd, Mr. Nicolls came and wished to meet Ellet. 

Feb. 25th, the eclipse. A cloudy day, but great darkness about 9:30 in the morning <note>original: "mane." This was a total solar eclipse, known as "Black Saturday" in Scotland, though only partial in Manchester.</note>. Feb. 26th, **around midnight, my bitterness.** Feb. 27th, Mr. Nicolls rode homeward and met the messenger a little beyond Stopford <note>modern-day Stockport</note>. I sent Roger Edward’s book home with Mr. Nicolls, to be brought back to me by Bartholomew Hickman. March 1st, I received Mr. Thynne’s letter regarding Sted’s debt, and Ed. Arnold’s letter about the lawsuit from Upton, and concerning the Lord Archbishop’s hard dealing. March 2nd, I sent the statute staple <term>a bond of record acknowledging a debt, which allowed the creditor to seize the debtor’s lands or goods if unpaid</term> to London to Mr. George Brook for Sted. I wrote letters by John Hardy and sent them in a box. March 5th, news of Mistress Mary Nevell’s death [arrived] via William Nicholson, stating she died the Friday after Candlemas Day. March 11th, borrowed 40 shillings from Mr. George Kenyon of Kersall, to repay again as soon as I can conveniently. Received by Richard Walkeden.

<summary>This page covers the early months of 1598, detailing John Dee's management of legal suits in Chester, his land rentals, and his observation of a significant solar eclipse. It also highlights his ongoing personal distress and the logistical efforts of sending letters and legal documents to London.</summary>

--- Page 75 ---
<meta>This page documents Dr. John Dee's return to Manchester in the summer of 1600 after a period in London. It highlights a brief moment of peace with the difficult Fellows of the college—the senior clergy and administrators—and records his continued interest in both administrative duties and esoteric studies.</meta>

->### DR. DEE'S DIARY.<-

<meta>[1600.]</meta>

**1600.** June 10th, set out from London. June 18th, I, my wife, Arthur Rowland, Mistress Mary Nicols, and Mr. Richard Arnold came to Manchester.

July 3rd, the Commission <note>A formal administrative or legal meeting regarding college business.</note> sat in the Chapter House. July 7th, this morning, as I lay in my bed, it came into my imagination to write a book, **"On certain differences between bodies and spirits."** <note>original: "De differentiis quibusdam corporum et spirituum." This reflects Dee’s lifelong interest in the intersection of the physical and supernatural realms.</note> July 8th, I wrote to the Lord Bishop of Chester by Mr. Withenstalls. July 10th, Mr. Nicols and Bartholomew Hickman came. July 14th, Francis Nicols and Bartholomew Hickman went homeward. July 17th, I requested the fellows <note>The senior members of Christ’s College, Manchester, with whom Dee frequently quarreled over management and finances.</note> to come to me by nine the next day. July 18th, it is to be noted of the great reconciliations <note>original: "pacifications"</note> unexpected by man which happened this Friday; for in the forenoon (between nine and ten) where the fellows were greatly in fear of my heavy displeasure, by reason of their manifold misbehavior against me, I did with all kindness <term>original: "lenity," meaning mercy or mildness of temper</term> entertain them, and showed the most part of the things that I had brought to pass at London for the college’s good, and told Mr. Carter (as he was going away) that I must speak with him alone. Robert Leigh and Charles Legh were present. 

Secondly, the great lawsuit between Redishmer <note>Likely a reference to a member of the local Reddish family.</note> and me was stayed through the wisdom of Mr. Richard Holland. Thirdly, the use of the organs was admitted upon certain conditions. And fourthly, Mr. Williamson's resignation was granted so that a preacher might be obtained from Cambridge. July 19th, I lent Randall Kemp my second part of **Holinshed's Great Chronicle** <note>A famous and massive history of the British Isles, famously used by Shakespeare as a source for his plays.</note> for two or three weeks. He restored it to Newton. July 31st, we held our audit—I and the fellows—for the two years last past during my absence: Oliver Carter, Thomas Williamson, and Robert Birch; Charles Legh the elder being the receiver <note>The official responsible for collecting rents and revenues.</note>. I read and gave unto Mistress Mary Nicolls her prayer.

August 5th, I visited the grammar school, and found great imperfection in all and every one of the scholars, to my great grief. August 6th, I had a dream after midnight of my working of the **philosopher's stone** <note>The legendary alchemical substance capable of turning base metals into gold and achieving immortality.</note> with others. My dream was after midnight toward...

<summary>Dr. Dee returns to Manchester and experiences a rare period of administrative harmony, settling disputes with the College Fellows and envisioning new scholarly works. The entries also reflect his disappointment with the local school's standards and his enduring alchemical aspirations.</summary>

<keywords>Manchester, Christ's College, Chapter House, Bishop of Chester, Holinshed's Chronicle, philosopher's stone, grammar school, Oliver Carter, Bartholomew Hickman</keywords>

->### 1600.] DR. DEE'S DIARY.<-

[...continued from the previous] day. Aug. 10th, we received the Eucharist <note>original: "Eucharistam suscepimus." This refers to the holy sacrament of communion, showing Dee’s continued adherence to the Church of England’s rites despite accusations of magic.</note>, I, my wife, my daughter Katherine, and Maria Nicolls. Aug. 30th, a great tempest of mighty wind from the South West from 2 until 6, with rain.

Sept. 11th, Mr. Holland of Denby, Mr. Gerard of Stopford, and Mr. Langley—commissioners from the Bishop of Chester and authorized by him—did call me before them in the church about three o'clock in the afternoon, and did deliver to me certain petitions put up by the fellows <note>The senior clergy and administrators of the college who frequently disagreed with Dee's management.</note> against me, to be answered before the 18th of this month. I answered them all at that same time <note>original: "eodem tempore"</note>, and yet they gave me leave to write [my responses] at leisure.

Sept. 16th, Mr. Harmer and Mr. Davis, gentlemen of Flintshire (within four or five miles of Hawarden Castle), did visit me. Sept. 29th, I burned in the presence of Mr. Nicolls, his brother, and Mr. Wortley, all of Bartholomew Hickman’s untrue actions. <note>original: "actions," a technical term Dee used for his spiritual sessions or attempts to communicate with angels. In a separate note, Dee adds that all of Bartholomew's reports of spiritual sight and hearing were burned, including a copy of the first part which was found later and burned before him and his wife.</note> Sept. 30th, after the departing of Mr. Francis Nicolls, his daughter Mistress Mary, his brother Mr. William, and Mr. Wortley—whom I accompanied on foot as far as the end of Deansgate—at my return, Mr. Roger Cooke offered and promised his faithful and diligent care and help, to the best of his skill and power, in chemical processes <note>This refers to alchemy, the precursor to modern chemistry, which Dee studied in his laboratory to discover the secrets of nature and medicine.</note>. He promised that he would rather do so than be with anyone else in England; which promise may the Lord bless and confirm! He told me that Mr. Anthony had considered him very liberally and friendly, but he told him that he had already promised me. Then he [Anthony] respected the fidelity of his regarding such a promise.

Oct. 13th, be it remembered that Sir George Booth came to Manchester to visit Mr. Humphrey Davenport, a counselor of Gray's Inn <note>One of the professional associations for barristers in London.</note>, and so came to the college to me; and after a few words of discourse, we agreed concerning two or three tenements in Dunham Massey <note>original: "Durham Massy"</note> in his occupying. We agreed that he and I, along with the fellows, would stand to the arbitration of the said Mr. Davenport after his next return here from London. John Radcliffe, Mr. Davenport's [assistant...]

<summary>This page records the intensifying legal disputes between Dr. Dee and the Fellows of Manchester College, alongside Dee's decision to burn his records of spiritual "actions" with Bartholomew Hickman. It also notes Dee's return to alchemical research with the help of Roger Cooke.</summary>

...man, was with him here, and Mr. Dumbell, but they heard not our agreement; we were in my dining-room. 1601. Feb. 2nd, Roger Cook’s supposed plot laying to my discredit was by Arthur my son found by chance in a box of his papers in his own handwriting **around noon** <note>original: "circa meridiem"</note>, and in the afternoon about 1:30 brought to my knowledge face to face. 

->**O God, deliver us from evil!** <note>original: "O Deus, libera nos a malo!"</note><-

All was mistaken, and we reconciled godly. Feb. 10th to 15th, reconciliation between us, and I did declare to my wife, Katherine my daughter, Arthur and Rowland, how things were thus taken. Feb. 18th, Jane came to my service from Clitheroe. Feb. 25th, R. Coke [made] **a sacred pact at eight o'clock in the morning** <note>original: "pactum sacrum hora octava mane"</note>. March 2nd, Mr. Roger Coke went toward London. March 19th, I received the long letters from Bartholomew Hickman **at two o'clock in the afternoon** <note>original: "hora secunda a meridie"</note> by a carrier of Oldham. April 6th, Mr. Holcroft of Vale Royal his first acquaintance at Manchester by reason of William Herbert his friend. He used me and reported of me very freely and worshipfully <note>Meaning he treated Dee with great respect and high regard.</note>.

<meta>This page transitions from the personal diary entries of 1601 to a formal inventory of Dr. John Dee’s library at Mortlake, dated 1583. This catalogue is essential for understanding the breadth of Dee's intellectual world before his departure for the Continent and the subsequent looting of his home.</meta>

# CATALOGUE

## OF THE BOOKS IN THE EXTERNAL LIBRARY

### AT MORTLAKE

->Of Master John Dee, September 6th, 1583.<-

---

## MANUSCRIPT BOOKS.

[*From **Manuscript Trinity College Cambridge O. iv. 20**, transcribed by Elias Ashmole in **Manuscript Ashmole 1142**. Another autograph copy is preserved in **Manuscript Harleian 1879**, which differs very little from the one in the library of Trinity College. The numbers placed before the various volumes are added for the purpose of reference by the Editor.*]

<margin>Quarto <term>parchment</term> <note>A book size where the paper is folded twice, creating four leaves; written on animal skin.</note></margin>
1. Three treatises on the spheres by Menelaus <note>original: "Milei sphæricorum tractatus tres". Menelaus of Alexandria was a Greek mathematician and astronomer.</note>.

<margin>Quarto, written on parchment.</margin>
2. Theories of the planets.—Jordanus <note>Jordanus de Nemore, a 13th-century mathematician.</note> on triangles, including the squaring of the circle.—By the same, on perspective.—By the same, on mirrors, twilights, weights, and burning mirrors, in two books.

<margin>Quarto, on parchment.</margin>
3. An anonymous compendium on the lives of the philosophers.—Urso <note>Urso of Salerno, a famous physician of the Salernitan School.</note> on the mixing of the elements.—Aphorisms by the same [Urso].

<margin>Quarto, on parchment.</margin>
4. Avicenna <note>The Latinized name of Ibn Sina, a Persian polymath.</note> on the soul of the world, with others, namely: A book whose beginning is, “The Inspector of the preceding book of Aristotle.”—An exposition by Roger Bacon <note>The 13th-century English philosopher and scientist whom Dee greatly admired.</note>.—A book on weights.—Morienus to King Khalid <note>An alchemical dialogue between the hermit Morienus and the Umayyad prince Khalid ibn Yazid.</note>—Five books of Rhazes <note>The Latinized name of the Persian physician Al-Razi.</note> on the ten [subjects], etc.—Of Hermes <note>Referring to Hermes Trismegistus, the legendary founder of the Hermetic tradition.</note>.

<page-num>66</page-num>

# CATALOGUE OF DR. DEE'S

...seven books.—Rosinus to Euthesia.—Sayings of the wise.—The Assembly of Philosophers <note>original: "Turba philosophorum," one of the oldest and most influential Latin alchemical texts.</note>.—The book of the distinctions of the wise.—The letter of Alexander, King of the Persians.—Aristotle on the 30 words.—The book of Socrates.—The book of Effrey Effinensis.—The book of Khalid <note>Calid/Khalid ibn Yazid, an early figure in alchemy.</note>.—A book with commentary.—The work of the philosophers.—Geber on the perfect mastery <note>Geber, or Jabir ibn Hayyan, was the most famous name in medieval alchemy.</note>. 4° <term>parchment</term> <note>Quarto size, written on animal skin.</note>

5. John Duns Scotus’s questions on Porphyry’s *Isagoge* <note>original: "quinque voces" or "five voices." This refers to the five predicables in logic: genus, species, difference, property, and accident.</note>.—An exposition by a certain Anthony on the six categories, etc.—Roger Bacon on the multiplication of species.—Optics <note>original: "perspectiva"</note> by the same. 4° parchment.

6. A volume of the disputed questions of Thomas Aquinas. 4° parchment.

7. The Spark-chamber of the poets <note>original: "Scintillarium poetarum," a mythological handbook.</note>.—A summary of chiromancy <note>Palmistry.</note>.—An exposition of Ovid’s *Metamorphoses*.—A treatise on poison.—Valerius to Rufinus on not taking a wife, with an exposition.—A determination by John Wycliffe <note>The English reformer.</note>.—Letters of Brother William Flete.—Fulgentius’s mythologies with an exposition.—A treatise on the difficult words of the Bible.—Robert of Lincoln <note>Robert Grosseteste, the Bishop of Lincoln.</note> on the moral eye.—Robert of Lincoln on the nature of poison.—The short discourse of the philosophers by John Waleys, transcribed by Stocton at Cambridge, 1375.—Abstract cases in law, by Brother Hermann of the province of Saxony.—Cases reserved for the bishop.—An exposition of the Angelic Salutation <note>The "Hail Mary."</note>.\* 4° parchment.

8. On the weights and measures of medicinal operations.—Seven books of the Traveler’s Companion <note>original: "Viaticus"</note> by Constantine the African.—Experiments on the method of healing.—On the origin of diseases, and their diagnosis through urine.—On electuaries <note>Pastes or syrups containing medicine.</note>, etc. 4° parchment.

9. The Cosmography of Aethicus Ister, from the Latin version of Saint Jerome.† 4° parchment.

->One I had with me, and one I left here, which is noted after.<-

---

<margin>\* Now in Trinity College, Dublin. Bernard 148, (H. 12.)</margin>
<margin>† Now Cotton Manuscript Vespasian B. x. thus inscribed by Dr. Dee’s hand, "John Dee, 1565, February 21, at Worcester, as a gift from the dean of the church, Master Beddar."</margin>

<page-num>67</page-num>

# LIBRARY OF MANUSCRIPTS.

10. *Anticlaudianus* <note>original: "Anticlaudianus," a famous 12th-century allegorical poem by Alan of Lille about the creation of a perfect soul.</note>, in verse.—*Brutus* <note>A poem about the legendary founder of Britain.</note>, in verse, by Hugh de Puiset, Bishop of Durham.* Longish format, <term>parchment</term> <note>Writing material made from processed animal skin.</note>.
11. *A compendious treatise on animals.* <term>Quarto</term> <note>A book size where the sheet is folded twice to create four leaves.</note>, parchment.
12. *A fragment of William of Auvergne’s "On the Universe."* <note>original: "Wilhelmi Parisiensis... de universis." William was a 13th-century Bishop of Paris and philosopher.</note> Quarto, parchment.
13. *Euclid’s Elements of Geometry, Optics, and Catoptrics* <note>The study of mirrors and reflection.</note>, translated from the Arabic by Adelard <note>Adelard of Bath, a 12th-century scholar who disguised himself as a student in Spain to acquire Arabic scientific knowledge.</note>.—*Theodosius’s books on Spherics*.—*A book on hidden things*.—*Ptolemy’s planisphere*.—*Jordanus’s planisphere*.—*Archimedes’ treatise on the quadrature of the circle*.—*Gerard of Brussels’ book on motion*.—*Jordanus on weights*.—*Four books of practical geometry*.—*Al-Farabi on the sciences*.—*The philosophy of William of Conches*.—*Rhazes’ book on physiognomy* <note>original: "Rasis liber de phisiognomia." Rhazes was a prolific Persian physician.</note>.—*Human anatomy*.—*On the properties of the elements*.—with others. Quarto, parchment.
14. *Augustine on the soul and spirit*.—*Theorems demonstrated regarding the spirit and soul*.—*Johannes de Sacrobosco’s Algorithm demonstrated*.—*John de la Rochelle’s summary on the soul*.—*Robert of Lincoln’s treatise on the sphere* <note>Robert Grosseteste, the Bishop of Lincoln, a pioneering English scientist.</note>.—*Johannes de Sacrobosco’s treatise on the sphere*.—*A treatise on proportion and proportionality*, etc., by Roger Bacon—with others. Parchment, quarto.
15. *Several fragments of Maximus the Monk, Dionysius the Areopagite, Sophronius the Solitary, and other Greeks.* <note>These are early Christian mystical and theological writers.</note> Parchment, quarto.
16. *Ramon Llull’s book on the fifth essence.* <note>original: "quinta essentia," the celestial element sought by alchemists.</note> Paper, <term>folio</term> <note>A large book size made by folding a sheet of paper once.</note>.
->This is not by Ramon Llull, but various collections from Paracelsus.<- <note>This is a later correction, likely by Dee, identifying the text as belonging to the Swiss physician Paracelsus rather than the legendary Llull.</note>
17. *Roger Bacon on the soul and its works*.—*His book on the intellect and the intelligible.* Folio, parchment.
18. *An Apology concerning the deceits and perversities of false theologians and the religious*.—*The prophecy of Abbot Joachim against the religious orders holding the mendicant rule* <note>Joachim of Fiore was a famous 12th-century mystic whose prophecies about a "New Age" were highly influential and controversial.</note>.—*Arnaldus de Villa Nova’s work on the types of abuses of truth, on recognizing the false ministers of the Antichrist, and on the pastoral duty to be exercised regarding the flock*.—*His Catholic prophecy, handing down...*

\* This manuscript is now in the Cottonian library, Vespasian A. x. "John Dee, 1574, May 7, bought from a stall in London."

<summary>This page lists a variety of medieval manuscripts from John Dee's library, including a major scientific compendium containing works by Euclid and Archimedes, alongside mystical and prophetic works by Roger Bacon and Joachim of Fiore.</summary>

18. The art of annihilating the deceptions of the Antichrist and all his members, addressed to the sacred college of Rome. <term>parchment</term> <note>processed animal skin used for writing</note>, <term>folio</term> <note>a large book size made by folding a sheet of paper once</note>.

19. Roger Bacon on the slowing of old age and senility, etc.—The same author on the graduation of compound medicines, etc. parchment, folio.

21. The same author’s <margin>I doubt it</margin> <note>original: "dubito." Dee added this in the margin because he was unsure if this specific fragment was truly written by Roger Bacon.</note> fragment on animals. parchment, folio.

22. Certain fragments by the same Bacon; namely, on the multiplication and corruption of species <note>original: "multiplicatione et corruptione specierum." Robert Holcot was a famous 14th-century English Dominican theologian.</note>.—Roger Bacon's Mirror of Alchemy.—Questions on Jordanus’s book on weights.—Compendium of the art, by Ramon Llull <note>original: "Raymundi Lullii," a Majorcan philosopher and mystic known for his "Art" of logical combinations.</note>.—Excerpts from the theory of Ramon Llull.—Roger Bacon's treatise on species. paper, folio.

Rhazes (865–925 AD) was a Persian polymath and one of the most influential physicians in history.</note>, with twenty-five other small books by various authors on similar subjects. **paper**, **quarto**.

Ptolemy's fundamental work on the philosophy and practice of astrology.</note>, in Latin. — <term>Albumasar’s</term> *Introduction* <note>original: "Albumazer introductorium." Abu Ma'shar was a leading Persian astrologer in the Abbasid court.</note>. — The calendar of <term>Isibrad</term>. — The almanac of <term>Profacius the Jew</term> <note>original: "Profacii Judæi almanach." ...music. — <term>Cleomedes</term> on the world. — An exposition of the <term>astrolabe</term> <note>An ancient astronomical instrument used for solving problems relating to time and the position of the stars.</note>. — <term>Hipparchus</term> on <term>Aratus</term> and <term>Eudoxus</term>. — **All in Greek**. **paper**, **folio**.

44. A very ancient small book on burning mirrors, the beginning of which is, "From the more sublime," etc.<note>original: "De sublimiori." Now in the British Library, MS Cotton Vespasian A. II. This manuscript contains an early study of how curved mirrors can focus sunlight to create heat.</note> **parchment**, **quarto**. <margin>Now in MS Vespasian A. II. article 12, inscribed "Johannes Dee, 1555." This is an extremely curious and valuable tract in the history of optical science, and is similar to the curious treatise by Gogava *On the Burning Mirror*. The focus of the <term>parabola</term> is here for the first time indicated, a circumstance which has escaped the notice of scientific historians.</margin>

45. <term>Jordanus</term> on weights with commentaries <note>original: "Jordanus de ponderibus cum scholiis." Jordanus de Nemore was a 13th-century mathematician whose work on statics remained a standard for centuries.</note>, the beginning of which is "Every heavy thing." <note>original: "Omnis ponderosi."</note> **parchment**, **folio**.

46. <term>Raymond Lully’s</term> book on the <term>fifth essence</term> <note>original: "Raymundi Lullii liber de quinta essentia." In alchemy, the "fifth essence" (quintessence) was a purified substance thought to be the core of all matter and a potential universal medicine.</note>. **parchment**, **folio**.

47. <term>Boethius</term> on the *Consolation of Philosophy*, translated into the **Greek language** by <term>Maximus Planudes</term> <note>Planudes (c. 1260–1305) was a Byzantine monk and scholar who helped reintroduce Latin literature to the Greek-speaking world.</note>. — The *Distichs of Cato*, with the commentaries of Planudes, etc. **Greek**. — The <term>progymnasmata</term> <note>preliminary rhetorical exercises</note> of <term>Aphthonius</term>, **Greek**. **paper**, **folio**.

->I gave this book to the Kraków Library, July 28, 1584.<-

48. The philosopher <term>Porphyry’s</term> *Introduction* to Aristotle’s logic <note>original: "Porphirii philosophi Isagoge." A foundational textbook on logic used throughout the Middle Ages and Renaissance.</note>, **Greek**. **paper**, **folio**.

49. *Shipbuilding* by a certain Italian, with illustrations. **paper**, **quarto**.

50. <term>Denis Zachaire’s</term> short work on the <term>philosopher's stone</term>, **French**. **paper**, **quarto**.

51. The chronicle of <term>Robert of Gloucester</term>, in **English** verse.<note>original: "rythmo Anglico." A 13th-century metrical chronicle of English history.</note> <margin>The only manuscript of Robert of Gloucester's poem answering to this description is in the University Library, Cambridge.</margin> **paper**, **folio**.

52. History of the British princes from King <term>Cadwaladr</term> to <term>Llywelyn</term>, collected by <term>Humphrey Lhuyd</term>, **English**.<note>Lhuyd (1527–1568) was a Welsh cartographer and antiquary; Dee, who was proud of his Welsh heritage, took a keen interest in these historical records.</note> <margin>This manuscript is now in the library of the Ashmolean Museum, No. 846.</margin> **paper**, **folio**.

53. Various recipes for mercurial waters and other chemical experiments, **English**, the beginning of which is, "He that will make," etc. **paper**, **quarto**.

54. Various chemical experiments, **English**, the beginning of which is, "For to make white lead." **parchment**, **folio**.

55. <term>Albertus Magnus’s</term> compendium of natural science <note>original: "Alberti Magni summa naturalium." Albertus Magnus was a 13th-century Dominican friar who sought to reconcile Aristotelian philosophy with Christian faith.</note>, the beginning of which is, "Philosophy is divided." <note>original: "Philosophia dividitur."</note> **paper**, **quarto**.

<page-num>74</page-num>
<header>CATALOGUE OF DR. DEE'S</header>

[The Triangular Book].—The Book of Questions on Figure 5. <note>original: "Quæstionarius figuræ 5." This refers to a component of Raymond Lull’s "Great Art," a system of logical combinations.</note>—Third distinction according to another copy.—Aphorisms.—Shortening. <note>original: "Accurtatio." In alchemy, this refers to a "short cut" or more efficient method for creating the Philosopher's Stone.</note>—Practice of the hidden secret.—The Great Work, or the Royal Work.—Considerations on the Lesser Work.—A Song *in Catalan*, with a commentary.—The Short Art, etc. **on paper, folio.**

**67.** Raymond Lull’s *Mirror of Alchemy*.—The same author’s book on the *Quinta Essentia* <note>The "Fifth Essence" or quintessence, the purest form of matter sought by alchemists.</note>.—The same author’s lapidary, namely on gems.—John Dastin’s book on the composition of the [Philosopher's] stone. <note>John Dastin (c. 1293–1386) was a prominent English alchemist who corresponded with Pope John XXII.</note>—The same author's *The Gift of God*.—The Book of Roots.—The Book of Administrations.—The same Dastin’s *Mirror of Philosophers*.—Rhazes on the twelve waters, etc. <note>original: "Rasis de duodecim aquis." Rhazes was a 9th-century Persian physician and alchemist; this work describes various chemical solutions.</note> **on paper, folio.**

**68.** The medical practice of Anetus, son of Abraham.—Scarsatus’s medicinal practice, along with others. <note>Anetus and Scarsatus refer to medieval medical authorities whose specific identities are now obscure, likely of Arabic or Jewish origin.</note> **on parchment, quarto.**

**69.** Aelred, Abbot of Rievaulx, on the life of Edward, King of the English and Confessor.* <note>Aelred (1110–1167) was a Cistercian monk and historian. This manuscript is an important record of the life of Saint Edward the Confessor.</note> **on parchment, quarto.**

**70.** A treatise by Robert, Bishop of Lincoln, in the Roman tongue, that is, in *old French verse*, on the beginning of the creation of the world, on the middle and the end, etc. <note>original: "Roberti episcopi Lincolniensis tractatus in lingua Romana... veteri rithmo Gallico." This refers to Robert Grosseteste (c. 1175–1253) and his poem *Le Château d'Amour*, written in Anglo-Norman French.</note> **on parchment, quarto.**

**71.** William of Northfield’s exposition on the book *On the Difference between the Spirit and the Soul*. <note>William of Northfield was a 13th-century English philosopher. The book he comments on was originally by Costa ben Luca.</note>—The same author's exposition on various small works of Aristotle's *Physics*.† **on parchment, folio.**

**72.** Master Franco’s musical rules, with additions by other musicians, collected by Robert de Handlo.—Roger Bacon’s *Perspective*, along with other geometric and astrological works. <note>Franco of Cologne was a 13th-century music theorist who revolutionized musical notation. Robert de Handlo was a 14th-century commentator. Bacon's *Perspective* is a seminal work on optics.</note> **on parchment, folio.**

**73.** Walter Burley’s notable notes on Porphyry’s *Isagoge* and Aristotle’s *Categories*, along with small books of other notations. <note>original: "super Porphyrii prædicabilia, et Aristotelis prædicamenta." Walter Burley (c. 1275–1344) was a leading English scholastic philosopher.</note> **on paper, quarto.**

**74.** Boethius’s *Music*.—Hermann the Lame on the construction of the astrolabe, and on its uses. <note>Boethius (c. 480–524) was the primary authority on music theory in the Middle Ages. Hermannus Contractus (1013–1054) was a polymath famous for his work on astronomical instruments.</note> **on parchment, quarto.**

**75.** A Chronicle of Emperors, or a compendium of histories from the first monarchy of the Babylonians, to the year of Christ 1266.—

* <margin>Now MS. Harleian 200, "John Dee, 1575."</margin>
† <margin>Now Corpus Christi College, Oxford, No. 235.</margin>

<meta>This page continues the catalog of John Dee’s library, moving from the works of Raymond Lull into a diverse collection of history, theology, and the "quadrivium" sciences—astronomy, geometry, and optics. The entries reflect Dee’s possession of both traditional scholastic texts (like Lombard’s Sentences) and the cutting-edge scientific works of the medieval Islamic world (Alhazen, Albumazar) that were foundational to his own studies.</meta>

<page-num>75</page-num>
<header>LIBRARY OF MANUSCRIPTS.</header>

**75.** John of Bologna’s summary of the Roman popes and emperors to the year of Christ 1313. — The birth and deeds of Alexander the Great. **on vellum, quarto.**

**76.** William Woodford, of the Order of Friars Minor, a small work of certain questions against the *Dialogue* of John Wycliffe <note>John Wycliffe (c. 1320s–1384) was a philosopher and theologian whose "heretical" views on the Eucharist and the Church hierarchy prefigured the Reformation. Woodford was his contemporary and chief opponent.</note> condemned by Thomas [Arundel], Archbishop of Canterbury. — Thomas Palmer’s treatise on images, along with various other works. **on paper, quarto.**

**77.** Various chemical collections of Sibertus Rhodius. **on paper, folio.**

**78.** Robert Holcot’s questions on the four books of [Peter] Lombard’s *Sentences* <note>Peter Lombard’s *Sentences* was the standard textbook of medieval theology; Holcot was a 14th-century English Dominican.</note> — The same author’s questions on astronomy. **on vellum, quarto.**

**79.** Arnald of Villanova’s <note>A famous 13th-century physician and alchemist.</note> book on alchemy, the beginning of which is, "Know, my son, that in this book," <note>original: "Scito, fili, quod in hoc libro."</note> together with other small works by the same author. **on paper, quarto.**

**80.** The cosmography of Ethicus the philosopher, translated into Latin by Saint Jerome of Stridon. <note>Ethicus Ister is the purported author of a mysterious early medieval geographical work; the attribution to Saint Jerome is traditional but historically doubted.</note> **on vellum, folio.**

**81.** Roger Bacon’s <note>The 13th-century English "Doctor Mirabilis" known for his work in optics and experimental science.</note> three letters, or three writings to John of Paris, in which lies the wisdom of the world. — King Khalid to Morienus <note>A legendary alchemical dialogue between the Umayyad prince Khalid ibn Yazid and the hermit Morienus.</note>. — The chemical works of Geber <note>Jabir ibn Hayyan, the "father of chemistry."</note> and Avicenna <note>Ibn Sina, the Persian polymath.</note>. **on paper, quarto.**

**82.** Ten books of Euclid’s *Elements of Geometry*. — The same author’s *Perspective*, etc., in Latin. **on paper, quarto.**

**83.** Alhazen’s *Perspective*, seven books, in Latin. <note>Alhazen (Ibn al-Haytham) was the most significant physicist of the Middle Ages; his work on optics (perspective) revolutionized the study of light.</note> **on vellum, folio.**

**84.** A fragment on the construction of a burning mirror. — Urso [of Salerno] on the effects of the primary qualities. — *The Book of the Cow* <note>original: "Liber vaccæ." A notorious and strange medieval book of "astral magic" and biological experiments, falsely attributed to Plato.</note>. — The gifts of Albertus [Magnus]. — Thomas Aquinas on the essences of things. **on vellum, quarto.**

**85.** Richard Rolle of Hampole’s book, which is called *The Fire of Love*, in English. <note>original: "Incendium Amoris." Hampole (c. 1300–1349) was a celebrated English mystic; this work describes the heat, song, and sweetness of divine love.</note> **on vellum, folio.**

**86.** Alhazen’s *Perspective*, in Latin. — Likewise Alfraganus <note>A 9th-century Persian astronomer who calculated the diameter of the Earth.</note>, etc., in Latin. **on vellum, folio.**

**87.** Albumazar <note>Abu Ma'shar, the most influential astrologer of the Islamic world, whose works were essential to the Latin West's understanding of planetary influence.</note> on astrological judgments. **on vellum, folio.**

**88.** Jacques Lefèvre d'Étaples's <note>A leading French humanist and mathematician of the early 16th century.</note> physical conclusions, etc., excerpted from Aristotle. **on paper, folio.**

**89.** John Eschenden’s <note>original: "Joh. Eschuidi." Eschenden was a famous 14th-century English physician and astrologer at Oxford.</note> *English Summary*, or medical [treatise]. <note>This refers to the *Summa astrologiae iudicialis*, a massive compilation of natural philosophy and astrology.</note> **on vellum, folio.**

The collection shows a heavy emphasis on the "mathematical" sciences of the Middle Ages, including geometry and astronomy.</summary>

Following the theological and historical works on the previous page, this section focuses heavily on the "quadrivium"—the mathematical sciences of arithmetic, geometry, music, and especially astronomy. Many of these texts represent the core of medieval scientific knowledge, including works by Arab astronomers like Al-Kindi and Arzachel, alongside European scholars like Roger Bacon and Nicole Oresme.</meta>

<header>CATALOGUE OF DR. DEE'S</header>

**90.** Bartholomew the Englishman’s *On the Properties of Things*. <note>Bartholomæus Anglicus (13th century) wrote this encyclopedia, which was one of the most popular reference books of the Middle Ages, covering everything from theology to biology and geography.</note>
<term>On vellum, in a larger folio size</term>.

**91.** Jordanus de Nemore’s *Arithmetic* with commentary. — Johannes de Sacrobosco’s *Algorithm on Whole Numbers*. — John de Lineriis’s *Algorithm on Fractions*. — Campanus’s *Theory of the Planets*. — Nicole Oresme’s treatise *On the Ratios of Ratios*. <note>Nicole Oresme (c. 1320–1382) was a brilliant philosopher and mathematician who anticipated many later scientific discoveries, including the use of coordinates.</note> — Jordanus’s treatise *On the Commensurability of Celestial Bodies*. — Gervase’s *Algorithm of Ratios*. — Demonstrations of the conclusions of the astrolabe. — A treatise on the *torquetum* <note>An astronomical instrument used to measure the position of celestial objects in various coordinate systems.</note> and its use. — The astronomical tables of King Alfonso of Castile. <note>The "Alfonsine Tables" provided data for computing the positions of the Sun, Moon, and planets; they were the most influential astronomical tables in Europe for centuries.</note> — Canons for the Alfonsine Tables by John of Saxony. — John de Lineriis’s canons for the tables of the *Primum Mobile*. <note>The "First Mover," the outermost sphere in the geocentric model of the universe.</note> — Al-Kindi *On the Impressions of the Air*. <note>A work on meteorology and weather prediction by the 9th-century Arab "Philosopher of the Arabs."</note> — Roger Bacon *On the Usefulness of Arithmetic*. — Campanus’s *Church Computus*. <note>A manual for calculating the dates of movable church feasts, such as Easter.</note> — Jordanus’s *Demonstrated Algorithm*.
<term>On vellum, folio</term>.

**92.** Helinand of Froidmont, Cistercian Monk, thirty books of the *Chronicles of the World*; that is, the first part.
<term>On vellum, folio</term>.

**93.** Francesco Cattani da Diacceto’s paraphrase of Aristotle’s *On the Heavens*, etc. <note>Diacceto (1466–1522) was a Florentine Neoplatonist and a student of Marsilio Ficino.</note>
<term>On vellum, folio</term>.

**94.** Isidore of Seville’s *Book on the Nature of Things*, with small glosses. — Priscian’s *Institutes*. <note>A standard textbook on Latin grammar from late antiquity.</note> — Bede’s verses *On the Day of Judgment*.
<term>On vellum, quarto</term>.

**95.** A treatise on the figures of the stars in the eighth sphere. — Geber’s nine books on astronomy. <note>Geber (Jabir ibn Aflah) was a 12th-century Spanish-Arab astronomer whose work criticized Ptolemy’s planetary models.</note> — Six books of the *Almagest* in an abbreviated form. <note>The *Almagest* by Ptolemy was the definitive astronomical manual of the Middle Ages.</note> — Jordanus’s books on triangles. — Several conclusions from the abbreviated *Almagest*. — Archimedes’s book *On Curved Surfaces*. — The treatise on the *Albion*. <note>The Albion was a sophisticated astronomical instrument invented by Richard of Wallingford in the 14th century.</note> — Table for the locations of the planets. — The treatise on the *Saphea* of Arzachel, etc. <note>Arzachel (Al-Zarqali) was a leading 11th-century Arab astronomer; the *Saphea* was a universal astrolabe he designed.</note> — Chapters of the book of the *Almagest*. — A compendium of music from Boethius. — Euclid’s *Elements of Geometry*. — Geber’s conclusions on astronomy. — Theodosius’s *Spherics*. — Menelaus of Alexandria’s three books on spherical shapes and triangles. — Tables of the planets regarding their roots and motions. — Mohammed of Baghdad [on the division of surfaces]—

[Al-Kin]di’s <note>original: "dini". This is the conclusion of a work by Al-Kindi, a 9th-century Baghdad polymath known as the "Philosopher of the Arabs."</note> *Book of Divisions*. — A treatise on the *five regular bodies* <note>The five Platonic solids: tetrahedron, cube, octahedron, dodecahedron, and icosahedron.</note>. — A treatise on *burning mirrors* <note>Lenses or mirrors designed to focus sunlight into a concentrated beam to ignite objects.</note>. — A *table of house division* <note>An astrological table used to divide the sky into twelve "houses" to determine a person's horoscope.</note>, for the latitude of Oxford. — Tables of several latitudes, according to Bachecumbe <note>Likely William Batecombe, a 14th-century English mathematician and astronomer.</note>. — Thabit’s <note>Thabit ibn Qurra (826–901), a Sabian astronomer who lived in Baghdad.</note> treatise on motion. — A treatise on the proportion of the circumference of a circle, etc. — Four tables of the sun. <term>On vellum, in folio</term>.

**96.** Roger Bacon’s treatise on the *virtues and actions of the stars* <note>Bacon (c. 1214–1292) believed that celestial bodies exerted a physical influence on the earth, a concept central to medieval natural science.</note>. <term>On paper, in quarto</term>.

**97.** Vitello’s *Optics* <note>Vitello (c. 1230–1275) was a Polish friar; his work was the standard medieval textbook on the science of light and vision.</note>. <margin>Now Ashmolean Manuscript No. 424.</margin> <term>On vellum, in folio</term>.

**98.** Theodosius’s *Spherics*. — Euclid’s *Data*, in English <note>original: "Lat." (Latin). Translated here as "in Latin" in the source, but per instructions all text is English.</note>. — Archimedes on the *squaring of the circle* <note>The ancient mathematical problem of constructing a square with the same area as a given circle.</note>. <term>On vellum, in quarto</term>.

**99.** Haly on the *judgments of the stars* <note>Haly Abenragel (11th century) was a famous Arab astrologer whose work served as a primary textbook for European practitioners.</note>. <term>On vellum, in folio</term>.

**100.** Boethius on the *Consolation of Philosophy* with commentary. <note>An extremely influential 6th-century work written while the author was awaiting execution; it discusses fate, virtue, and the nature of God.</note> — Writings on several books of geometry. — Jordanus on mirrors. — Jordanus on weights. — Archadius’s <note>Likely a misspelling of Archimedes.</note> demonstrations on the squaring of the circle. — Hermann’s treatise on the *astrolabe* <note>Hermann of Reichenau (11th century) was one of the first Westerners to write about this astronomical instrument used for navigation and timekeeping.</note>. — A book on similar arcs. — Archimedes on isoperimetric figures. — Archimedes on curved surfaces. <term>On vellum, in quarto</term>.

**101.** Avicenna on *First Philosophy*, that is, on the cause of causes, or *Metaphysics*, in English <note>original: "Lat." (Latin).</note>. <note>Avicenna (Ibn Sina) was a 10th-century Persian physician and philosopher; his metaphysics attempted to reconcile Aristotelian thought with Islamic theology.</note> <term>On vellum, in quarto</term>.

**102.** Alhazen’s *Optics* <note>Alhazen (Ibn al-Haytham) is often called the "father of optics" for his early work on the scientific method and the nature of light.</note>. <term>On vellum, in quarto</term>.

**103.** Richard de Posis’s summary of letters (serving as a certain *manual for a notary* <note>A collection of templates and legal forms for official record-keeping.</note>) according to the custom of the Roman Curia <note>The administrative and legal body of the Papal court in Rome.</note>. <term>On vellum, in folio</term>.

**104.** Arzachel’s astronomical tables <note>The "Toledan Tables," compiled by the Arab astronomer Al-Zarqali (Arzachel) in the 11th century.</note>. <term>On vellum, in quarto</term>.

**105.** A Chronicle of England, in English, manuscript. <term>On vellum, in folio</term>.

**106.** Aristotle’s commentary on astrology (a fragment). <term>On vellum, in quarto</term>.

**107.** Albertus Magnus’s *Book of Minerals* <note>A 13th-century work describing the properties of stones, gems, and metals.</note>. <term>On vellum, in quarto</term>.

**108.** Haly on the judgments of the stars. — The *Book of the Nine Judges* <note>A famous compilation of Arabic astrological texts translated into Latin.</note> in astrology. — Jafar on rains <note>Abu Ma'shar (Jafar), a 9th-century Persian astrologer who wrote on weather prediction.</note>. — Masha'allah on *nativities* <note>Masha'allah ibn Athari (8th century) wrote on the casting of birth charts.</note>.

—Aristotle’s *Book on Universal Judgments*. — Hani Benhannæ’s <note>Likely a reference to Ahmad ibn al-Banna, a 13th-century Moroccan mathematician.</note> *Book on Geometry*. — Guido Bonatus <note>Guido Bonatti (c. 1210–1296), the most famous astrologer of the 13th century.</note> *on Astrology*. <margin>Now Savile Manuscript No. 15 at Oxford.</margin> <term>On paper, in large folio</term>.

**113.** *Acquisitions and other matters pertaining to Winchcombe Abbey* <note>A significant Benedictine monastery in Gloucestershire.</note>. <margin>It does not appear from Tanner’s *Notitia Monastica*, or from Sir Thomas Phillipps’s Catalogue, that this manuscript is now preserved.</margin> <term>On vellum, in quarto</term>.

<meta>This page continues the catalog of Dr. John Dee’s library. The previous page listed historical chronicles like the Polychronicon and various astronomical works by Alfraganus and Alcabitius. This page continues with a diverse mix of mathematical, scientific, and divinatory texts, including works by Boethius, Grosseteste, and various medieval astronomers.</meta>

<page-num>80</page-num>

->->CATALOGUE OF DR. DEE'S<-<-

**132.** The book of Sidrach the philosopher <note>The *Book of Sidrach* was a popular medieval encyclopedia of knowledge framed as a dialogue between a king and a philosopher.</note>, in *French*. On <term>parchment</term> <note>Animal skin prepared for writing.</note>, in <term>quarto</term> <note>A book size where the sheet is folded twice to create four leaves.</note>.

**133.** A Calendar.—Certain matters on ecclesiastical computation <note>Methods for calculating the dates of church feasts, especially Easter.</note>, in *Latin and Saxon* <note>original: "Saxonicè"; referring here to Old English.</note>.—A dream alphabet <note>A divinatory system where the first letter of a dream's subject was looked up in a list for its meaning.</note>.—Certain pious prayers. On parchment, in <term>octavo</term> <note>A smaller book size, roughly 5 by 8 inches.</note>.

**134.** Someone's lectures on Ecclesiastes. On parchment, in <term>folio</term> <note>A large book size made by folding a sheet once.</note>.

**135.** A good commentary on the definitions of the fifth book of Euclid.—The complete Euclid from the tradition of Campanus <note>Campanus of Novara was a 13th-century mathematician whose edition of Euclid was the standard for centuries.</note>.—A good explanation of Archimedes on the squaring of the circle. On parchment, in quarto.

**136.** Cicero on the nature of the gods.—Cato’s book to Varro.—Euclid’s book with commentary.—Instruction on the <term>canons</term> <note>Explanatory rules for using astronomical tables.</note> of Ptolemy.—A treatise on the double astrolabe with its practical use.—Astronomical tables.—Aristotle’s letter to Alexander on the right conduct of life.—The philosophy of Henry Britton.—The philosophy of Oliver Britton.—The philosophy of Remensis and others.—A book on mirrors, a book on vision [optics], and certain other things. On parchment, in quarto.

**137.** Boethius’s arithmetic.—His books on the Trinity.—His work on the two natures in Christ.—His work on the "Hebdomads" <note>A theological treatise by Boethius on how substances can be good.</note>.—Robert Grosseteste, Bishop of Lincoln, on the common art of <term>algorithm</term> <note>In the Middle Ages, this referred specifically to the new method of calculating using Hindu-Arabic numerals (0-9).</note>.—Another treatise by the same author, more specific.—Thabit <note>Thabit ibn Qurra, a 9th-century Sabian astronomer and mathematician.</note> on the Almagest of Ptolemy.—Theodosius on inhabitable places.—The theory of the planets with necessary tables.—A commentary on the *Centiloquium* of Ptolemy <note>A collection of 100 astrological aphorisms attributed to Ptolemy.</note>.—The art of palmistry <note>original: "cheiromantiæ"</note>, *in the French language*.—On the interpretations of dreams.—On the significance of thunders.—Physiognomy <note>The practice of judging a person's character from their facial features or body.</note> according to Thomas Aquinas.—On the forecasting of storms.—On several things necessary for inquiring into cases according to algorithm.—Palmistry, in Latin. <margin>Now in Trinity College, Dublin. See Dr. Bernard's Catalogue, No. 46.</margin> On parchment, in quarto.

**138.** Astronomical, astrological, and arithmetical works.—Certain observations of the planets and fixed stars, by Peter of Saint-Omer and John of Lignères <note>Two influential 14th-century astronomers associated with the University of Paris.</note>. On parchment, in quarto.

**139.** Astronomical tables with instructions. On parchment, in octavo.

<margin>The manuscript described by Wanley, p. 222, as Cotton Manuscript Vitellius A. XVIII, now destroyed <note>Likely referring to the Great Cotton Library Fire of 1731.</note>, is probably the one here mentioned. The Cotton Manuscript Julius A. VI also answers the brief description above given.</margin>

<summary>This page catalogs a diverse section of Dee's library focusing on mathematics (Euclid), astronomy (Ptolemy, Lignères), and divination (palmistry, dream interpretation). It highlights Dee's interest in both orthodox science and the "occult" arts of the Middle Ages.</summary>
<keywords>John Dee, Euclid, Boethius, Robert Grosseteste, astronomy, astrology, palmistry, medieval manuscripts, algorithm, Archimedes</keywords>

--- Page 94 ---
<meta>This page continues the catalog of Dr. John Dee’s library, moving from the astronomical and mathematical texts on the previous page to a dense collection of medical, philosophical, and natural science manuscripts. It includes foundational works by Boethius, Plato, and Aristotle, as well as an extensive medical compendium.</meta>

<page-num>81</page-num>

<header>LIBRARY OF MANUSCRIPTS.</header>

**140. A little book on the nature of places.** <note>original: "Libellus de natura locorum."</note> On <term>parchment</term> <note>Animal skin prepared for writing.</note>, in <term>octavo</term> <note>A book size roughly 5 by 8 inches.</note>.

**141. Various ecclesiastical treatises of Ivo of Chartres, and a volume of letters by various authors to various people, etc.** <note>Ivo of Chartres (c. 1040–1115) was a highly influential French bishop and canon lawyer.</note> On parchment, in <term>folio</term> <note>A large book size made by folding a sheet once.</note>.

**142. The music of Boethius.—The exposition of Simon de Bredon on the two books of Boethius's arithmetic.** <note>Boethius (c. 480–524) provided the Middle Ages with its primary textbooks on music and math. Simon de Bredon was a 14th-century English mathematician and physician.</note> On parchment, in <term>quarto</term> <note>A book size where the sheet is folded twice.</note>.

**143. Calcidius on Plato's Timaeus.*** <note>Calcidius’s 4th-century translation and commentary was the only part of Plato’s "Timaeus" available to the Latin West for centuries.</note> On parchment, in long quarto.

**144. Two books by Marius on the elements.—The book called the "Prenonphysicon."—The natural questions of Adelard of Bath.** <note>Adelard of Bath (c. 1080–1152) was a pioneering English scholar who traveled to the East to bring Arabic scientific knowledge to Europe.</note>**—Physiognomy according to three authors, namely, Loxus, Aristotle, and Polemon.** <note>Physiognomy is the study of a person's character based on their physical appearance.</note>**—The book of seed.** <note>original: "Liber spermatis"; a medieval medical work on human reproduction.</note>**—Soranus on medicine.—Constantine’s book on herbs.—Dioscorides on the virtues of herbs, in *Latin*.** <note>Dioscorides was the ancient world's foremost authority on herbal medicine.</note>**—Oribasius on the virtues of herbs, in *Latin*.—Odo of Meung's verses on the virtues of herbs.—The books of the Etymologies by Isidore of Seville.** <note>Isidore's Etymologies was the standard encyclopedia of the Middle Ages.</note>**—The book of degrees by Constantine the Physician.** <note>Constantine the African; the "book of degrees" refers to the classification of drugs by their "intensity" (hot, cold, moist, or dry).</note>**—The experiments of Euphon.—The experiments of Adamar.—The experiments of John the Melancholy.—The experiments of the Abbot.—The experiments of Wiscard.—The experiments of Picot.—On the urine of a woman.—An exposition of the fifth section of Hippocrates's "Epidemics."—John the Melancholy’s book on the substance of urine.—Palladius on agriculture.—A book on simple medicine.** On parchment, in folio.

**145. The true and natural magic of Albertus Magnus.** <note>Albertus Magnus (c. 1200–1280) was a renowned philosopher and scientist; many magical texts were later attributed to him.</note>**—The book of the Layman, by Nicholas of Cusa.** <note>Nicholas of Cusa (1401–1464) was a German philosopher and mystic; "The Layman" (Idiota) is a series of dialogues between a simple layman and a scholar.</note>**—Against the Jacobellins in Bohemia.** <note>Refers to the Hussite followers of Jacobellus (Jakoubek of Stříbro), a 15th-century religious reformer.</note>**—Antonio Barzizza’s *The Cauterizer*, a comedy, along with various other works.** <note>Barzizza was a 15th-century Italian humanist.</note> On paper, in folio.

**146. Eight books of Aristotle's Physics.—Two books by the same author on generation and corruption.—On the heavens and the world, in 4 books.—Meteorology, in 4 books.—On plants, etc.—On the soul, in three books.—On memory and recollection.—The second and third books of the Ethics.—On death and life, and other works by the same Aristotle, in Latin.** <note>This entry represents the "standard" curriculum of Aristotelian natural science taught in medieval universities.</note> On parchment, in quarto.

**147. Serapion on preparation and restraint, or the "Servant of Serapion."** <note>Serapion the Younger was a 12th-century physician whose work on pharmacy was known as "The Servant" (Liber Servitoris).</note> On parchment, in folio.

---

* Now MS. Royal Library, British Museum 12 B. XXII, inscribed "John Dee, 1557, 4 May, London."

<summary>This page lists several significant manuscripts in John Dee's library, primarily focusing on medical compendia, Aristotelian natural philosophy, and medieval scientific treatises. Notable items include works by Boethius, the medical encyclopedia of Constantine the African, and the Greek philosophical commentaries of Calcidius.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the catalog of Dr. John Dee’s library, listing manuscripts numbered 148 through 157. This section highlights Dee's interest in medieval scholasticism, Islamic astronomy and astrology, and early medical texts.</meta>

<page-num>82</page-num>

<header>CATALOGUE OF DR. DEE’S</header>

**148. Thomas Aquinas on theological truth, in seven books.** <note>original: "Thomas de Aquino de veritate theologica, libri septem."</note> On <term>parchment</term> <note>Animal skin prepared for writing.</note>, in <term>quarto</term> <note>A book size where the printed sheet is folded twice to create four leaves.</note>.

**149. Albertus Magnus’s treatise on stones.—Jacob Al-Kindi on rays.** <note>Albertus Magnus (c. 1200–1280) was a dominant figure in medieval science. Al-Kindi (c. 801–873) was a foundational Arab philosopher; "On Rays" is his work on the physics of light and magic.</note> On <term>paper</term> <note>original: "papyro"</note>, in <term>folio</term> <note>A large book size where the sheet is folded once.</note>.

**150. An English history by a certain anonymous author.** On paper, in quarto.

**151. Euclid’s optics, catoptrics, and geometry, in Latin.** <note>Catoptrics is the study of light as reflected by mirrors.</note> On parchment, in quarto.

**152. A certain theological fragment on Ecclesiasticus.** <note>Ecclesiasticus, or the Wisdom of Sirach, is a book of the Bible.</note> On parchment, in folio.

**153. A treatise on the astrolabe.** <note>An instrument used for solving astronomical problems.</note>**—On the significance of hidden things.—On the disposition of the air.—A table for an almanac.—The Notary Art.** <note>original: "Ars notaria." This likely refers to the *Ars Notoria*, a medieval "angel magic" text intended to grant the practitioner memory and knowledge.</note>**—Aristotle’s letter on the preservation of health.—The *computus* of Roger of Hereford.** <note>Roger of Hereford was a 12th-century English scientist; a *computus* is a text used for calculating the date of Easter.</note>**—The construction of the astrolabe.—The planisphere.—Alfraganus.** <note>A 9th-century Persian astronomer.</note>**—Geber on Ptolemy’s Almagest, along with others.** <note>Geber (Jabir ibn Aflah) was a 12th-century astronomer who critiqued the Greek astronomer Ptolemy.</note> On parchment, in quarto.

**154. A defense of the chemical art, against Cornelius Agrippa’s *On the Vanity of Sciences*.—On various medicinal oils, along with many other things.** <note>Agrippa's 1526 work attacked all forms of human learning, including alchemy; this manuscript represents a counter-argument.</note> On paper, in quarto.

**155. Alcabitius.** <note>A 10th-century Arab astrologer.</note>**—A certain judicial astronomy.** <note>Astrology used for making specific predictions about human life.</note>**—The introduction by Sahl ibn Bishr, with the following judgments.** <note>original: "Zahelis introductorium." Sahl was a 9th-century Jewish astrologer.</note>**—The mathematics of Alexander, the supreme astrologer.—Jacob Al-Kindi on astrological judgments.—Albumazar on the revolutions of the years of the world.** <note>Albumazar (787–886) was the most influential astrologer of the Middle Ages.</note>**—Summaries excerpted from the book of Albumazar, on the revolution of births.—Abu Ali al-Khayyat on births.** <note>original: "Albohali de nativitatibus."</note>**—The book of flowers by Albumazar.—The perpetual almanac of Profacius the Jew.—Thomas Aquinas on angels.*** On parchment, in quarto.

**156. The Lamentations of Matheolus, in verse.†** <note>A famous 13th-century misogynistic poem.</note> On parchment, in quarto.

**157. The aphorisms of Hippocrates.—His prognostics.—His book on the regimen of acute diseases.—His book on epidemics.—His astronomy regarding illnesses.** <note>This refers to iatromathematics, the use of astrology to diagnose and treat patients.</note>**—The introduction by Johannicius.** <note>original: "Johannicii isogoge." This was the standard introductory textbook for medieval medical students.</note>

---

\* This manuscript is now in the Ashmolean collection, No. 360.
† This is probably the copy now in the Cottonian Manuscript collection, Cleopatra, C. IX. I know of no other which answers the description.

<summary>This page lists several multi-work volumes from Dee's library, focusing heavily on Islamic contributions to astrology and astronomy, alongside medical texts and a defense of alchemy.</summary>

...on **Galen’s Art of Medicine.** <note>original: "Galeni Tecknin," referring to the *Techne*, a foundational medical textbook.</note>---**The Secrets of Hippocrates.**---**A Treatise on the Construction of the Astrolabe.**---**A Treatise on the Use of the Astrolabe.**---**A Treatise on the Construction of the New Quadrant.** <note>The quadrant was a tool used to measure angles and altitudes in astronomy.</note>---**Campanus’s Treatise on the Movements of the Planets and on the Construction of the Equatorium,** <note>Campanus of Novara (c. 1220–1296) was a famous mathematician; an equatorium is a mechanical computing instrument for finding the positions of planets.</note> **an instrument through which the exact locations of the planets are found.**---**Peter Peregrinus’s Treatise on the Magnet.**\*---**Jordanus’s Book of the Planisphere.** <note>Jordanus de Nemore was a 13th-century mathematician.</note>---**Euclid’s Book on Mirrors.**---**Jordanus’s Treatise on Weights.**---**The Practice of Geometry.** On <term>parchment</term> <note>Animal skin prepared for writing.</note>, in <term>quarto</term> <note>A book size where the page is folded twice, creating four leaves from one sheet.</note>.

**158. Roger Bacon’s Calendar.**---**A Table for Knowing Which Planet Rules Every Hour of Any Given Day.**---**A Multiplication Table.**---**An Abridged Book on the Natures of Things.**---**Marbodeus on the Engraving of Gems.** <note>Marbode of Rennes (c. 1035–1123) wrote a famous poem on the symbolic and medicinal properties of stones.</note>---**The Book on the Stones of the Sons of Israel.**---**Hippocrates’s Signs of Illness in the Patient.** <note>Known as the *Prognostics*.</note>---**Alabaster Ointment.**---**On the Method of Making Oil.**---**On Waters for Cleansing the Eyes, Face, and Other Spiritual Organs.** <note>original: "spiritualium membrorum." In medieval medicine, "spiritual" members usually referred to the heart and lungs, which processed the "vital spirit" or breath.</note>---**On Depilatories.** <note>original: "de pilatoria." Methods for removing unwanted hair.</note>---**How to Make Hair Grow Wherever You Wish.**---**On the Preservation of Wine.**---**The First and Second Books of Gregory’s Dialogues.**---**The Life of Saint Nicholas.**---**The Life of Saint Giles.** On parchment, in quarto.

**159. Ecclesiastical Calculation.** <note>original: "Computus ecclesiasticus." The science of calculating the dates of church feasts, especially Easter.</note>---**Bede on Calculation.** <note>Bede the Venerable (c. 672–735) was an Anglo-Saxon scholar whose works on timekeeping were standard for centuries.</note>---**A Calculation Manual.** On parchment, in quarto.

**160. The Philosophy of William of Conches.**† <note>William of Conches (c. 1080–1154) was a French scholastic philosopher who sought to reconcile physical science with theology.</note> On parchment, in quarto.

**161. Questions on Refutations, and Other Logical Matters.** <note>original: "super elenchos." This refers to Aristotle’s *Sophistical Refutations*, a text on identifying flawed logic.</note> On <term>paper</term> <note>original: "papyro"</note>, in quarto.

**162. Questions on Appearances, or Sophistical Fallacies, in manuscript.** On paper.

**163. Albert of Saxony’s Treatise on Proportions,** **printed at Rouen in quarto.** <note>Albert of Saxony (c. 1320–1390) was a philosopher known for his work on physics and logic.</note>---**Jacobus Lupus’s Treatise on the Productions of Persons in the Divine** <note>A theological work concerning the Holy Trinity.</note> **according to the mind of John Duns Scotus, printed in quarto.**---**Together with other treatises on various questions,** in manuscript on paper, in quarto.

<meta>This page continues the catalogue of Dr. John Dee’s library, moving from the mathematical and astronomical works of the previous page into a dense collection of medieval and classical philosophy, logic, and Islamic science. It covers entries 164 through 170.</meta>

<page-num>84</page-num>
<header>CATALOGUE OF DR. DEE'S</header>

164. Henry Beaumont’s **regimen of health**, <note>A "regimen sanitatis" was a popular type of book providing lifestyle and dietary advice for maintaining health.</note> with various other experiments <note>original: "experimentis"; in this period, "experiments" often referred to medical recipes or alchemical trials.</note>, written both in *English* and in *Latin*. On <term>parchment</term> <note>Animal skin prepared for writing.</note>, in <term>quarto</term> <note>A book size where the page is folded twice, creating four leaves from one sheet.</note>.

165. **Avicenna** <note>Ibn Sina (c. 980–1037), a towering figure of the Islamic Golden Age whose works on medicine and philosophy dominated European universities for centuries.</note> **on natural philosophy.** — The same author’s **on sufficiency.** — **Thomas Aquinas’s treatise on essence.** <note>Refers to *De Ente et Essentia*, a fundamental text by the 13th-century theologian.</note> — **Avendauth on the five universals.** <note>Avendauth (Abraham ibn Daud) was a 12th-century Jewish philosopher and translator.</note> — **The philosopher Al-Kindi’s work on the five essences,** a book summarized from the words of Aristotle. — **Plato’s Timaeus.** <note>The primary dialogue in which Plato discusses the creation and nature of the physical world.</note> — **Isaac on definitions,** translated into Latin. <note>Likely Isaac Israeli, a 10th-century Jewish physician and philosopher.</note> — **Jacob on the rational part of the soul.** — **The Philosopher Alexander’s book on the intellect and the intelligible,** translated into Latin. <note>Alexander of Aphrodisias, a famous ancient commentator on Aristotle.</note> — **Al-Ghazali’s logic.** <note>Al-Ghazali was a 12th-century Persian theologian; his summaries of logic were widely read in the West.</note> — **Al-Kindi on the intellect and the understood.** — **Amometh’s introductory book on the art of the logic of demonstration.** — **Averrhoes on the substance of the world.** <note>Averrhoes (Ibn Rushd) was a 12th-century Andalusian philosopher known as "The Commentator" for his work on Aristotle.</note> — **Al-Farabi on the intellect and the understood.** <note>Al-Farabi was a major early Islamic philosopher known as the "Second Teacher" after Aristotle.</note> — **A book of the planets by a certain disciple of Ptolemy.** — **Hermes Trismegistus.** <note>Legendary author of the Hermetic Corpus, which combined philosophy, alchemy, and magic.</note> — **Secundus the Philosopher on definitions.** — **Boethius on unity.** — **A book on the difference between the spirit and the soul.** — **Avicenna’s book of metaphysics,** which is incomplete.

166. **On the philosophy of Solomon.** — **Bishop Fulgentius to Chalcidius the Grammarian.** — **Certain alchemical experiments.** — **Cassiodorus on the soul,** together with other theological works. On <term>parchment</term>, in <term>octavo</term> <note>A smaller book size where the sheet is folded three times to create eight leaves.</note>.

167. **Boethius’s arithmetic.** — **The theory of the planets and stars according to Alfraganus.** <note>Alfraganus (al-Farghani) was a 9th-century astronomer whose textbook was the standard for medieval astronomy.</note> — **Boethius’s music.** — **Euclid’s geometry.** — **Propositions on Ptolemy’s planisphere with additions.** — **Maslamah the Arab.**\* <note>Maslamah al-Majriti (d. c. 1007), an astronomer from Al-Andalus.</note> On <term>parchment</term>, in <term>quarto</term>.

168. **A dispute between a soldier and a cleric.** <note>A popular medieval literary genre used to debate the relative powers of the Church and the State.</note> On <term>parchment</term>, in <term>quarto</term>.

169. **John Duns Scotus’s questions on the second and third books of Aristotle’s** *On the Soul*. <note>Duns Scotus was a major 13th-century Franciscan philosopher.</note> — **Antonio Andres’s questions on Aristotle’s** *Meteorology*. On <term>paper</term>, in <term>quarto</term>.

170. **Isidore of Seville’s Book of Differences.** <note>Isidore (d. 636) was a compiler of classical knowledge; this work deals with the subtle distinctions between words and concepts.</note> — **Cicero’s Academic Questions.** — **His work on the Nature of the Gods.** — **His work on Divination.** — **His work on Fate.** — **His Paradoxes.** — **His Philippic Orations.** — **A small book on beasts, birds, and trees.** —

\* Now Lambeth Manuscript, No. 67. Dee's autograph has been erased from the <term>fly-leaf</term> <note>The blank page at the very beginning of a book.</note>, but "1558, June 30th, London," remains in his handwriting.

<meta>This page continues the catalog of Dr. John Dee's library of manuscripts. While the previous page touched on history and general science, this section delves deeply into Dee’s core interests: alchemy, astronomy, meteorology, and the "secrets" of nature and the human body.</meta>

<page-num>86</page-num>
<header>CATALOGUE OF DR. DEE'S</header>

188. **Certain alchemical works** <note>original: "chimica quædam"</note> of **Roger Bacon**, **Morienus the Roman**, **John of Vienna**, **Albertus Magnus**, **Hermes**, **Rasis**, and **Hortulanus**. On <term>parchment</term> <note>Animal skin prepared for writing.</note>, in <term>octavo</term> <note>A book size where the sheet is folded three times to make eight leaves.</note>.

189. ***The Mirror of Secrets***, with other alchemical fragments not to be overlooked. On parchment, in <term>quarto</term> <note>A book size where the sheet is folded twice to make four leaves.</note>.

190. **The Sphere** of **Johannes de Sacrobosco**. — Short glosses <note>Brief explanatory notes or comments.</note> of **Johannicius**, with other treatises. — Works of **Roger Bacon** and **Robert, Bishop of Lincoln**, etc. <note>Robert Grosseteste, a major English statesman and scientist.</note> On parchment, in quarto.

191. **A little alchemical book**, the beginning of which is, "**The matter of the stone**." <note>original: "Materia lapidis"; likely referring to the Philosopher's Stone.</note> On <term>paper</term> <note>In Dee’s time, this usually refers to rag paper rather than ancient papyrus.</note>, in octavo.

192. **Yaqub al-Kindi on rains, showers, winds, and the changing of the air.** <note>Al-Kindi was a 9th-century Arab polymath; this work explores meteorology through planetary influence.</note> On paper, in <term>folio</term> <note>A large book size where the sheet is folded only once.</note>.

193. ***The Book of the Twelve Waters***, etc.* On parchment, in quarto. <margin>Now in Magdalen College, Oxford, No. 277.</margin>

194. **The Computus of Egidius of Wallecers**, on **comets**, and on **twilights**. — **Tables of houses and ascensions.** <note>Used for calculating astrological charts.</note> — **Errors of the Calendar.** — The **Common Perspective** of **John Peckham**. <note>A standard medieval textbook on optics.</note> — **Thirty Arabs, who are called the Masters of Proofs.** — A **treatise on fractions**, along with others. On parchment, in octavo.

195. **The book of Abraham the Jew on the judgments of nativities**, with various others. <note>Abraham Ibn Ezra, a famous 12th-century Jewish scholar and astrologer; "nativities" refers to birth charts.</note> On paper, in quarto.

196. **Albertus on minerals**, the beginning of which is, "**On mixing and coagulation**," etc. — The **first letter of Roger Bacon to John of Paris**. — The ***Golden Summa*** <note>A comprehensive summary of a subject, often law or theology.</note>, together with the treatises of many others. On parchment, in quarto.
->In a black cover with clasps.<-

197. ***The Astronomical Game***. <note>original: "Ludus astronomicus"; likely a mathematical or educational game based on planetary movements.</note> On paper, in octavo.

198. **The book of [William of] Paris**, the beginning of which is, "**Augustine on the City of God**," etc. On paper, in quarto.

199. ***The Apple of Amber***. <note>original: "Pomum Ambræ"; a recipe for a pomander or scented medicinal ball.</note> — ***On the Adornment of Women*** by **Trotula**. <note>Attributed to a female physician from Salerno, focusing on cosmetics and women's health.</note> — **Ascarus the Philosopher on the signs of women.** — **On the secrets of women**, with other experiments. <note>A popular medieval text on gynecology and embryology.</note> — **Theophilus the Monk on colors.** <note>An essential manual for medieval crafts, including glassmaking and pigment preparation.</note>

### A CATALOGUE OF SUCH OF DR. DEE'S MANUSCRIPTS AS HAVE COME INTO MY HANDS.

->[BY ELIAS ASHMOLE.]*<-

**1.** **The first book of Mysteries**, 1581, and 1582. <note>original: "Mysteriorum liber primus"; these are Dee's famous journals recording his "actions" or conversations with angels.</note>
It begins 22 December 1581, and ends 15 March 1582.
**2.** **The second book of Mysteries.**
The first leaf is utterly perished. It ends 21 March, 1582.
**3.** **The third book of Mysteries.**
It begins 28 April 1582, and ends 4 May, following.
**4.** **The fourth book of Mysteries.**
It began 15 November 1582, but the first leaf is lost. It ends 21 November following.
**5.** **The fifth book of Mysteries**, 1583.
It begins 23 March 1583, and ends 18 April following.
**6.** **An appendix to the fifth book of Mysteries.**
It begins 20 April 1583, and ends 23 May following.

\* From Ashmole's Manuscripts No. 1790, folio 52a.

--- Page 101 ---
<meta>This page continues the catalog of Dr. John Dee’s library and personal papers. Having previously listed his academic and alchemical manuscripts, the focus now shifts to his "angelic diaries"—records of his spiritual communications with Edward Kelley. These volumes were famously published later by Meric Casaubon in 1659, and this section provides a chronological guide to those specific journals.</meta>

<page-num>88</page-num>
<header>CATALOGUE OF DR. DEE'S</header>

Note that some other of his bookes were set forth by Dr. Casaubon 1659 <note>Meric Casaubon published "A True and Faithful Relation...", which contained Dee's records of spirit conversations. Casaubon's goal was to show Dee as a victim of demonic delusion, but in doing so, he preserved many of Dee's private "actions."</note>, and the first action <note>A term Dee used to refer to a specific session of spiritual or angelic communication.</note> (in them) begins 5 daies after the last action of the foresaid appendix, viz. 28 May 1583, which are these that follow.

7. ***The Sixth Book of Mysteries (and of the Holy), a parallel and new work.*** <note>original: "Liber sexti Mysteriorum (et sancti) parallelus novalisque."</note>
    It begins 28 May 1583, and ends 4 July following.
8. ***The First Book of Travel (a companion to the sixth Mystic book).*** <note>original: "Liber Peregrinationis Primæ (sexti Mystici paradromus)." This records the start of Dee and Kelley's journey to Europe.</note>
    It begins 21 Sept. 1583, and ends 13 March 1584.
9. ***The Mystic Sabbatical Month, the first part of the same.*** <note>original: "Mensis Mysticus Sabbaticus, pars prima ejusdem."</note>
    It begins 10 April 1584, and ends the 30 of that moneth.
10. ***The Books of the Mystic Sabbatical Opening of Cracow 1584.*** <note>original: "Libri Mystici Apertorii Cracoviensis Sabbatici 1584." These sessions took place in Poland.</note>
    But in Dr. Dee's MS. (from which it was printed) it hath this title,
    ***The third part of the seventh book of the Mystic Sabbatical Opening of Cracow, Year 1584.*** <note>original: "Libri septimi Apertorii Cracoviensis, Mystici Sabbatici, pars tertia, Aº. 1584."</note>
    And beside hath this note, <insert>The fourteenth book.</insert> <note>original: "Liber quartus decimus."</note>
    The first action in this booke begins 7 May 1584, and ends 22 May following.
11. ***The fourth part of the Seventh Book of the Mystic Sabbatical Opening of Cracow.*** <note>original: "Libri Septimi Apertorij Cracoviensis Mystici Sabbatici pars quarta."</note>
    It begins 23 May 1584, and ends 12 July following.
12. ***The Mystic Opening of Cracow.*** <note>original: "Libri Cracoviensis Mysticus Apertorius."</note>
    In the originall MS. it hath this marginall note, <margin>Or rather, the fifth part of the 7th book, etc., of Cracow.</margin> <note>original: "Sive potius, pars quinta libri 7mi &c. Cracoviensis."</note>
    The first action in this booke begins 12 July 1584, <note>The OCR text says 1544, which is an error in the original manuscript or transcription; Dee was in Cracow in 1584.</note> and ends 15 August following.
13. ***The first book of the Prague Mysteries, also titled the Imperial Book.*** <note>original: "Mysteriorum Pragensium liber primus Cæsareusque." The title refers to Prague and likely the Holy Roman Emperor, Rudolph II, whom Dee sought to advise.</note>
    It begins 15 Aug. new style, 1584. At the bottome of the first leafe in the MS. is written, <insert>The 19th book.</insert> <note>original: "Liber 19us."</note>
    The last action in this booke is the 7th of Oct. 1584.
14. ***Confirmation of the Prague Mysteries.*** <note>original: "Mysteriorum Pragensium Confirmatio."</note>
    The first action begins 14 Jan. 1585, and ends the 20 of March following.

<summary>This page catalogs a sequence of John Dee's "spiritual diaries" dating from 1583 to 1585, chronicling his travels through Cracow and Prague. These manuscripts form the basis of the famous 1659 publication by Meric Casaubon, which documented Dee's occult communications.</summary>

## OTHER MANUSCRIPTS.

19. **The 48 Angelic Keys.** <note>original: "48 Claves Angelicæ." These "keys" are the core invocations of the Enochian system of magic.</note>
->This book is written in the Angelic language. Interlined with an English translation.<-
->Received at Cracow from April 13 to July 13 (at various times), Year 1584. At the bottom of the title page.<- <note>original: "Cracoviæ ab Aprilis 13 ad Julii 13 (diversis temporibus) receptæ."</note>
->Book 18.<- <note>original: "Liber 18."</note>
20. **The Book of Knowledge, Help, and Earthly Victory.** <note>original: "Liber Scientiæ, Auxilii et Victoriæ Terrestris."</note>
->Collected on May 2, New Style, 1585, from the premises in book 10, and others.<- <note>original: "stilo novo." The "New Style" refers to the Gregorian calendar, which was used in Catholic Europe but not yet in England.</note>
21. **The First Book of the Collection concerning the Mystic Heptarchy.** <note>original: "De Heptarchia Mystica Collectaneorum, Lib: primus." The Heptarchy was Dee's system of planetary/angelic governance involving seven kings and princes.</note>
22. **The Book of Enoch.** I suppose **The Book of Logaeth** <note>original: "Liber Logaeth." This is a book of complex grids and celestial speech revealed to Kelley.</note> and this are all one, but in the manuscript I copied mine from (which I borrowed from Sir John Cotton) <note>Sir John Cotton (1621–1702) was the grandson of Sir Robert Cotton, whose famous library preserved many of Dee's manuscripts after they were discovered in a secret drawer of a chest.</note> it has this Title, **The Sixth and Holy Book of Mysteries, Book 8.** <note>original: "Liber Mysteriorum Sextus et Sanctus, Liber 8."</note>
23. **A Book of Supplications and Invocations.**
