15. **The Book of the Confirmed Prague Mysteries.** <note>original: "Mysteriorum Pragensium Confirmatorum liber." Prague was the capital of the Holy Roman Empire and a major center for occult study under Emperor Rudolf II, who briefly patronized Dee and his scryer Edward Kelley.</note>

This refers to a specific "action" or spiritual session involving Francesco Pucci (1543–1597), a radical Italian humanist and theologian who spent time in Dee's circle before eventually returning to Catholicism; he was later executed for heresy.</note>

17. **The Book of Resurrection, to which the manuscript adds, and the Foundation of the 42 Months.** <note>original: "Liber Resurectionis... et 42 Mensium Fundamentum." The "42 months" refers to a specific period of time found in the biblical Book of Revelation (11:2 and 13:5), suggesting that Dee and Kelley were interpreting their spiritual communications through a lens of apocalyptic prophecy.</note>
->It begins the 30 of April 1586.<-
->The first and second action out of seven: is also added in the manuscript.<- <note>original: "Actio prima et secunda ex septem." Dee often organized his records into sequences of seven "actions" or sessions.</note>
->The last action in this book is 21 Jan. 1587.<-

18. **The third Action. Memorable matters of the divine mysteries, from the beginning of the written third action (out of seven), which was dedicated to the day of April 4th, Year 1587.** <note>original: "Actio tertia. Mysteriorum divinorum memorabilia, ab actionis (ex septem) tertiæ, descriptæ exordio, cui dies 4° Aprilis, A°. 1587, dicata fuit."</note>
->It begins 4 April 1587, and ends 23 May following.<-
->Thus far from the Printed Book.<- <note>This refers to the portion of Dee's diaries that were eventually published by the scholar Meric Casaubon in 1659 under the title "A True and Faithful Relation...", which remains the primary source for Dee's mystical activities.</note>

<meta>This page documents the proceedings of the Camden Society’s annual general meeting in 1842. It follows the printer's imprint of J.B. Nichols and Son, who are formally thanked in this text for their contributions to the Society's publications.</meta>

# THE CAMDEN SOCIETY. <note>The Camden Society was a learned society founded in London in 1838 to publish early historical and literary materials that had previously remained in manuscript form.</note>

->FOR THE PUBLICATION OF<-

## EARLY HISTORICAL AND LITERARY REMAINS.

At a General Meeting of the Camden Society held at the Freemasons' Tavern, Great Queen Street, Lincoln's Inn Fields <note>A prominent London venue frequently used in the 19th century for the meetings of scientific and literary organizations.</note>, on Monday the 2nd of May, 1842,

->THOMAS AMYOT, <term>ESQ. F.R.S., TREAS. S.A.</term>, DIRECTOR,<- <note>Thomas Amyot was a noted antiquarian. His titles indicate he was a Fellow of the Royal Society (F.R.S.) and the Treasurer of the Society of Antiquaries (Treas. S.A.).</note>
->IN THE CHAIR.<-

**The Director** having opened the business of the Meeting,

**The Secretary** read the Report of the Council agreed upon at their meeting of the 28th of April last, whereupon it was

**Resolved**, That the said Report be received and printed, and that the **Thanks** of the Society be given to the Council for their services.

**The Thanks** of the Society were also voted to the Editors of the Society's publications; to the Right Honourable Thomas Grenville <note>A politician and renowned book collector whose massive library of over 20,000 volumes was later bequeathed to the British Museum.</note>; to the Master and Fellows of St. Peter's College, Cambridge <note>Now commonly known as Peterhouse, the oldest constituent college of the University of Cambridge.</note>; to J. I. Blackburn, <term>Esq. M.P.</term> <note>John Ireland Blackburne, a Member of Parliament for Warrington.</note>; to the Local Secretaries; and to Messrs. Nichols <note>The printing firm of J. B. Nichols and Son, who handled the Society’s technical production.</note>, for the assistance towards attaining the objects of the Society mentioned in the Report as having been given by them.

**The Secretary** then read the Report of the Auditors, agreed upon at their meeting of the 30th of April last, whereupon it was

**Resolved**, That the said Report be received and approved, and that the **Thanks** of the Society be given to the Auditors for their trouble.

**Thanks** were then voted to the Director, Treasurer, and Secretary for their services during the past year; and

<sig>a</sig> <note>This small letter at the bottom of the page is a "signature mark," used by 19th-century printers to ensure that the large sheets of paper were folded and bound in the correct sequence.</note>

<summary>This page records the minutes and resolutions of the 1842 annual meeting of the Camden Society, including formal thanks to its council, editors, and prominent donors like Thomas Grenville.</summary>
<keywords>Camden Society, Thomas Amyot, Thomas Grenville, St. Peter's College Cambridge, 19th-century learned societies, historical preservation</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the proceedings of the Camden Society’s annual meeting held on May 2, 1842, moving from the presentation of the annual report to the formal election of the Society’s leadership and the subsequent first meeting of the new Council.</meta>

<header>ANNIVERSARY MEETING OF 1842.</header>

The Meeting then proceeded to the election of Officers, when
**The Right Hon. Lord Francis Egerton, <term>M.P.</term>** <note>Member of Parliament. Lord Francis Egerton (1800–1857) was a politician, writer, and patron of the arts who later became the 1st Earl of Ellesmere.</note>
was elected **President**, and
**THOMAS AMYOT, <term>Esq. F.R.S. Treas. S.A.</term>** <note>Thomas Amyot (1775–1850) was a prominent antiquary. F.R.S. stands for Fellow of the Royal Society; Treas. S.A. indicates he was the Treasurer of the Society of Antiquaries.</note>
**The Right Hon. Lord BRAYBROOKE.**
**JOHN BRUCE, <term>Esq. F.S.A.</term>** <note>Fellow of the Society of Antiquaries. Bruce was a key figure in founding the Camden Society.</note>
**JOHN PAYNE COLLIER, <term>Esq. F.S.A.</term>** <note>A famous scholar of Elizabethan drama and Shakespeare.</note>
**CHARLES PURTON COOPER, <term>Esq. Q.C. D.C.L. F.R.S. F.S.A.</term>** <note>Q.C. stands for Queen’s Counsel; D.C.L. for Doctor of Civil Law.</note>
**T. CROFTON CROKER, <term>Esq. F.S.A. M.R.I.A.</term>** <note>M.R.I.A. stands for Member of the Royal Irish Academy; Croker was well-known for his collections of Irish folklore.</note>
**Sir HENRY ELLIS, <term>K.H. F.R.S. Sec. S.A.</term>** <note>K.H. refers to the Knight of the Royal Guelphic Order; Sec. S.A. means Secretary of the Society of Antiquaries. Ellis was the Principal Librarian of the British Museum.</note>
**JAMES ORCHARD HALLIWELL, <term>Esq. F.R.S., F.S.A.</term>** <note>A noted collector of nursery rhymes and a Shakespearean scholar.</note>
**The Rev. JOSEPH HUNTER, <term>F.S.A.</term>**
**Sir FREDERIC MADDEN, <term>K.H., F.R.S., F.S.A.</term>** <note>Keeper of Manuscripts at the British Museum.</note>
**JOHN HERMAN MERIVALE, <term>Esq. F.S.A.</term>**
**The Rev. LANCELOT SHARPE, <term>M.A., F.S.A.</term>**
**THOMAS STAPLETON, <term>Esq. F.S.A.</term>**
**WILLIAM JOHN THOMS, <term>Esq. F.S.A.</term>** <note>Thoms is credited with coining the term "folklore" in 1846.</note> and
**THOMAS WRIGHT, <term>Esq. M.A., F.S.A.</term>**
were elected as the **Council**, and
**JOHN YONGE AKERMAN, <term>Esq. F.S.A.</term>**
**CHARLES FREDERICK BARNWELL, <term>Esq. F.R.S., F.S.A.</term>,** and
**PETER CUNNINGHAM, <term>Esq.</term>**
were elected **Auditors** of the Society for the ensuing year.
**Thanks** were then voted to the **Director**, for his able conduct in the Chair.

### ELECTION OF OFFICERS, 1842.

At a Meeting of the **COUNCIL** of the Camden Society held at No. 25,
Parliament Street, Westminster, on Thursday the 5th May, 1842,
**THOMAS AMYOT, Esq.** in the Chair;
The Council having proceeded to the Election of Officers,—
**THOMAS AMYOT, Esq.** was elected **Director**; **JOHN BRUCE, Esq.**
**Treasurer**; and **WILLIAM J. THOMS, Esq.** **Secretary**, for the Year next
ensuing.

It also documents the first Council meeting following the general election, where the specific roles of Director, Treasurer, and Secretary were filled.</summary>

<keywords>Camden Society, Lord Francis Egerton, Thomas Amyot, John Bruce, William John Thoms, 1842 Election, Victorian antiquaries, John Payne Collier, Sir Henry Ellis, Sir Frederic Madden, James Orchard Halliwell, Society of Antiquaries, historical preservation</keywords>

<meta>This page contains the Report of the Council for the Camden Society, dated May 1, 1842. It details the society's financial health, membership stability, and the scholarly works published during the preceding year, following the election of officers described on the previous page.</meta>

# REPORT OF THE COUNCIL.

->DATED 1st MAY, 1842.<-

THE Council elected on the 3rd May 1841 are pleased to be able to report that the affairs of the Society continue in a condition of unabated prosperity.

The number of 1200 Members, to which the Society is limited, has been maintained; and there continues to be a large number of Candidates for admission upon vacancies.

The investment standing in the names of the Trustees for the Society has been increased, since the last General Meeting, from the sum of £408 0s. 4d., <term>Three per Cent. consols</term><note>Short for "consolidated annuities," these were a form of British government debt (bonds) that paid a steady 3% interest; they were considered the safest possible investment for societies and trust funds in the 19th century.</note>, to £574 13s. 8d. This increase has arisen from the investment of sums received for Compositions; and the Council recommend that whatever sum may now be in hand on that account, should be added to the investment.

The Council have added the following gentlemen to the list of <term>Local Secretaries</term><note>Volunteers who acted as regional representatives for the Society, collecting subscriptions and recruiting members in specific cities.</note>:—

> The Rev. Joseph Bosworth, <term>LL.D.</term> <note>Doctor of Laws. Bosworth was a famous scholar of Anglo-Saxon.</note> for Nottingham.
> Richard Rees, <term>Esq. F.S.A.</term> <note>Fellow of the Society of Antiquaries.</note> for Cardiff, in the place of Captain **W. H. Smyth**, retired.
> James Heywood Markland, <term>Esq. F.R.S. F.S.A.</term> <note>F.R.S. stands for Fellow of the Royal Society.</note> for Bath, in the place of Rev. William L. Nichols, who has removed.

The Publications for the past year have been—

The Second Book of the Travels of Nicander Nucius, a <term>Corcyræan</term> <note>From Corcyra, the ancient name for the island of Corfu.</note> Gentleman who came to England in the suite of an Ambassador from the Netherlands, sent by the Emperor Charles V. to the Court of Henry VIII.: translated from the Original Greek <term>MS.</term> <note>Manuscript.</note> formerly belonging to Archbishop Laud, and now preserved in the Bodleian Library. Edited by the REV. JOHN ANTONY CRAMER, D.D. Principal of New Inn Hall, and Public Orator, Oxford.

The Latin Poetry of Walter Mapes <note>Also known as Walter Map (c. 1140–1210), a witty medieval writer and Archdeacon of Oxford known for his satirical look at the court and the church.</note>, Archdeacon of Oxford at the beginning of the thirteenth century. Edited by THOMAS WRIGHT, <term>Esq. M.A., F.S.A.</term> and

Three <term>inedited</term> <note>Meaning previously unpublished.</note> Early English Metrical Romances. Edited, from a <term>MS.</term> in the possession of J. I. Blackburn, <term>Esq. M.P.</term> <note>Member of Parliament.</note>, by JOHN ROBSON, Esq.

-># REPORT OF THE COUNCIL, 1842.<-

**The Rev. GEORGE FREDERICK NOTT, <term>Doctor of Divinity</term> <note>Original: "D.D."; the highest academic degree in theology.</note>, <term>Fellow of the Society of Antiquaries</term> <note>Original: "F.S.A."; a prestigious title for those recognized for their contributions to history and antiquities.</note>**
**EDWARD SKEGG, <term>Esquire</term> <note>Original: "Esq."; a title of courtesy for a man of high social status, often used for gentlemen or professionals.</note> <term>Fellow of the Royal Geographical Society</term> <note>Original: "F.R.G.S."; a professional body for the advancement of geography and exploration.</note>**
**JOHN SYDNEY TAYLOR, <term>Esquire</term>.**
**<term>Lieutenant-General</term> <note>Original: "Lieut.-Gen."; a high-ranking military officer.</note> WILLIAM THORNTON.**
**HENRY WOODTHORPE, <term>Esquire</term>, <term>Fellow of the Society of Antiquaries</term>.**

The Council report that the Treasurer, having found that other engagements prevented his any longer devoting to the accounts and financial correspondence of the Society the time and attention which, after an experience of three years, he found to be absolutely necessary to be given to them, submitted to the Council the propriety of their accepting his resignation, or of making some other arrangement with respect to the accounts. The Council, regarding the subject as one vitally affecting the welfare of the Society, proceeded immediately to take it into their consideration, and are happy to be able to report that they have effected an arrangement by which the accounts are now kept, and the financial correspondence is attended to, by the Secretary, the Treasurer still continuing responsible to the Society. This arrangement will put the Society to a small annual expense, which can very well be afforded, and which the Council are persuaded the Members will think well bestowed, if it has the effect of preserving accuracy in the accounts. The recent arrangements in the Post Office <note>This likely refers to the "Uniform Penny Post" reforms of 1840, which made sending mail and money orders significantly cheaper and more reliable.</note> render it easy for country Members to transmit their subscriptions by Post Office orders, which course the Council recommend to be adopted, making the Post Office orders payable to the Treasurer.

After the lapse of four years of continuing prosperity the Council hope that the <term>Camden Society</term> <note>Founded in 1838, this society was dedicated to publishing rare historical documents and early English literature.</note> may be regarded as having taken a permanent station amongst established publishing associations. Many societies have been founded upon similar principles, and one considerably out-numbers this Society in Members: but there is no one which can produce better evidences of stability and prosperity, or which has greater reason to be satisfied with the estimation in which its works are regarded by the public. Amongst the Candidates for admission recently entered there are many Public Libraries and other bodies, whose desire to participate in

<summary>This page details the administrative restructuring of the Camden Society's finances due to the Treasurer's workload and celebrates the Society's four-year milestone as a stable, prestigious institution.</summary>

<keywords>Camden Society, Treasurer, financial management, Post Office orders, George Frederick Nott, Edward Skegg, John Sydney Taylor, William Thornton, Henry Woodthorpe, academic honors</keywords>

--- Page 125 ---
<meta>This page concludes the 1842 Report of the Council, highlighting the Camden Society's growing international reputation and the value of its scholarly publications. It then transitions into the formal Report of the Auditors, providing a certification of the Society's financial records for the preceding year.</meta>

<page-num>8</page-num>
<header>REPORT OF THE COUNCIL, 1842.</header>

The benefits of membership reflect the strong reputation of the Society, both in this and other countries; and the high prices maintained by our books when copies appear on the market provide encouraging proof of the demand for them from collectors and literary scholars. In four years, the Society has issued eighteen volumes, all of which were works excluded from the ordinary mode of publication, yet worthy of being published, of great use to historical researchers, and likely to retain a permanent place in the literature of the country.

The forthcoming works are fully expected to maintain, if not increase, the reputation of the Society, and the Council see no reason to doubt that the Society may long continue to be useful and prosperous in its position and membership numbers.

---

# REPORT OF THE AUDITORS,

->DATED 30TH APRIL, 1842.<-

**WE**, the Auditors appointed to audit the accounts of the <term>Camden Society</term> <note>A learned society founded in London in 1838 to publish early historical and literary materials.</note>, report to the Society that the Treasurer has presented his accounts to us from the 27th day of April, 1841, to the 30th day of April, 1842. We have examined these accounts, along with the vouchers <note>Original: "vouchers"; documents such as receipts or bills that prove a recorded transaction took place.</note> relating to them, and find them to be correct and satisfactory.

And we further report that the following is a correct abstract of the receipts and expenditure of the Society during the period to which we have referred.

The Treasurer has also reported to us that the Secretary and himself have made arrangements by which they hope, during the ensuing year, almost, if not altogether, to get rid of all arrear of outstanding subscriptions <note>Unpaid membership dues from previous years.</note>. We trust that the Members will assist them in this very desirable object; and especially that Members resident in the country will transmit their subscriptions at once to the Treasurer by Post Office orders <note>A relatively new and secure method for sending money via the mail system, which had been reorganized in 1838.</note>. The adoption of this course has been suggested by several of the Local Secretaries <note>Volunteer representatives who managed the Society's affairs in specific regional districts outside of London.</note>; and it is obvious that it will greatly lessen the troublesome and not very agreeable duties, which are thrown upon those officers by the present practice, will lessen the number of mistakes, and will greatly promote the prosperity of the Society.

Dated the 30th April, 1842.

(Signed)  LANCELOT SHARPE <note>A clergyman and scholar known for his interest in philology and antiquities.</note>.
          B. CORNEY <note>Bolton Corney (1784–1870), a noted literary critic and researcher.</note>.

## LAWS OF THE CAMDEN SOCIETY,
### ADOPTED AT THE GENERAL MEETING, MAY 2, 1842.

I. That the Society shall be entitled "**The Camden Society** <note>The Society was named after William Camden (1551–1623), a famous historian and antiquarian often called the "father of English history."</note>, for the Publication of Early Historical and Literary Remains."

II. That the objects of the Society shall be, First, the publication of <term>inedited Manuscripts</term> <note>Handwritten documents that had never before been printed or published.</note>; Second, the reprinting of Works of sufficient rarity and importance to make Reprints desirable; and Third, the publication of Translations of Historical Works not previously rendered into English.

III. That the Society shall consist of One Thousand Two Hundred Members, being Subscribers of One Pound annually; such Subscription to be paid in advance, on or before the first day of May in every year.

IV. That the management of the affairs of the Society shall be vested in a President and a Council consisting of fifteen Members, which President and Council shall be elected annually by the Society at large, at a General Meeting to be held on the 2nd day of May, being the Anniversary of Cam- <note>This refers to the birth of William Camden on May 2, 1551.</note>

<meta>catchword: Cam-</meta>

<summary>This page contains the conclusion of the 1842 Auditors' Report and the first four governing laws of the Camden Society, detailing its mission to publish unpublished historical manuscripts and rare texts.</summary>
<keywords>Camden Society, Auditors' Report, membership fees, Post Office orders, inedited manuscripts, historical translations, William Camden, Society laws, 1842 General Meeting</keywords>

--- Page 128 ---
<meta>This page continues the list of formal "Laws" or bylaws of the Camden Society from page 10, specifically detailing the administrative structure, election processes, and financial rules of the organization.</meta>

<page-num>11</page-num>
<header>LAWS OF THE CAMDEN SOCIETY.</header>

<note>This sentence completes Law IV from the previous page, regarding the date of the General Meeting.</note> Camden’s birth <note>William Camden was born on May 2, 1551. The Society held its annual meetings on his birthday to honor his legacy.</note>; or on the Monday following, when the 2nd of May shall happen to fall upon a Sunday.

V. That the President and Council shall, from amongst their own body, elect a Director, who shall act as Chairman of the Council, in the absence of the President, and also a Treasurer, and a Secretary.

VI. That the **Accompts** <note>An archaic spelling of "accounts."</note> of the Receipts and Expenditure of the Society shall be audited annually by three Auditors, to be elected at the General Meetings, and that the Report of the Auditors, with an Abstract of the Accompts, shall be published.

VII. That the names of Members proposed to be elected as President, Council, and Auditors, shall be transmitted by the proposers to the Secretary, one fortnight before the General Meeting, and that notice of the persons so proposed shall be forwarded by the Secretary one week before the General Meeting, to all the Members residing within the limits of the **Twopenny Post** <note>A local mail service in London where letters were delivered for a flat fee of two pence. This indicates the Society’s heavy concentration of members in the capital.</note>, and to all other Members who shall, in writing, request to receive the same.

VIII. That no Member shall be entitled to vote at any General Meeting whose Subscription is in arrear <note>Members who have not paid their annual dues cannot participate in official decisions.</note>.

IX. That in every year one-fifth in number of the Council of the year preceding shall be ineligible for re-election; and that in case any Member of the Council shall not attend more than one-third of the number of Meetings of the Council, such Member shall be considered to be one of the retiring Members <note>A rule designed to ensure "new blood" in leadership and to remove inactive participants.</note>.

X. That in the absence of the President and Director, the Council at their Meetings shall elect a Chairman, who shall have a **casting vote** <note>An extra vote used by the chairperson to break a tie.</note> in case of equality of numbers, and shall also retain his right to vote upon all questions submitted to the Council.

XI. That the Funds of the Society shall be disbursed in payment of <term>necessary expenses</term> <note>These primarily included the costs of transcribing manuscripts, printing, and binding the books provided to members.</note> incident to the production of the Works of the Society, and that all other expenses shall be avoided as much as possible.

XII. That, after the Members of the Society shall have reached One <note>The text cuts off here; historical records indicate the membership was capped at 1,200 people to maintain the quality and exclusivity of the publications.</note>

<summary>This page outlines the governance of the Camden Society, including the election of officers, the requirement for annual financial audits, and the rules for Council member rotation and voting.</summary>
<keywords>bylaws, auditing, Twopenny Post, voting rights, Council attendance, financial transparency, election procedures</keywords>

--- Page 129 ---
<meta>This page concludes the governing rules of the Camden Society, continuing the numerical list of "Laws" from the previous page. It focuses on membership limits, book distribution, and the process for amending the Society's bylaws.</meta>

<page-num>12</page-num>

-># LAWS OF THE CAMDEN SOCIETY.<-

XII. <note>This sentence completes a law from the previous page establishing a membership cap.</note> [One] Thousand Two Hundred, vacancies in that number shall be filled up by the Council, from time to time as they occur. <note>The Camden Society limited its membership to 1,200 individuals to ensure the organization remained manageable and that the cost of printing books for every member stayed within their budget.</note>

XIII. That every Member not in arrear <note>Meaning "not behind on payments."</note> of his Annual Subscription, shall be entitled to One Copy of every Work published by the Society during that year.

XIV. That the Members shall be invited to contribute or recommend Works for publication.

XV. That Editors of Works printed by the Society shall be entitled to Twenty Copies of the Works they edit.

XVI. That the Council shall determine what number of copies of each Work shall be printed, and that the copies over and above those required by the Members shall be sold in such manner, and at such prices, as shall be fixed by the Council, the proceeds being carried to the account of the Society.

XVII. That the Publications of the Society shall all form separate and distinct Works, without any other connexion than that which must necessarily exist between the volumes of such Works as consist of several Volumes. <note>This rule ensured that the Society’s books were not part of a single, rigid series, allowing members to collect individual titles of historical interest.</note>

XVIII. That any Member of the Society may at any time **compound** <term>compound: to pay a single lump sum (in this case, £10) to cover all future annual dues, effectively becoming a life member.</term> for his future Annual Subscriptions, by payment of £10 over and above his Subscription for the current year.

XIX. That every Member of the Society who shall intimate to the Council his desire to withdraw from the same, or who shall not pay his Subscription for the current year within three Months after his Election, or after such Subscription shall have become due, shall thereupon cease to be a Member of the Society.

XX. That the Council may appoint **Local Secretaries** <note>These were representatives in various cities and regions who helped manage the Society's affairs outside of London, assisting with recruitment and the distribution of books.</note> in such places, and with such authorities as to them shall seem expedient; every Local Secretary being a Member of the Society.

XXI. That no alteration shall be made in these Laws, except at a General Meeting, nor then, unless One Month's notice of any alteration intended to be proposed at such Meeting shall have been given in writing to the Secretary.

<summary>This page outlines the final administrative bylaws of the Camden Society, including the 1,200-member limit, the distribution of publications to members, and the rules for resignation and amending the Society's laws.</summary>
<keywords>Camden Society, membership cap, annual subscription, publication rules, life membership, Local Secretaries, bylaws amendment</keywords>

The Members whose names are printed in Capitals were on the Council of the year.* <note>The Council acted as the governing board, deciding which historical manuscripts would be published.</note>

# Front Binding of the Volume

->[The Front Cover]<-

<note>The image shows the front cover of a leather-bound book. The leather is dark and aged, bearing the marks of historical use.</note>

The cover is decorated with <term>blind-tooling</term> <note>A decorative technique where patterns are stamped into leather using heated tools without the application of gold leaf or color, resulting in a subtle, indented design.</note>. Because the leather is dark and the lighting is low, the specific details of the decoration are difficult to distinguish, but they appear to include:

*   **Decorative borders** stamped along the edges of the cover.
*   <unclear>Faint floral or botanical motifs</unclear> <note>Commonly used in early modern bookbinding to provide texture and elegance to a volume.</unclear>
*   <unclear>A central heraldic crest or decorative medallion</unclear> <note>This central design often identified the library, the owner, or the coat of arms of a patron.</note>

<margin>Traces of embossed patterns</margin>
